There were no college boys among them except Joe Lane who was flunking out in the School of Dentistry. He did not interest Emma Lou. As it was with Joe, so it was with all the other boys. She invariably picked them to pieces when they took her out, and remained so impassive to their emotional advances that they were soon glad to be on their way and let her be. Emma Lou was determined not to go out of her class, determined either to associate with the “right sort of people” or else to remain to herself.

Had anyone asked Emma Lou what she meant by the “right sort of people” she would have found herself at a loss for a comprehensive answer. She really didn’t know. She had a vague idea that those people on the campus who practically ignored her were the only people with whom she should associate. These people, for the most part, were children of fairly well-to-do families from Louisiana, Texas and Georgia, who, having made nest eggs, had journeyed to the West for the same reasons that her grandparents at an earlier date had also journeyed West. They wanted to live where they would have greater freedom and greater opportunity for both their children and themselves. Then, too, the World War had given impetus to this westward movement. There was more industry in the West and thus more chances for money to be made, and more opportunities to invest this money profitably in property and progeny.

===Chinese===
假期一完，埃玛·卢不大情愿地回了学校。功课她谈不上兴趣，只是没有别的事可做，倒也跟得上，分数考得很高。这期间，有人给她引见了好些青年男子，她偶尔也同他们出去。也都是格雷斯的相知，同格雷斯的其余朋友一个成色。里头没有一个大学生，除了乔·莱恩——牙科学院里眼看待不下去的那位。他引不起埃玛·卢的兴趣。乔如此，别的男孩子也如此。他们带她出去，她回回把人挑剔个稀烂；人家献殷勤，她冷得像块冰。不多久，那些人乐得顺坡下驴，由她去了。埃玛·卢打定主意不降自己的格：要么同「上等人」往来，要么干脆独来独往。

倘若有人问埃玛·卢，「上等人」究竟指什么，她会发现自己答不上个囫囵话来。她实在不知道。她只有个模糊的念头：校园上那些几乎不把她放在眼里的人，才是她唯一该结交的。这些人多半是路易斯安那、得克萨斯和佐治亚几家小康之家的儿女；那几家攒下了一份家底，便像早年间她外祖父母一样，举家西迁。他们想住到一个自己和儿女都能更自由、机会更多的地方去。再者，世界大战又给这股西迁加了把劲。西部工业多，挣钱的机会多，把钱投在房产和儿女身上生利的门路也多。

===Original===
The greater number of them were either mulattoes or light brown in color. In their southern homes they had segregated themselves from their darker skinned brethren and they continued this practice in the North. They went to the Episcopal, Presbyterian, or Catholic churches, and though they were not as frankly organized into a blue vein society as were the Negroes of Boise, they nevertheless kept more or less to themselves. They were not insistent that their children get “whiter and whiter every generation,” but they did want to keep their children and grandchildren from having dark complexions. A light brown was the favored color; it was therefore found expedient to exercise caution when it came to mating.

The people who, in Emma Lou’s phrase, really mattered, the businessmen, the doctors, the lawyers, the dentists, the more moneyed Pullman porters, hotel waiters, bank janitors, and majordomos, in fact all of the Negro leaders and members of the Negro upper class, were either light skinned themselves or else had light skinned wives. A wife of dark complexion was considered a handicap unless she was particularly charming, wealthy, or beautiful. An ordinary looking dark woman was no suitable mate for a Negro man of prominence. The college youths on whom the future of the race depended practiced this precept of their elders religiously. It was not the girls in the school who were prejudiced—they had no reason to be, but they knew full well that the boys with whom they wished to associate, their future husbands, would not tolerate a dark girl unless she had, like Verne, many things to compensate for her dark skin. Thus they did not encourage a friendship with someone whom they knew didn’t belong. Thus they did not even pledge girls like Grace, Emma Lou, and Hazel into their sorority, for they knew that it would make them the more miserable to attain the threshold only to have the door shut in their faces.

===Chinese===
他们大多不是黑白混血，就是浅棕色。在南方老家，他们同肤色更深的弟兄们不相往来，到了西部，照旧行这一套。他们上圣公会、长老会或天主教的教堂；虽不像博伊西的黑人那样明火执仗地结成蓝筋会，却也差不多自成一体。他们不坚持儿女「一代更比一代白」，但确实不愿意儿孙长成深肤色。浅棕是受欢迎的颜色；因此到了婚配的关口，审慎从事便成了明智之举。

照埃玛·卢的说法，那些真正要紧的人——生意人、医生、律师、牙医，阔绰些的普尔曼卧车乘务员、旅馆侍应、银行门房、大宅管家，总之黑人领袖与黑人上层的全体——不是自己生得浅色，就是娶了浅色的太太。深肤色的太太被认作拖累，除非她格外有魅力、有钱或有貌。一个长相平常的深色女人，配不上有声望的黑人男子。种族的前途所系的那些大学生，把长辈这一训条奉行得虔诚至极。倒不是学校里的姑娘们存有偏见——她们犯不上；可她们心里透亮：她们想结交的小伙子、她们未来的丈夫，容不下深肤色姑娘，除非她像弗恩那样，有许许多多补偿这身黑皮肤的本钱。于是，她们不肯鼓励一段明知没有名分的交情。于是，她们连格雷斯、埃玛·卢、黑兹尔这样的姑娘，也不拉进姊妹会，因为她们知道：让这样的人摸到门槛，又当脸吃闭门羹，只会叫她们更苦。

---
===Original===
Summer vacation time came and Emma Lou went back to Boise. She was thoroughly discouraged and depressed. She had been led to expect so much pleasure from her first year in college and in Los Angeles; but she had found that the people in large cities were after all no different from people in small cities. Her Uncle Joe had been wrong—her mother and grandmother had been right. There was no place in the world for a dark girl.

Being at home depressed her all the more. There was absolutely nothing for her to do nor any place for her to go. For a month or more she just lingered around the house, bored by her mother’s constant and difficult attempts to be maternal, and irritated by her Cousin Buddy’s freshness. Adolescent boys were such a nuisance. The only bright spot on the horizon was the Sunday School Union picnic scheduled to be held during the latter part of July. It was always the crowning social event of the summer season among the colored citizens of Boise. Both the Methodists’ and Baptists’ missions cooperated in this affair and had their numbers augmented by all the denominationally unattached members of the community. It was always a gala, democratic affair designed to provide a pleasant day in the out-of-doors. It was, besides the annual dance fostered by the local chapters of the Masons and the Elks, the only big community gathering to which the entire colored population of Boise looked forward.

Picnic day came, and Emma Lou accompanied her mother, her uncle, and her cousin to Bedney’s Meadow, a green, heavily forested acre of park land, which lay on the outskirts of the city, surrounded on three sides by verdant foothills. The day went by pleasantly enough. There were the usually heavily laden wooden tables, to which all adjourned in the late afternoon, and there were foot races, games, and canoeing.

Emma Lou took part in all these activities and was surprised to find that she was having a good time. The company was congenial, and she found that since she had gone away to college she had become somewhat of a personage. Everyone seemed to be going out of his way to be congenial to her. The blue veins did not rule this affair. They were, in fact, only a minority element, and, for one of the few times of the year, mingled freely and unostentatiously with their lower caste brethren.

===Chinese===
暑假到了，埃玛·卢回了博伊西。她彻底灰了心、丧了气。她本指望大学第一年和洛杉矶能给她多少快乐；可她发现，大城市的人说到底同小城的人没什么两样。乔舅舅错了——母亲和外祖母对了。世上就是没有深色姑娘待的地方。

待在家里，更叫她抑郁。她实在无事可做，也无处可去。个把月里，她就在家里转悠：母亲费劲地献着母爱，叫她腻烦；巴迪表弟毛手毛脚没个正形，叫她光火。半大的男孩子就是讨嫌。地平线上唯一的光亮，是定于七月下旬开的主日学校联合会野餐会。那素来是博伊西有色市民一夏的社交压轴。卫理公会和浸信会的布道堂联手经办，再由社区里教门无所属的街坊们来添数。回回都是一场皆大欢喜的、民主的盛事，图的就是在露天里痛痛快快过一天。除了当地共济会和麋鹿会分会办的一年一度舞会，这就是博伊西全体有色居民都巴望的唯一大型集会了。

野餐日到了，埃玛·卢随母亲、舅舅和表弟去了贝德尼草场——市郊一片绿意浓覆的公园绿地，三面围着苍翠的山麓丘陵。这一天过得还算快活。照例是几张堆得冒尖的木头长桌，傍晚时大家移座开饭；还有赛跑、游戏、划船。

这些活动埃玛·卢样样参加了，还惊觉自己竟玩得开心。同伴们和气；她又发现，自从上了趟大学，自己竟成了个人物。人人都像是格外殷勤地来凑她的和气。蓝筋会辖不着这个场合。他们在这里其实只占少数，一年里难得这么几回，同低阶的弟兄们无拘无束、不事张扬地打成一片。

===Original===
All during the day, Emma Lou found herself paired off with a chap by the name of Weldon Taylor. In the evening they went for a stroll up the precipitous footpaths in the hills which grew up from the meadow. Weldon Taylor was a newcomer in the West trying to earn sufficient money to reenter an eastern school and finish his medical education. Emma Lou rather liked him. She admired his tall, slender body, the deep burnish of his bronze colored skin, and his mass of black curly hair. Here, thought Emma Lou, is the type of man I like. Only she did wish that his skin had been colored light brown instead of dark brown. It was better if she was to marry that she did not get a dark skin mate. Her children must not suffer as she had and would suffer.

The two talked of commonplace things as they walked along, comparing notes on their school experiences, and talking of their professors and their courses of study. It was dusk now and the sun had disappeared behind the snow capped mountains. The sky was a colorful haze, a master artist’s canvas on which the colors of day were slowly being dominated by the colors of night. Weldon drew Emma Lou off the little path they had been following, and led her to a huge boulder which jutted out, elbow like, from the side of a hill, and which was hidden from the meadow below by clumps of bushes. They sat down, his arm slipped around her waist, and, as the darkness of night more and more conquered the evanescent light of day, their lips met, and Emma Lou grew lax in Weldon’s arms. …

===Chinese===
一整天里，埃玛·卢总同一个名叫韦尔登·泰勒的小伙子配成对儿。傍晚，两人沿着自草场拔地而上的山间陡径去散步。韦尔登·泰勒是新来西部的，想攒够钱回东部的学校，把医学念完。埃玛·卢相当喜欢他。她赏看那副高挑瘦长的身材、铜色皮肤深沉的光泽、一头浓密的黑鬈发。埃玛·卢心想：这便是我中意的男人类型了。只可惜他的皮肤是深棕，而不是浅棕。她将来要嫁人，最好别再摊上一个深色肤色的配偶。她的儿女，可不能再像她这样受过苦、再接着受苦。

两人一路说着家常，交流各自的学校见闻，谈各自的教授和功课。天擦黑了，太阳落到雪峰背后。天空是一片彩色的薄霭，一位大师的画布，白昼的颜色正一点点让位于夜的颜色。韦尔登把埃玛·卢从两人循着的小径上引开，带她到一块巨石跟前——那石头从山坡上肘子似的探出来，又被一丛丛灌木遮着，草场上望不见。两人坐下，他的胳膊揽上她的腰；随着夜色愈来愈压倒白昼转瞬即逝的光，两片嘴唇凑到了一起，埃玛·卢在韦尔登的怀里软了下来……

===Original===
When they finally returned to the picnic grounds all had left save a few stragglers like themselves who had sauntered away from the main party. These made up a laughing, half-embarrassed group, who collected their baskets and reluctantly withdrew from the meadow to begin the long walk back to their homes. Emma Lou and Weldon soon managed to fall at the end of the procession, walking along slowly, his arm around her waist. Emma Lou felt an ecstasy surging through her at this moment greater than she had ever known before. This had been her first intimate sexual contact, her first awareness of the physical and emotional pleasures able to be enjoyed by two human beings, a woman and a man. She felt some magnetic force drawing her to this man walking by her side, which made her long to feel the pleasure of his body against hers, made her want to know once more the pleasure which had attended the union of their lips, the touching of their tongues. It was with a great effort that she walked along apparently calm, for inside she was seething. Her body had become a kennel for clashing, screaming compelling urges and desires. She loved this man. She had submitted herself to him, had gladly suffered momentary physical pain in order to be introduced into a new and incomparably satisfying paradise.

Not for one moment did Emma Lou consider regretting the loss of her virtue, not once did any of her mother’s and grandmother’s warnings and solicitations revive themselves and cause her conscience to plague her. She had finally found herself a mate; she had finally come to know the man she should love, some inescapable force had drawn them together, had made them feel from the first moment of their introduction that they belonged to one another, and that they were destined to explore nature’s mysteries together. Life was not so cruel after all. There were some compensatory moments. Emma Lou believed that at last she had found happiness, that at last she had found her man.

Of course, she wasn’t going back to school. She was going to stay in Boise, marry Weldon, and work with him until they should have sufficient money to go East, where he could reenter medical school, and she could keep a home for him and spur him on. A glorious panorama of the future unrolled itself in her mind. There were no black spots in it, no shadows, nothing but luminous landscapes, ethereal in substance.

It was the way of Emma Lou always to create her worlds within her own mind without taking under consideration the fact that other people and other elements, not contained within herself, would also have to aid in their molding. She had lived to herself for so long, had been shut out from the stream of things in which she was interested for such a long period during the formative years of her life, that she considered her own imaginative powers omniscient. Thus she constructed a future world of love on one isolated experience, never thinking for the moment that the other party concerned might not be of the same mind. She had been lifted into a superlatively perfect emotional and physical state. It was unthinkable, incongruous, that Weldon, too, had not been similarly lifted. He had for the moment shared her ecstasy, therefore, according to Emma Lou’s line of reasoning, he would as effectively share what she imagined would be the fruits of that ecstatic moment.

The next two weeks passed quickly and happily. Weldon called on her almost every night, took her for long walks, and thrilled her with his presence and his love making. Never before in her life had Emma Lou been so happy. She forgot all the sad past. Forgot what she had hitherto considered the tragedy of her birth, forgot the social isolation of her childhood and of her college days. What did being black, what did the antagonistic mental attitudes of the people who really mattered mean when she was in love? Her mother and her Uncle Joe were so amazed at the change in her that they became afraid, sensed danger, and began to be on the lookout for some untoward development; for hitherto Emma Lou had always been sullen and morose and impertinent to all around the house. She had always been the antisocial creature they had caused her to feel she was and, since she was made to feel that she was a misfit, she had encroached upon their family life and sociabilities only to the extent that being in the house made necessary. But now she was changed—she had become a vibrant, joyful being. There was always a smile on her face, always a note of joy in her voice as she spoke or sang. She even made herself agreeable to her Cousin Buddy, who in the past she had either ignored or else barely tolerated.

===Chinese===
等两人终于回到野餐场，人已散尽，只剩几对像他们这样从大队里溜开的落伍者。这些人是笑笑闹闹、又有点不好意思的一群，收起篮子，恋恋不舍地离开草场，走上回家的长路。埃玛·卢和韦尔登不久便落到了队伍末尾，慢慢走着，他的胳膊还揽着她的腰。此刻，一股前所未有的狂喜涌遍埃玛·卢的全身。这是她头一回亲密的性接触，头一回体察到一女一男两个人所能享受的肉体与情感的欢愉。她觉得有一股磁力，把她吸向身边这个男人，叫她渴望再领受他的身体贴上她身体的快乐，叫她想再尝一回那两唇相就、舌头相触的滋味。她面上走得镇定，其实费了好大的劲，因为心里已经翻腾开了。她的身体成了一座犬舍，关满了相互冲撞、尖声叫嚷、按捺不住的冲动与欲望。她爱这个男人。她把自己交给了他，甘愿忍受一时的皮肉之痛，好被引进一座崭新的、无与伦比地餍足的乐园。

埃玛·卢一刻也没有想过要懊悔自己的失身；母亲和外祖母的那些告诫叮嘱，一回也没有浮上来折磨她的良心。她总算给自己找着了伴侣；总算认识了该爱的男人；是某种逃不开的力量把他们牵到了一起，叫他们打照面的头一刻起就觉得彼此本是一体，命定要一同去探求自然的奥秘。生活到底不那么残酷。总有几段补偿的光景。埃玛·卢相信，她终于寻到了幸福，终于寻到了她的男人。

自然，她不回学校了。她要留在博伊西，嫁给韦尔登，同他一道做工，攒够了钱就去东部：他回医学院念书，她给他撑起一个家，给他打气。一幅辉煌的未来图景在她脑子里铺展开来。图上没有一个黑点，没有一丝阴影，只有一派明光朗照的风景，质地轻盈如烟。

埃玛·卢的老毛病便是这样：世界一概在自己脑子里造，全不考虑旁人旁物——那些不在她自身之内的东西——也得参与这世界的塑形。她独来独往得太久，在性格塑成的年月里，被关在她有兴趣的那股生活之流外头太久，以至于把自己的想象力当成了全知。于是她凭一段孤零零的经历，盖起了未来的爱情世界，一刻也没想过，当事的另一方兴许不作此想。她被托举到一个至善至美的情感与肉体境界；那么，韦尔登若不曾同样被托举上去，便不可思议、不合情理了。他既一时分享了她的狂喜，那么，照埃玛·卢的推理，他自会同样有效地，分享她所想象的那阵狂喜结出的果实。

接下来两个星期，过得又快又快活。韦尔登几乎夜夜来看她，带她做长距离的散步，用他的陪伴和情爱叫她心醉。埃玛·卢平生从没有这样幸福过。她把伤心的往事全忘了。忘了她一向当作身世之悲的东西，忘了童年和大学时光的孤绝。恋爱着的时候，「黑」算什么，「上等人」的敌意又算什么？母亲和乔舅舅叫她身上的变化惊着了，反而担起心来，嗅出危险，开始留意什么不测的苗头；因为在此以前，埃玛·卢在家里向来是阴沉着脸、闷闷不乐、说话没大没小的。他们一向把她造成了他们让她自觉的那种不合群的东西；既然人人都当她是个格格不入的人，她对家庭生活和家里应酬的参与，也就只限于住在家里所必不可少的那些。可如今她变了——变成一个生气勃勃、欢欢喜喜的人。脸上总挂着笑，说话唱歌，嗓音里总带着喜气。她甚至对巴迪表弟也和气起来——从前她对他，不是不理不睬，就是勉强容忍。

===Original===
“She must be in love, Joe,” her mother half whined.

“That’s good,” he answered laconically. “It probably won’t last long. It will serve to take her mind off herself.”

“But suppose she gets foolish?” Jane had insisted, remembering no doubt her own foolishness, during a like period of her own life, with Emma Lou’s father.

“She’ll take care of herself,” Joe had returned with an assurance he did not feel. He, too, was worried, but he was also pleased at the change in Emma Lou. His only fear was that perhaps in the end she would make herself more miserable than she had ever been before. He did not know much about this Weldon fellow, who seemed to be a reliable enough chap, but no one had any way of discerning whether or no his intentions were entirely honorable. It was best, thought Joe, not to worry about such things. If, for the present, Emma Lou was more happy than she had ever been before, there would be time enough to worry about the future when its problems materialized.

“Don’t you worry about Emma Lou. She’s got sense.”

“But, Joe, suppose she does forget herself with this man? He is studying to be a doctor and he may not want a wife, especially when. …”

“Damn it, Jane!” her brother snapped at her. “Do you think everyone is like you? The boy seems to like her.”

===Chinese===
「她准是恋爱了，乔。」母亲半是哀怨地说。

「那是好事，」他答得干脆，「多半长不了。正好叫她别老想着自己。」

「可要是她犯起糊涂来呢？」简不依不饶——多半是想起了自己当年犯糊涂的那段日子，同埃玛·卢的父亲。

「她自会当心自己。」乔回答的口气笃定，心里却没底。他也担心，可为埃玛·卢的变化高兴。他唯一怕的是，到头来她把自己弄得比先前任何时候都苦。这个韦尔登他知之不多，瞧着倒是个靠得住的小伙子；只是谁也没有办法断定，他的用意究竟全不全正经。乔想，这种事还是别操心的好。眼下埃玛·卢既然比哪会儿都开心，等将来的难处真冒了头，再愁也不迟。

「你别替埃玛·卢操心。她有数。」

「可是乔，万一她跟这个人忘乎所以了呢？他学的是医生，兴许不肯要老婆，尤其是……」

「见鬼，简！」她哥哥冲她喝道，「你以为谁都跟你一样？那小伙子像是喜欢她。」

===Original===
“Men like anyone they can use, but you know as well as I that no professional man is going to marry a woman dark as Emma Lou.”

“Men marry anyone they love, just as you and I did.”

“But I was foolish.”

“Well?”

“That’s right—Be unconcerned. That’s right—Let her go to the devil. There’s no hope for her anyway. Oh—why—why did I marry Jim Morgan?” and she had gone into the usual crying fit which inevitably followed this self-put question.

Then, without any warning, as if to put an end to all problems, Weldon decided to become a Pullman porter. He explained to Emma Lou that he could make more money on the railroad than he could as a hotel waiter in Boise. It was necessary for his future that he make as much money as possible in as short a time as possible. Emma Lou saw the logic of this and agreed that it was the best possible scheme, until she realized that it meant his going away from Boise, perhaps forever. Oakland, California, was to be his headquarters, and he, being a new man, would not have a regular run. It was possible that he might be sent to different sections of the country each and every time he made a trip. There was no way of his knowing before he reported for duty just where he might be sent. It might be Boise or Palm Beach or Albany or New Orleans. One never knew. That was the life of the road, and one had to accept it in order to make money.

===Chinese===
「男人嘛，谁肯将就就用谁。可你我都明白，吃专业饭的男人，是不会娶埃玛·卢这么黑的女人的。」

「男人娶的是爱的人，就像你我当年。」

「可我当年糊涂。」

「那不结了。」

「着啊——你倒无所谓。着啊——随她下地狱去。反正她也没指望了。唉——我当年——干吗要嫁吉姆·摩根呢？」说罢，她照例哭了一场——这句自问之后，照例必有这一场。

然后，毫无征兆，像是要一刀斩断所有问题似的，韦尔登决定去当普尔曼卧车乘务员。他向埃玛·卢解释，跑铁路比在博伊西当旅馆侍应挣得多。为了前程，他必须尽快、尽多地挣钱。埃玛·卢听出这话在理，也赞成这是顶好的算计——直到她醒悟过来：这意味着他要离开博伊西，兴许永远不回来了。他的派驻地是加利福尼亚的奥克兰；他是新人，轮不上固定班线。说不定每出一趟车，都给派去不同的地方；报到之前，谁也说不准去哪儿。也许是博伊西，也许是棕榈滩，也许是奥尔巴尼，也许是新奥尔良。天知道。跑铁路的日子就是这样，想挣钱，就得认。

===Original===
It made Emma Lou shiver to hear him talk so dispassionately about the matter. There didn’t seem to be the least note of regret in his voice, the least suggestion that he hated to leave her or that he would miss her, and, for the first time since the night of their physical union, Emma Lou began to realize that perhaps after all he did not feel toward her as she did toward him. He couldn’t possibly love her as much as she loved him, and, at the same time, remain so unconcerned about having to part from her. There was something radically wrong here, something conclusive and unexpected which was going to hurt her, going to plunge her back into unhappiness once more. Then she realized that not once had he ever spoken of marriage or even hinted that their relationship would continue indefinitely. He had said that he loved her, he had treated her kindly, and had seemed as thrilled as she over their physical contacts. But now it seemed that since he was no longer going to be near her, no longer going to need her body, he had forgotten that he loved her. It was then that all the old preachments of her mother and grandmother were resurrected and began to swirl through her mind. Hadn’t she been warned that men didn’t marry black girls? Hadn’t she been told that they would only use her for their sexual convenience? That was the case with Weldon! He hadn’t cared about her in the first place. He had taken up with her only because he was a stranger in the town and lonesome for a companion, and she, like a damn fool, had submitted herself to him! And now that he was about to better his condition, about to go someplace where he would have a wider circle of acquaintances, she was to be discarded and forgotten.

===Chinese===
听他这样冷冷静静地谈这件事，埃玛·卢直打寒噤。他的声音里似乎没有一丝惋惜，没有半点舍不得她、会惦记她的意思；于是，自那夜肉体结合以来头一遭，埃玛·卢开始明白：他对她的感情，兴许终究不像她对他那样。他不可能像她爱他那样爱她，又同时对离别这样无所谓。这里面有什么从根本上就不对的东西，有什么决定性的、出乎意料的东西，就要伤到她，就要把她重新掼回不幸里去。她这才发觉，他从来没有提过结婚，连一句「他们的关系会一直继续下去」的暗示也没有。他说过他爱她；他待她好；肉体接触时，他看起来同她一样心醉。可如今，既然他不再待在她身边，不再需要她的身体，他仿佛忘了自己爱过她。就在这时，母亲和外祖母的那些老说教统统还了魂，在她脑子里打旋。不是早有人警告过她，男人不娶黑姑娘吗？不是早有人告诉她，他们只会拿她图性事的方便吗？韦尔登就是这样！他起头就没把她放在心上。他同她好，只因为他在镇上人生地不熟，闷得慌想个伴儿；而她呢，像个大傻瓜，把自己交给了他！如今他要奔前程了，要去一个相识圈子宽得多的地方了，她就该被扔掉、被忘掉了。

===Original===
Thus Emma Lou reasoned to herself and grew bitter. It never occurred to her that the matter of her color had never once entered the mind of Weldon. Not once did she consider that he was acting toward her as he would have acted toward any girl under similar circumstances, whether her face had been white, yellow, brown, or black. Emma Lou did not understand that Weldon was just a selfish normal man and not a color prejudiced one, at least not while he was resident in a community where the girls were few, and there were none of his college friends about to tease him for liking “dark meat.” She did not know that for over a year he had been traveling about from town to town, always seeking a place where money was more plentiful and more easily saved, and that in every town he had managed to find a girl, or girls, who made it possible for him to continue his grind without being totally deprived of pleasurable moments. To Emma Lou there could only be one reason for his not having loved her as she had loved him. She was a black girl and no professional man could afford to present such a wife in the best society. It was the tragic feature of her life once more asserting itself. There could be no happiness in life for any woman whose face was as black as hers.

Believing this more intensely than ever before Emma Lou yet felt that she must manage in some way to escape both home and school. That she must find happiness somewhere else. The idea her Uncle Joe had given her about the provinciality of people in small towns reentered her mind. After all Los Angeles, too, was a small town mentally, peopled by mentally small southern Negroes. It was no better than Boise. She was now determined to go East where life was more cosmopolitan and people were more civilized. To this end she begged her mother and uncle to send her East to school.

“Can’t you ever be satisfied?”

“Now Jane,” Joe as usual was trying to keep the peace—

“Now Jane, nothing! I never saw such an ungrateful child.”

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢这样一路想，心里愈来愈苦。她压根没想到，肤色这回事，韦尔登脑子里其实一回也没有装过。她一刻也不曾考虑：他这样待她，换了任何别的姑娘，情形相仿，他也照样这样待——无论那张脸是白的、黄的、棕的还是黑的。埃玛·卢不明白，韦尔登不过是个自私的正常男人，倒不是个存肤色偏见的人——至少在他眼下住的地方如此：那儿姑娘稀罕，又没有他的大学同学在旁边，拿他喜欢「黑肉」（dark meat，指肤色黑的姑娘）打趣。她也不知道，一年多来，他辗转一个又一个镇子，专找钱多好攒的地方；而在每个镇子，他总有法子找到一个、或几个姑娘，让他熬得住那份苦役，不至于全然没了快活的时辰。在埃玛·卢看来，他不像她爱他那样爱她，缘由只可能有一个：她是个黑姑娘，吃专业饭的男人娶了这样一张脸的太太，在头等社交场里拿不出手。她一生的悲剧特征，又一次抬了头。脸像她这么黑的女人，这辈子休想有幸福。

这信念比以往任何时候都更凶地攫住她；埃玛·卢却愈发觉得，自己总得想法子，既逃出这个家、也逃出这所学校，总得在别处寻着幸福。乔舅舅灌给她的那套小城人心胸狭窄的论调，又回到她脑子里。说到底，洛杉矶精神上也是个小城，住满了精神上矮小的南方黑人，不见得比博伊西强。她这下拿定主意要去东部：那边的生活更世界主义，人也更文明。为着这个，她求母亲和舅舅送她去东部上学。

「你就永远不知足？」

「好了简，」乔照例出来打圆场——

「好了简，什么好了！我从没见过这么不知道感恩的孩子。」

===Original===
“I’m not ungrateful. I’m just unhappy. I don’t like that school. I don’t want to go there any more.”

“Well, you’ll either go there or else stay home.” Thus Jane ended the discussion and could not be persuaded to reopen it.

And rather than remain home Emma Lou returned to Los Angeles and spent another long miserable, uneventful year in the University of Southern California, drawing more and more within herself and becoming more and more bitter. When vacation time came again she got herself a job as maid in a theater, rather than return home, and studied stenography during her spare hours. School began again and Emma Lou reentered with more determination than ever to escape should the chance present itself. It did, and once more Emma Lou fled into an unknown town to escape the haunting chimera of intra-racial color prejudice.

===Chinese===
「我不是不知道感恩。我只是不快活。我不喜欢那个学校。我不想再去了。」

「那你要么去那儿，要么待在家里。」简就此把话谈死，再劝也不肯松口。

于是埃玛·卢宁可不回家，回到了洛杉矶，在南加州大学又熬了漫长、惨淡、无事可记的一年，愈发往自己心里缩，人也愈发刻毒。再到放假，她宁可不回家，在一家剧院给自己寻了个打杂女佣的差事，空下来的时候便自修速记。开学后，埃玛·卢再入校门，比哪时都更铁了心：一旦有机会，一定逃走。机会果真来了。埃玛·卢又一次逃进一座陌生的城市，去躲种族内部肤色偏见那缠人的幻魇。

## Part II / 第二部

Harlem / 哈莱姆

===Original===
Emma Lou turned her face away from the wall, and quizzically squinted her dark, pea-like eyes at the recently closed door. Then, sitting upright, she strained her ears, trying to hear the familiar squeak of the impudent floorboards, as John tiptoed down the narrow hallway toward the outside door. Finally, after she had heard the closing click of the double-barrelled police lock, she climbed out of the bed, picked up a brush from the bureau and attempted to smooth the sensuous disorder of her hair. She had just recently had it bobbed, boyishly bobbed, because she thought this style narrowed and enhanced the fulsome lines of her facial features. She was always trying to emphasize those things about her that seemed, somehow, to atone for her despised darkness, and she never faced the mirror without speculating upon how good-looking she might have been had she not been so black.

Mechanically, she continued the brushing of her hair, stopping every once in a while to give it an affectionate caress. She was intensely in love with her hair, in love with its electric vibrancy and its unruly buoyance. Yet, this morning, she was irritated because it seemed so determined to remain disordered, so determined to remain a stubborn and unnecessary reminder of the night before. Why, she wondered, should one’s physical properties always insist upon appearing awry after a night of stolen or forbidden pleasure? But not being anxious to find an answer, she dismissed the question from her mind, put on a stocking-cap, and jumped back into the bed.

She began to think about John, poor John who felt so hurt because she had told him that he could not spend any more days or nights with her. She wondered if she should pity him, for she was certain that he would miss the nights more than he would the days. Yet, she must not be too harsh in her conclusions, for, after all, there had only been two nights, which, she smiled to herself, was a pretty good record for a newcomer to Harlem. She had been in New York now for five weeks, and it seemed like, well, just a few days. Five weeks—thirty-five days and thirty-five nights, and of these nights John had had two. And now he sulked because she would not promise him another; because she had, in fact, boldly told him that there could be no more between them. Mischievously, she wished now that she could have seen the expression on his face, when, after seeming moments of mutual ecstasy, she had made this cold, manifesto-like announcement. But the room had been dark, and so was John. Ugh!

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢把脸从墙上转开，眯起那双黑黑的、豌豆似的小眼睛，狡黠地望向那扇刚刚关上的门。随后她坐直身子，竖起耳朵，想捕捉那几块放肆的地板熟悉的吱呀声——约翰正踮着脚，沿狭窄的门厅往外门走去。终于，她听见双闩警锁合拢的咔哒一响，这才爬下床来，从梳妆台上拿起发刷，试图抚顺那一头撩人散乱的头发。前不久她刚把头发剪短，剪成男孩子似的短发，因为她觉得这种发式能使自己丰腴的面部轮廓显得紧凑、增色。她总是想方设法突出身上那些似乎多少能为自己受人鄙夷的黑肤色赎罪的地方，而每当对镜自照，她总会暗自琢磨：倘若不是这么黑，自己该是多么好看。

她机械地继续梳着头发，不时停下来，爱怜地抚它一抚。她深深爱着这头头发，爱它那触电般的活力，爱它那不驯的蓬松劲头。可是今天早上，她却来了气：这头发偏偏铁了心要乱着，偏偏铁了心要固执地、多余地提醒人昨夜的一切。她想不明白，为什么人的身体经过一夜偷来的或禁戒的欢愉之后，各个部位总要坚持摆出一副凌乱相？不过她也不想深究答案，便把这个念头从心里打发掉，戴上护发睡帽，一翻身又钻回床上。

她开始想起约翰，可怜的约翰，因为他再不能在她这里消磨白天黑夜而满心委屈。她琢磨着是不是该可怜他，因为她断定，比起白天，他会更舍不得那些夜晚。不过，下结论也不必太刻薄，毕竟统共只有两个晚上——想到这里她暗自笑了——对哈莱姆的新客来说，这份记录已相当体面。她来纽约已经五个星期，却仿佛——唉，不过才几天似的。五个星期，三十五个白天、三十五个夜晚，这些夜晚里约翰占了两个。如今他闷闷不乐，因为她不肯再答应给他一夜；因为她索性直截了当地告诉他，两人之间到此为止。她促狭地想，当时要是能看见他的脸色就好了——那是彼此似乎都到了销魂的时刻之后，她冷冰冰地发表了那份宣言式的通告。可惜屋里一片黑，约翰也是一身黑。呸！

===Original===
She had only written home twice. This, of seemed quite all right to her. She was not concerned about anyone there except her Uncle Joe, and she reasoned that since he was preparing to marry again, he would be far too busy to think much about her. All that worried her was the pitiful spectacle of her mother, her uncle, and her cousin trying to make up lies to tell inquiring friends. Well, she would write today, that is, if she did not start to work, and she must get up at eight o’clock—was the alarm set?—and hie herself to an employment agency. She had only thirty-five dollars left in the bank, and, unless it was replenished, she might have to rescind her avowals to John in order to get her room paid.

She must go to sleep for another hour, for she wished to look “pert” when she applied for a job, especially the kind of job she wanted, and she must get the kind of job she wanted in order to show those people in Boise and Los Angeles that she had been perfectly justified in leaving school, home, and all, to come to New York. They all wondered why she had come. So did she, now that she was here. But at the moment of leaving she would have gone any place to escape having to remain in that hateful Southern California college, or having to face the more dreaded alternative of returning home. Home? It had never been a home.

It did seem strange, this being in Harlem when only a few weeks before she had been over three thousand miles away. Time and distance—strange things, immutable, yet conquerable. But was time conquerable? Hadn’t she read or heard somewhere that all things were subject to time, even God? Yet, once she was there and now she was here. But even at that she hadn’t conquered time. What was that line in Cullen’s verse, “I run, but Time’s abreast with me?” She had only traversed space and defied distance. This suggested a more banal, if a less arduous thought tangent. She had defied more than distance, she had defied parental restraint—still there hadn’t been much of that—friendly concern—there had been still less of that, and malicious, meddlesome gossip, of which there had been plenty. And she still found herself unable to understand why two sets of people in two entirely different communities should seemingly become almost hysterically excited because she, a woman of twenty-one, with three years’ college training and ample sophistication in the ways of sex and self-support, had decided to take a job as an actress’ maid in order to get to New York. They had never seemed interested in her before.

===Chinese===
她统共给家里写过两封信。这在她看来完全没什么不妥。那边她谁也不挂心，除了乔舅舅，而她盘算着，他既要再婚，必定忙得不可开交，哪有工夫惦记她。她唯一放心不下的，是那副可怜景象：母亲、舅舅和表姐得现编些谎话，去应付问东问西的朋友。好吧，今天就写，当然，前提是她还没开始上班；八点钟必须起床——闹钟定了吗？——然后奔职业介绍所去。银行里她只剩三十五美元，要是进项不补上，为了付房租，她说不定得收回对约翰的那番宣言。

她还得再睡一个钟头，因为去谋职时她想显得“俏丽精神”（pert），尤其是她想谋的那类差事；而她非谋到想谋的差事不可，好向博伊西和洛杉矶那些人证明：她辍了学、离开了家、抛开一切跑到纽约来，是完全有道理的。他们都纳闷她来干什么。如今她自己到了这儿，也纳闷起来。可在动身那一刻，只要能逃开那所可恨的南加州大学，逃开回家这条更可怕的退路，天涯海角她都肯去。家？那从来就不是个家。

这事确实透着古怪：如今人在哈莱姆，而几个星期前，她还在三千多英里之外。时间与距离——古怪的东西，不可移易，却又可以征服。可时间真能征服吗？她不是在哪儿读到过、听说过，万事皆臣服于时间，连上帝也不例外？然而，从前她身在那边，如今她身在这边。但即便如此，她也没能征服时间。卡伦的那句诗怎么说来着——“我奔跑，时间却与我并肩而行”？她不过是横越了空间，冒犯了距离。这一想又引出一条更俗气、却也不那么费力的思路。她冒犯的岂止是距离：她冒犯了父母的管束——其实那管束本也不多——冒犯了友人的关怀——那更是少得可怜——还冒犯了恶毒多事的长舌流言，这个倒有的是。而她至今不明白，为什么两个截然不同的地方的两拨人，会因为她——一个二十一岁的女子，受过三年大学教育，在情爱和自食其力两方面都相当老到——为了来纽约决意去给一位女演员当女佣，就激动得近乎歇斯底里。从前他们可从来没显得关心过她。

===Original===
Now she wondered why had she been so painfully anxious to come to New York. She had given as a consoling reason to inquisitive friends and relatives, school. But she knew too well that she had no intentions of ever reentering school. She had had enough of *that* school in Los Angeles, and her experiences there, more than anything else, had caused this foolhardy hegira to Harlem. She had been desperately driven to escape, and had she not escaped in this manner she might have done something else much more mad.

Emma Lou closed her eyes once more, and tried to sublimate her mental reverie into a sleep-inducing lullaby. Most of all, she wanted to sleep. One had to look “pert” when one sought a job, and she wondered if eight o’clock would find her looking any more “pert” than she did at this present moment. What had caused her to urge John to spend what she knew would be his last night with her when she was so determined to be at her best the following morning! O, what the hell was the use? She was going to sleep.

---

===Chinese===
如今她倒纳闷，当初自己为什么那样急不可耐、痛苦难当地非要来纽约。为了宽慰好奇的亲友，她给出的理由是学业。可她心里再清楚不过，自己压根不打算再回学校。洛杉矶*那*所学校她是受够了，而她在那儿的种种遭遇，比什么都更促成了这场冒失的大逃亡。她当时被逼得不顾一切只想逃走，倘若不是这样逃出来，她说不定会干出别的疯得多的事来。

埃玛·卢又闭上眼睛，试图把心里的浮想升华为一支催眠的摇篮曲。最要紧的是，她要睡着。谋职的人得显得“俏丽精神”，她怀疑到了八点钟，她又能比此刻精神到哪里去。明明打定主意第二天早晨要以最佳状态示人，她却撺掇约翰来度她明知是他的最后一夜，这是中了什么邪！唉，多想有什么用？睡觉。

---
===Original===
The alarm had not yet rung, but Emma Lou was awakened gradually by the sizzling and smell of fried and warmed-over breakfast, by the raucous early morning wranglings and window to window greetings, and by the almost constant squeak of those impudent hall floorboards as the various people in her apartment raced one another to the kitchen or to the bathroom or to the front door. How could Harlem be so happily busy, so alive and merry at eight o’clock. Eight o’clock? The alarm rang. Emma Lou scuttled out of the bed and put on her clothes.

An hour later, looking as “pert” as possible, she entered the first employment agency she came to on 135th Street, between Lenox and Seventh Avenues. It was her first visit to such an establishment and she was particularly eager to experience this phase of a working girl’s life. Her first four weeks in Harlem had convinced her that jobs were easy to find, for she had noticed that there were three or four employment agencies to every block in business Harlem. Assuring herself in this way that she would experience little difficulty in obtaining a permanent and tasty position, Emma Lou had abruptly informed Mazelle Lindsay that she was leaving her employ.

“But, child,” her employer had objected, “I feel responsible for you. Your—your mother! Don’t be preposterous. How can you remain in New York alone?”

===Chinese===
闹钟还没响，埃玛·卢却渐渐醒了——油煎的、回锅的早饭滋滋作响，气味钻进鼻子；清早的争吵粗声大嗓，窗口与窗口隔空互道早安；还有门厅里那些放肆的地板几乎不停的吱呀声——同住的人们你追我赶，奔厨房、奔浴室、奔大门。哈莱姆怎么到了八点钟还这样喜气洋洋地忙碌，这样有生气、这样欢腾。八点了？闹钟响了。埃玛·卢一骨碌爬下床，穿上衣服。

一小时后，她尽量把自己拾掇得“俏丽精神”，走进135街上她碰见的第一家职业介绍所——就在伦诺克斯大道和第七大道之间。这是她头一回去这种地方，格外想尝尝做工姑娘生涯的这一课。在哈莱姆的最初四个星期使她确信差事好找，因为她注意到，哈莱姆的商街，每一块都有三四家职业介绍所。她就这样宽慰自己，说谋一份长久又体面的位置不会费什么事，于是突然告知玛泽尔·林赛（Mazelle Lindsay），她要辞工了。

“可是孩子，”雇主反驳道，“我对你是有责任的。你——你母亲！别荒唐了。你一个人怎么能在纽约待下去？”

===Original===
Emma Lou had smiled, asked for her money once more, closed her ears to all protest, bid the chagrined woman goodbye, and joyously loafed for a week.

Now, with only thirty-five dollars left in the bank, she thought that she had best find a job—find a job and then finish seeing New York. Of course she had seen much already. She had seen John—and he—oh, damn John, she wanted a job.

“What can I do for you?” the harassed woman at the desk was trying to be polite.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢微微一笑，再讨了一回工钱，把一切抗议关在耳外，向这位懊恼的女人道了别，欢欢喜喜闲荡了一个星期。

如今银行里只剩三十五美元，她想最好还是赶紧找份差事——找了差事，再把纽约看完整。当然，她已经看了不少。约翰她也见识过了——还有他——唉，去他的约翰，她要的是差事。

“有什么事吗？”桌子后面那个被吵得头昏的女人竭力保持客气。

===Original===
“I—I want a job.” *R-r-ring.* The telephone insistently petitioned for attention, giving Emma Lou a moment of respite, while the machine-like woman wearily shouted monosyllabic answers into the instrument, and, at the same time, tried to hush the many loud-mouthed men and women in the room, all, it seemed, trying to out-talk one another. While waiting, Emma Lou surveyed her fellow job-seekers. Seedy lot, was her verdict. Perhaps I should have gone to a more high-toned place. Well, this will do for the moment.

“What kinda job d’ye want?”

“I prefer,” Emma Lou had rehearsed these lines for a week, “a stenographic position in some colored business or professional office.”

===Chinese===
“我——我想找份工作。”*铃——铃——*电话不依不饶地讨人理会，倒给了埃玛·卢片刻喘息；那位机器似的女人对着话筒，有气无力地喊出一个个单音节的答复，一边还得去压屋里许多大嗓门的男女——他们似乎人人都在抢着压倒旁人。等候的当口，埃玛·卢打量起同来谋职的人们。一群寒碜货，这是她的评语。也许该去家气派些的铺子。嗯，眼下这儿先将就吧。

“你要哪样的活儿？”

“我希望，”这套话埃玛·卢已排演了一个星期，“在有色人种的商行或事务所里谋一个速记员的职位。”

===Original===
“’Ny experience?”

“No, but I took two courses in business college, during school vacations. I have a certificate of competency.”

“’Ny reference?”

“No New York ones.”

===Chinese===
“有经验没？”

“没有。不过上学放假期间，我上过两期商学院的课程。我有合格证书。”

“有介绍人没？”

“纽约的没有。”

===Original===
“Where’d ya work before?”

“I—I just came to the city.”

“Where’d ya come …?” *R-r-ring.* The telephone mercifully reiterated its insistent blare, and, for a moment, kept that pesky woman from droning out more insulting queries.

“Now,” she had finished again, “where’d ya come from?”

===Chinese===
“你以前在哪儿做过事？”

“我——我刚到城里。”

“你从哪儿……”*铃——铃——*电话仁慈地又扯起它那不依不饶的鸣叫，一时拦住了那讨厌的女人，没让她再哼出些侮辱人的盘问来。

“好了，”她又应付完一个电话，“你从哪儿来？”

===Original===
“Los Angeles.”

“Ummm. What other kind of work would ya take?”

“Anything congenial.”

“Waal, what is that, dishwashing, day work, nurse girl?”

===Chinese===
“洛杉矶。”

“唔。别的活儿你肯干吗？”

“只要合意的都行。”

“嗐，那算个啥，洗碗、打日工、当保姆？”

===Original===
Didn’t this damn woman know what congenial meant? And why should a Jewish woman be in charge of a Negro employment agency in Harlem?

“Waal, girlie, others waiting.”

“I’ll consider anything you may have on hand, if stenographic work is not available.”

“Wanta work part-time?”

“I’d rather not.”

“Awright. Sit down. I’ll call you in a moment.”

===Chinese===
这该死的女人难道不懂“合意”是什么意思？再说，哈莱姆的黑人职业介绍所，凭什么让一个犹太女人当家？

“行了，小妞儿，后头还排着人呢。”

“要是没有速记的活儿，手头有什么我都可以考虑。”

“打半天工干不干？”

“我不想。”

“行啦。坐会儿。回头叫你。”

===Original===
“What can I do for you, young man?” Emma Lou was dismissed.

She looked for a place to sit down, and, finding none, walked across the narrow room to the window, hoping to get a breath of fresh air, and at the same time an advantageous position from which to watch the drama of someone else playing the role of a job-seeker.

*R-r-ring.*

“Whadda want? Wait a minute. Oh, Sadie.”

===Chinese===
“有什么事吗，年轻人？”——就这么把埃玛·卢打发了。

她找地方坐下，没找着，便穿过狭窄的屋子走到窗前，指望透一口新鲜空气，顺便占个有利位置，看看别人出演求职者这出戏。

*铃——铃——*

“要啥？等会儿。哦，萨迪。”

===Original===
A heavy set, dark-brown-skinned woman, with full, flopping breasts, and extra wide buttocks, squirmed off a too narrow chair, and bashfully wobbled up to the desk.

“Wanta’ go to a place on West End Avenue? Part-time cleaning, fifty cents an hour, nine rooms, yeah? All right? Hello, gotta girl on the way. Bye. Two and a half, Sadie, Here’s the address. Run along now, don’t idle.”

*R-r-ring.* “’Lo, yes. What? Come down to the office. I can’t sell jobs over the wire.”

===Chinese===
一个身材粗壮、深棕色皮肤的女人，奶子丰满耷拉，屁股格外宽大，从一把嫌窄的椅子里扭着挪下来，怯生生地摇晃着走到桌前。

“去西端大道一户人家干不干？半天清洁工，一小时五毛，九个房间，成不？好嘞？喂，姑娘这就派过去。再见。两个半钟头，萨迪，地址在这儿。快去吧，别磨蹭。”

*铃——铃——*“喂，是。什么？上所里来谈。电话里我可卖不成活儿。”
===Original===
Emma Lou began to see the humor in this sordid situation, began to see something extremely comic in all these plaintive, pitiful-appearing colored folk, some greasy, some neat, some fat, some slim, some brown, some black (why was there only one mulatto in this crowd?), boys and men, girls and women, all single-filing up to the desk, laconically answering laconic questions, impertinently put, showing thanks or sorrow or indifference, as their cases warranted, paying off promptly, or else seeking credit, the while the Jewish overseer of the dirty, dingy office asserted and reasserted her superiority.

Someone on the outside pushed hard on the warped door. Protestingly it came open, and the small stuffy room was filled with the odor and presence of a stout, black lady dressed in a greasy gingham housedress, still damp in the front from splashing dishwater. On her head was a tight turban, too round for the rather long outlines of her head. Beneath this turban could be seen short and wiry strands of recently straightened hair. And her face! Emma Lou sought to observe it more closely, sought to fathom how so much grease could gather on one woman’s face. But her head reeled. The room was vile with noise and heat and body-smells, and this woman—

“Hy, Rosie, Yer late. Got a job for ya.”

The greasy-faced black woman grinned broadly, licked her pork chop lips and, with a flourish, sat down in an empty chair beside the desk. Emma Lou stumbled over three pairs of number ten shoes, pulled open the door and fled into the street.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢开始看出这龌龊局面里的滑稽，开始看出这些愁眉苦脸、可怜巴巴的有色人种身上某种极度可笑的东西——有的油腻，有的齐整，有的胖，有的瘦，有的棕色，有的黑色（这一群人里怎么只有一个黑白混血？）——半大的孩子和男人，姑娘和妇人，一个个挨到桌前，用寥寥数字回答寥寥数字的问话，而那些问话又是出口无礼；接着按各人的情形，或道谢，或丧气，或无所谓，爽爽快快交钱，或者赊账——与此同时，这间肮脏昏暗的铺子里的犹太总管，则一遍又一遍地摆着她的威风。

外头有人使劲推那扇翘了边的门。门抗议似的开了，小小的闷屋子顿时充满一位黑大个太太的气味和身形——她穿着油渍麻花的格子家居裙，前襟还湿着，是涮洗碗水溅的。头上裹一顶紧头巾，圆滚滚的，衬得她那颗偏长的脑袋愈发不合衬。头巾底下露出几绺刚拉直的短而硬的头发。还有她那张脸！埃玛·卢想凑近瞧瞧，想弄明白一个人的脸上怎么积得出这么多油垢。可她头晕了。屋里满是噪音、热气和体味，熏得人发呕，而这个女人——

“嘿，罗西，你来晚啦。有你一份活儿。”

那油脸的黑女人咧嘴一笑，舔了舔猪排似的厚嘴唇，大模大样地在桌边一把空椅子上落了座。埃玛·卢绊过三双十号大鞋，拉开门逃到街上。

===Original===
She walked hurriedly for about twenty-five yards, then slowed down and tried to collect her wits. Telephone bells echoed in her ears. Sour smells infested her nostrils. She looked up and discovered that she had paused in front of two garbage cans, waiting on the curbstone for the scavenger’s truck.

Irritated, she turned around and retraced her steps. There were few people on the street. The early morning work crowds had already been swallowed by the subway kiosks on Lenox Avenue, and it was too early for the afternoon idlers. Yet there was much activity, much passing to and fro. 135th Street, Emma Lou mumbled to herself as she strolled along. How she had longed to see it, and what a different thoroughfare she had imagined it to be! Her eyes sought the opposite side of the street and blinked at a line of monotonously regular fire-escape decorated tenement buildings. She thanked whoever might be responsible for the architectural difference of the Y.M.C.A., for the streaming bit of Seventh Avenue near by, and for the arresting corner of the newly constructed teachers’ college building, which dominated the hill three blocks away, and cast its shadows on the verdure of the terraced park beneath.

But she was looking for a job. Sour smells assailed her nostrils once more. Rasping voices. Pleading voices. Tired voices. Domineering voices. And the insistent ring of the telephone bell all reechoed in her head and beat against her eardrums. She must have staggered, for a passing youth eyed her curiously, and shouted to no one in particular, “oh, *no*, now.” Someone else laughed. They thought she was drunk. Tears blurred her eyes. She wanted to run, but resolutely she kept her steady, slow pace, lifted her head a little higher, and, seeing another employment agency, faltered for a moment, then went in.

This agency, like the first, occupied the ground floor front of a tenement house, three-quarters of the way between Lenox and Seventh Avenue. It was cagey and crowded, and there was a great conversational hubbub as Emma Lou entered. In the rear of the room was a door marked “private,” to the left of this door was a desk, littered with papers and index cards, before which was a swivel chair. The rest of the room was lined with a miscellaneous assortment of chairs, three rows of them, tied together and trying to be precise despite their varying sizes and shapes. A single window looked out upon the street, and the Y.M.C.A. building opposite.

===Chinese===
她急急走出约莫二十五码，才放慢脚步，定一定神。电话铃声还在耳边回响。酸馊的气味侵占着她的鼻孔。她一抬头，发现自己正停在两只垃圾桶前——垃圾桶在路缘上等着清洁车。

她心头火起，转身往回走。街上人不多。清早上工的人潮早被伦诺克斯大道的地铁入口吞了下去，午后闲逛的闲人又还不到时辰。可街上依然一派忙碌，人来人往络绎不绝。135街——埃玛·卢一路溜达，一路喃喃自语。她曾多么渴望见识这条街，而她想象中的，又是何等不同的一条大道啊！她的目光投向街对面，对着一排式样单调划一、缀满防火梯的廉价公寓楼直眨眼。她暗暗感谢那些建起基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）的人——多亏它的式样别具一格——也感谢近旁第七大道川流不息的一角，还要感谢新建师范学院大楼那引人注目的街角：大楼雄踞三个街区外的山坡上，把影子投在下面梯形公园的绿荫上。

可她是要找差事的。酸馊气味又一次袭击她的鼻孔。刺耳的嗓音。哀求的嗓音。疲惫的嗓音。专横的嗓音。还有那不依不饶的电话铃声，一齐在她脑袋里回荡，撞着她的耳膜。她一定是踉跄了一下，因为一个过路的小伙子好奇地打量她，冲着不知谁嚷了一句：“噢，*别*这样嘛。”另有人笑了。他们当她是喝醉了。泪水模糊了她的眼睛。她想跑，却狠着心保持平稳的慢步，把头又抬高了一点，一眼瞧见又一家职业介绍所，迟疑了一瞬，走了进去。

这家介绍所和头一家一样，占着一幢廉价公寓楼的底层临街一面，位置在伦诺克斯大道到第七大道之间的四分之三处。屋里像笼子一样局促而拥挤，埃玛·卢进门时，一片嗡嗡的人声鼎沸。房间后头有一扇标着“闲人免进”的门，门左边一张书桌，堆满纸片和索引卡，桌前一把转椅。屋子其余地方排着一溜各式椅子，共三排，用绳子拴在一起，尽管大小形状参差，还竭力想排得整齐。一扇独窗朝街，望得见对面的基督教青年会大楼。

===Original===
All of the chairs were occupied and three people stood lined up by the desk. Emma Lou fell in at the end of this line. There was nothing else to do. In fact, it was all she could do after entering. Not another person could have been squeezed into that room from the outside. This office too was noisy and hot and pregnant with clashing body smells. The buzzing electric fan, in a corner over the desk, with all its whirring, could not stir up a breeze.

The rear door opened. A slender, light-brown-skinned boy, his high cheekbones decorated with blackheads, his slender form accentuated by a tight fitting jazz suit of the high-waistline, one-button coat, bellbottom trouser variety, emerged smiling broadly, cap in one hand, a slip of pink paper in the other. He elbowed his way to the outside door and was gone.

“Musta got a job,” somebody commented. “It’s about time,” came from someone else, “he said he’d been sittin’ here a week.”

The rear door opened again and a lady with a youthful brown face and iron-gray hair sauntered in and sat down in the swivel chair before the desk. Immediately all talk in the outer office ceased. An air of anticipation seemed to pervade the room. All eyes were turned toward her.

===Chinese===
椅子全坐满了，还有三个人在桌边站成排。埃玛·卢排到队尾。别无他法。说实在的，进了门她也只能做到这一步——再从外头塞一个人进来，这屋子也容不下了。这间铺子照旧又吵又热，充斥着彼此冲撞的体味。书桌上方角落里那台嗡嗡作响的电扇，任凭怎么呼呼地转，也扇不出一丝风来。

后门开了。一个瘦长的浅棕色皮肤的小伙子走了出来，高额骨上缀着黑头粉刺，一身紧巴巴的爵士装——高腰线、单排一粒扣的上衣、喇叭裤——把他瘦长的身条裹得愈发分明。他咧着大嘴笑，一只手捏着帽子，另一只手捏一条粉红纸片。他侧着膀子挤到大门，一转眼没影了。

“准是找着活儿了，”有人搭话。“也该轮着他了，”另一个声音说，“他说他在这儿干坐了一个星期。”

后门又开了，一位太太慢悠悠踱进来，棕色的脸庞不见老，头发却已铁灰。她在桌前那把转椅上落座。外间的话声顿时全停了。一屋子似乎弥漫着屏息期待的空气。所有的眼睛都转向她。

===Original===
For a moment she fingered a pack of red index cards, then, as if remembering something, turned around in her chair and called out:

“Mrs. Blake says for all elevator men to stick around.”

There was a shuffling of feet and a settling back into chairs. Noticing this, Emma Lou counted six elevator men and wondered if she was right. Again the brown aristocrat with the tired voice spoke up:

“Day workers come back at one-thirty. Won’t be nothing doin’ ’til then.”

Four women, all carrying newspaper packages, got out of their chairs, and edged their way toward the door, murmuring to one another as they went, “I ain’t fixin’ to come back.”

“Ah, she keeps you hyar.”

===Chinese===
她拨弄了一会儿一叠红色索引卡，随后像想起了什么，在椅子里转过身，扬声喊道：

“布莱克太太说，开电梯的都别走远。”

一阵挪脚步的窸窣，众人又坐回椅子里。看在眼里，埃玛·卢数出六个开电梯的，又拿不准数得对不对。那位嗓音疲惫的棕肤贵妇又开了口：

“做日工的一点半再来。那之前没活儿。”

四个女人，人人拎着报纸包的包裹，从椅子上起身，侧着身子往门口挪，一路低声嘀咕：“我可不打算再跑一趟。”

“嗐，她这是留你在这儿干耗呢。”

===Original===
They were gone.

Two of the people standing in line sat down, the third approached the desk, Emma Lou close behind.

“I wantsa—”

“What kind of job do you want?”

===Chinese===
她们走了。

站队的两个坐下了，第三个走向桌前，埃玛·卢紧跟在后。

“我想讨个——”

“你要什么差事？”

===Original===
Couldn’t people ever finish what they had to say?

“Porter or dishwashing, lady.”

“Are you registered with us?”

“No’m.”

“Have a seat. I’ll call you in a moment.”

===Chinese===
人们说话，就永远不能让人把话说完吗？

“勤杂工，或者洗碗，太太。”

“在我们这儿登记过没有？”

“没呢，太太。”

“坐会儿。回头叫你。”

===Original===
The boy looked frightened, but he found a seat and slid into it gratefully. Emma Lou approached the desk. The woman’s cold eyes appraised her. She must have been pleased with what she saw for her eyes softened and her smile reappeared. Emma Lou smiled, too. Maybe she was “pert” after all. The tailored blue suit—

“What can I do for you?”

The voice with the smile wins. Emma Lou was encouraged.

“I would like stenographic work.”

“Experienced?”

===Chinese===
那小伙子面有惧色，但还是找到位子，感激地一屁股溜了进去。埃玛·卢走向桌前。那女人冷冷的眼睛把她上下打量。想必是看得中意，她的眼神柔和下来，笑容又回到了脸上。埃玛·卢也报以微笑。看来她到底还是“俏丽精神”的。那身定做的蓝套装——

“有什么事吗？”

带着笑的嗓音最占便宜。埃玛·卢受了鼓舞。

“我想做速记的工作。”

“有经验吗？”

===Original===
“Yes.” It was so much easier to say than “no.”

“Good.”

Emma Lou held tightly to her underarm bag.

“We have something that would just about suit you. Just a minute, and I’ll let you see Mrs. Blake.”

===Chinese===
“有。”说“有”总比说“没有”省事得多。

“很好。”

埃玛·卢紧紧攥着腋下的挎包。

“我们手头有个位置，简直正合你。稍等，我带你去见布莱克太太。”
===Original===
The chair squeaked and was eased of its burden. Emma Lou thought she heard a telephone ringing somewhere in the distance, or perhaps it was the clang of the streetcar that had just passed, heading for Seventh Avenue. The people in the room began talking again.

“Dat last job.” “Boy, she was dressed right down to the bricks.”

“And I told him.…” “Yeah, we went to see ‘Flesh and the Devil.’” “Some parteee.” “I just been here a week.”

Emma Lou’s mind became jumbled with incoherent wisps of thought. Her left foot beat a nervous tattoo upon a sagging floorboard. The door opened. The gray-haired lady with the smile in her voice beckoned, and Emma Lou walked into the private office of Mrs. Blake.

===Chinese===
转椅吱呀一声，卸下了它的负担。埃玛·卢仿佛听见远处什么地方电话在响，又或许只是刚驶过去、正朝第七大道开的那辆有轨电车哐当一响。屋里的人又说起话来。

“上一份差事。”“乖乖，她那身衣裳穿得，齐整到家了。”

“我就跟他讲了……”“可不，我们去看了《肉体与魔鬼》。”“派对真带劲。”“我来了才一个礼拜。”

埃玛·卢脑子里塞满东拉西扯的碎念，乱作一团。她的左脚在一块下陷的地板上紧张地踏着点子。门开了。那位嗓音里带笑的灰发太太招了招手，埃玛·卢便走进了布莱克太太的私室。

===Original===
Four people in the room. The only window facing a brick wall on the outside. Two telephones, both busy. A good-looking young man, fingering papers in a filing cabinet, while he talked over one of the telephones. The lady from the outer office. Another lady, short and brown, like butterscotch, talking over a desk telephone and motioning for Emma Lou to sit down. Blur of high powered electric lights, brighter than daylight. The butterscotch lady hanging up the receiver.

“I’m through with you young man.” Crisp tones. Metal, warm in spite of itself.

“Well, I ain’t through with you.” The fourth person was speaking. Emma Lou had hardly noticed him before. Sullen face. Dull black eyes in watery sockets. The nose flat, the lips thick and pouting. One hand clutching a derby, the other clenched, bearing down on the corner of the desk.

“I have no intention of arguing with you. I’ve said my say. Go on outside. When a cook’s job comes in, you can have it. That’s all I can do.”

“No, it ain’t all you can do.”

“Well, I’m not going to give you your fee back.”

===Chinese===
屋里四个人。唯一的窗子朝着一堵砖墙。两部电话，都占着线。一个俊俏的年轻男人，一边对着其中一部电话说话，一边在文件柜里翻弄纸页。外间那位太太。还有一位太太，个子矮小，棕色皮肤，像块奶油太妃糖，正对着桌上的电话讲话，一面示意埃玛·卢坐。大功率电灯白花花一片，比日光还亮。奶糖色太太挂上了听筒。

“你的事完了，年轻人。”干脆利落的声调。金属一般，却又不由自主地带着暖意。

“哼，你的事完了，我的还没完。”第四个人说话了。埃玛·卢先前几乎没留意他。一张阴沉的脸。水汪汪的眼窝里一双呆滞的黑眼睛。鼻梁扁平，嘴唇厚而噘着。一只手攥着圆顶礼帽，另一只手握成拳，死死抵住桌角。

“我没打算跟你吵。话我说完了。出去吧。有了厨子的活儿，就给你。我能做的就这些。”

“不，你能做的不止这些。”

“反正，介绍费我是不退你的。”

===Original===
The lady from the outside office returns to her post. The good-looking young man is at the telephone again.

“Why not, I’m entitled to it.”

“No, you’re not. I send you on a job, the man asks you to do something, you walk out, Mister Big I-am. Then, show up here two days later and want your fee back. No siree.”

“I didn’t walk out.”

“The man says you did.”

“Aw, sure, he’d say anything. I told him I came there to be a cook, not a waiter. I—”

===Chinese===
外间那位太太回到了自己的岗位。俊俏的年轻男人又拿起了电话。

“凭什么不退，那钱是我应得的。”

“你不应得。我派你去上工，东家让你做点事，你掉头就走，自居大爷的主儿。过了两天倒跑到这儿来，要退介绍费。没门儿。”

“我没掉头就走。”

“东家说你走了。”

“嗐，可不是嘛，他什么话说不出来。我跟他说了，我是来当厨子的，不是来当跑堂的。我——”

===Original===
“It was your place to do as he said, then, if not satisfied, to come here and tell me so.”

“I am here.”

“All right now. I’m tired of this. Take either of two courses—go on outside and wait until a job comes in or else go down to the license bureau and tell them your story. They’ll investigate. If I’m right—”

“You know you ain’t right.”

“Not according to you, no, but by law, yes. That’s all.”

===Chinese===
“你的本分就是照他说的做，要是委屈，再来这儿跟我说。”

“我这不是来了。”

“好了。这一套我受够了。两条路随你挑——要么出去等着来活儿，要么上执照局去讲你的理。他们会调查的。要是我占理——”

“你自己知道你不占理。”

“照你说不占理，照法律占理。就这样。”

===Original===
Telephone ringing. Warm metal whipping words into it. The good-looking young man yawning. He looks like a Y.M.C.A. secretary. The butterscotch woman speaking to Emma Lou:

“You’re a stenographer?”

“Yes.”

“I have a job in a real estate office, nice firm, nice people. Fill out this card. Here’s a pen.”

“Mrs. Blake, you know you ain’t doin’ right.”

Why didn’t this man either shut up or get out?

===Chinese===
电话铃响。温热的金属里抽打着话语。俊俏的年轻男人打着呵欠。他看着像个青年会干事。奶糖色女人对埃玛·卢说话了：

“你是做速记的？”

“是。”

“我手头有个房地产事务所的活儿，铺子体面，人也体面。把这张卡片填了。笔在这儿。”

“布莱克太太，你自己知道，你这事办得不地道。”

这男人怎么就不能闭嘴，或者干脆滚出去？

===Original===
“I told you what to do. Now please do one or the other. You’ve taken up enough of my time. The license bureau—”

“You know I ain’t goin’ down there. I’d rather you keep the fee, if you think it will do you any good.”

“I only keep what belongs to me. I’ve found out that’s the best policy.”

Why should they want three people for reference? Where had she worked before? Lies. Los Angeles was far away.

“Then, if a job comes in you’ll give it to me?”

===Chinese===
“该怎么办我告诉过你了。现在请你二选一。我的时间你占得够多了。执照局——”

“你知道我不会去的。那笔钱你留着吧，要是你觉得它对你有什么好处。”

“我只留属于我的东西。我算是明白了，这才是最稳妥的方针。”

为什么要三个介绍人？她从前在哪儿做过事？谎话。洛杉矶远着呢。

“那么，来了活儿你会给我？”

===Original===
“That’s what I’ve been trying to tell you.”

“Awright.” And finally he went out.

Mrs. Blake grinned across the desk at Emma Lou. “Your folks won’t do, honey.”

“Do you have many like that?”

===Chinese===
“我一直想跟你说的就是这个。”

“行吧。”他总算走了出去。

布莱克太太隔着桌子朝埃玛·卢咧嘴一笑：“你那几位保人可不成啊，亲爱的。”

“这样的人你这儿多吗？”

===Original===
The card was made out. Mrs. Blake had it in her hand. Telephones ringing, both at once. Loud talking in the outer office. Lies. Los Angeles was far away. I can bluff. Mrs. Blake had finished reading over the card.

“Just came to New York, eh?”

“Yes.”

“Like it better than Los Angeles?”

===Chinese===
卡片填好了。布莱克太太拿在手里。两部电话同时响起来。外间话声嘈杂。谎话。洛杉矶远着呢。我可以蒙混过去。布莱克太太把卡片从头看完了。

“刚来纽约，嗯？”

“是。”

“比洛杉矶喜欢这儿？”

===Original===
The good-looking young man turned around and stared at her coldly. Now he did resemble a Y.M.C.A. secretary. The lady from the outer office came in again. There was a triple crisscross conversation carried on. It ended. The short bob-haired butterscotch boss gave Emma Lou instructions and information about her prospective position. She was half heard. Sixteen dollars a week. Is that all? Work from nine to five. Address on card. Corner of 139th Street, left side of the avenue. Dismissal. Smiles and good luck. Pay the lady outside five dollars: Awkward, flustered moments. Then the entrance door and 135th Street once more. Emma Lou was on her way to get a job.

---

===Chinese===
俊俏的年轻男人转过身来，冷冷地盯着她。这会儿他可真像青年会干事了。外间那位太太又进来了。三个人你来我往、横七竖八地聊了一通。聊完了。这位剪着短发的奶糖色矮个子上司，向埃玛·卢交代她那未来差事的种种事项和情况。她只听进去一半。一星期十六美元。就这点？九点到五点上班。地址在卡片上。139街拐角，大道左侧。打发她走。笑脸和好运。付给外间那位太太五美元：一阵局促慌乱。然后是大门，又是135街。埃玛·卢这就去找那份差事了。

---
===Original===
She walked briskly to the corner, crossed the street and turned north on Seventh Avenue. Her hopes were high, her mind a medley of pleasing mental images. She visualized herself trim and pert in her blue tailored suit being secretary to some well-groomed Negro businessman. There had not been many such in the West, and she was eager to know and admire one. There would be other girls in the office, too, girls who, like herself, were college trained and reared in cultured homes, and through these fellow workers she would meet still other girls and men, get in with the right sort of people.

She continued daydreaming as she went her way, being practical only at such fleeting moments when she would wonder—would she be able to take dictation at the required rate of speed?—would her fingers be nimble enough on the keyboard of the typewriter? Oh, bother. It wouldn’t take her over one day to adapt herself to her new job.

A street crossing. Traffic delayed her and she was conscious of a man, a blurred tan image, speaking to her. He was ignored. Everything was to be ignored save the address digits on the buildings. Everything was secondary to the business at hand. Let traffic pass, let men aching for flirtations speak, let Seventh Avenue be spangled with forenoon sunshine and shadow, and polka-dotted with still or moving human forms. She was going to have a job. The rest of the world could go to hell.

===Chinese===
她轻快地走到街角，过了马路，沿第七大道往北拐。她兴致高昂，脑子里一团讨人喜欢的幻象。她想象自己穿着那身定做的蓝套装，利落俏丽，给某位衣冠楚楚的黑人实业家当女秘书。西部这种人可不多，她巴不得结识一位、仰慕一位。事务所里还会有别的姑娘，同她一样受过大学教育、在体面人家养大；通过这些同事，她还会结识更多的姑娘和男人，挤进正经人的圈子。

她一路走，一路接着做梦，只在几个倏忽而过的当口务实一下——口述记录要求的速度，她跟得上吗？——她的手指在打字机键盘上，够不够灵巧？唉，烦什么。用不了一天，她就能把新差事应付自如。

一处街口。车流耽搁了她，她察觉有个男人在跟她搭话，一团棕褐色的模糊影子。她没理睬。什么都可以不理，只有楼门上的门牌数字不能不理。眼前这桩事高于一切。让车流过去吧，让心痒难搔想调情的男人去说吧，让第七大道上午的阳光和阴影洒成一片亮点，让或静或动的人形缀成圆点吧。她就要有一份差事了。世界其余的部分都见鬼去。

===Original===
Emma Lou turned into a four-story brick building and sped up one flight of stairs. The rooms were not numbered and directing signs in the hallway only served to confuse. But Emma Lou was not to be delayed. She rushed back and forth from door to door on the first floor, then to the second, until she finally found the office she was looking for.

Angus and Brown were an old Harlem real estate firm. They had begun business during the first decade of the century, handling property for a while in New York’s far-famed San Juan Hill district. When the Negro population had begun to need more and better homes, Angus and Brown had led the way in buying real estate in what was to be Negro Harlem. They had been fighters, unscrupulous and canny. They had revealed a perverse delight in seeing white people rush pell-mell from the neighborhood in which they obtained homes for their colored clients. They had bought three six-story tenement buildings on 140th Street, and, when the white tenants had been slow in moving, had personally dispossessed them, and, in addition, had helped their incoming Negro tenants fight fistic battles in the streets and hallways, and legal battles in the court.

Now they were a substantial firm, grown fat and satisfied. Junior real estate men got their business for them. They held the whip. Their activities were many and varied. Politics and fraternal activities occupied more of their time than did real estate. They had had their hectic days. Now they sat back and took it easy.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢拐进一幢四层的砖楼，噔噔上了一层楼梯。房间没有编号，门厅里的指示牌只会把人绕糊涂。可什么也拦不住埃玛·卢。她在一楼挨门挨门地来回找，又上二楼，终于找到了她要的那间事务所。

安格斯和布朗（Angus and Brown）是哈莱姆一家老牌房地产行。本世纪头一个十年里开张，一度在纽约赫赫有名的圣胡安山地区经管房产。等到黑人大众开始需要更多、更好的住所，安格斯和布朗又一马当先，在日后成为黑人哈莱姆的地界置办房产。他们是斗士，既不讲信义又精明狡黠。眼见白人从他们为有色人种主顾弄到住房的街区狼狈溃逃，他们流露出一种乖张的快意。他们在140街买下三幢六层的公寓楼，白人房客搬得拖沓，他们便亲自上门撵人，不仅如此，还帮着新搬进来的黑人房客在街上和楼道里打拳脚仗，在法庭上打官司。

如今他们是家殷实的商号，养得脑满肠肥，心满意足。跑腿的年轻地产经纪人替他们兜揽生意。鞭子握在他们手里。他们的营生五花八门。政治和兄弟会的事务占去他们的时间，比房地产还多。忙碌折腾的岁月他们也有过。如今他们跷起二郎腿，坐享清福。

===Original===
Emma Lou opened the door to their office, consisting of one medium-sized outer room overlooking 139th Street and two cubby holes overlooking Seventh Avenue. There were two girls in the outer office. One was busy at a typewriter; the other was gazing over her desk through a window into the aristocratic tree-lined city lane of 139th Street. Both looked up expectantly. Emma Lou noticed the powdered smoothness of their fair skins and the marcelled waviness of their shingled brown hair. Were they sisters? Hardly, for their features were in no way similar. Yet that skin color and that brown hair—.

“Can I do something for you?” The idle one spoke, and the other ceased her peck-peck-pecking on the typewriter keys. Emma Lou was buoyant.

“I’m from Mrs. Blake’s employment agency.”

“Oh,” from both. And they exchanged glances. Emma Lou thought she saw a quickly suppressed smile from the fairer of the two as she hastily resumed her typing. Then—

“Sit down a moment, won’t you, please? Mr. Angus is out but I’ll inform Mr. Brown that you are here.” She picked a powder puff from an open side drawer in her desk, patted her nose and cheeks, then got up and crossed the office to enter cubby hole number one. Emma Lou observed that she, too, looked “pert” in a trim, blue suit and high-heeled patent leather oxfords—

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢推开他们事务所的门：一间中等大小的外间，窗子朝139街，还有两个小隔间，窗子朝第七大道。外间坐着两个姑娘。一个忙着打字；另一个从桌子上方透过窗子，望向139街那条绿树成荫的贵族气派小街。两人都满怀期待地抬起头。埃玛·卢注意到她们浅色皮肤扑粉后的光洁，和那剪成齐颈短发、烫出马塞尔波浪的棕色头发的卷曲。她们是姐妹吗？不大像，两人的眉眼毫无相似之处。可那肤色，那棕发——。

“有什么要帮忙的吗？”闲着的那位开了口，另一位也停下了在打字机键上啄个不停的指尖。埃玛·卢心里一轻快。

“我是布莱克太太职业介绍所介绍来的。”

“哦。”两人齐声应道，还交换了一个眼色。埃玛·卢仿佛看见两人中肤色较浅的那位匆匆压下一抹笑，赶紧接着打她的字。接着——

“请稍坐一会儿，好吗？安格斯先生出去了，我去通报布朗先生你来了。”她从办公桌敞开的抽屉里拈出一个粉扑，扑了扑鼻子和脸颊，起身穿过外间，走进一号小隔间。埃玛·卢瞧见她也“俏丽精神”——一身利落的蓝套装，脚上一双漆皮高跟系带鞋——

===Original===
“Mr. Brown?” She had opened the door.

“Come in Grace. What is it?” The door was closed.

Emma Lou felt nervous. Something in the pit of her stomach seemed to flutter. Her pulse raced. Her eyes gleamed and a smile of anticipation spread over her face, despite her efforts to appear dignified and suave. The typist continued her work. From the cubby hole came a murmur of voices, one feminine and affected, the other masculine and coarse. Through the open window came direct sounds and vagrant echoes of traffic noises from Seventh Avenue. Now the two in the cubby hole were laughing, and the girl at the typewriter seemed to be smiling to herself as she worked.

What did this mean? Nothing, silly. Don’t be so sensitive. Emma Lou’s eyes sought the pictures on the wall. There was an early twentieth century photographic bust-portrait, encased in a bevelled glass frame, of a heavyset good-looking, brown-skinned man. She admired his mustache. Men didn’t seem to take pride in such hirsute embellishments now. Mustaches these days were abbreviated and limp. They no longer were virile enough to dominate and make a man’s face appear more strong. Rather, they were only insignificant patches weakly keeping the nostrils from merging with the upper lip.

===Chinese===
“布朗先生？”她推开了门。

“进来吧，格雷斯。什么事？”门关上了。

埃玛·卢紧张起来。胃底下仿佛有什么东西在扑腾。脉搏加快了。她眼里放光，一脸期待的笑意漫开来，尽管她还竭力想显得庄重从容。打字员照旧做她的活。小隔间里传出一阵低语，一个嗓音女气而做作，另一个男声而粗哑。敞开的窗子送进来第七大道上车马喧嚣的声响和四处游荡的回音。这会儿隔间里那两个笑了起来，打字机前的姑娘一边工作，一边似乎也在独自微笑。

这是什么意思？没什么意思，傻丫头。别这么神经过敏。埃玛·卢的眼睛去找墙上挂的照片。有一幅二十世纪初的半身照，嵌在斜边玻璃相框里，是个魁梧俊朗的棕肤色男人。她羡慕他的小胡子。如今的男人似乎已不屑于在这种须发装饰上引以为豪了。时下的小胡子都剪得短短、蔫塌塌的，再没有足够的雄风镇住一张脸、使它显得更刚强。倒不如说，那不过是几小撮无足轻重的毛，有气无力地拦着鼻孔，别让它跟上唇连成一片。

===Original===
Emma Lou wondered if that was Mr. Brown. He had a brown face and wore a brown suit. No, maybe that was Mr. Angus, and perhaps that was Mr. Brown on the other side of the room, in the square, enlarged kodak print, a slender yellow man, standing beside a motor car, looking as if he wished to say, “Yeah, this is me and this is my car.” She hoped he was Mr. Angus. She didn’t like his name and since she was to see Mr. Brown first, she hoped he was the more flatteringly portrayed.

The door to the cubby hole opened and the girl Mr. Brown had called Grace, came out. The expression on her face was too businesslike to be natural. It seemed as if it had been placed there for a purpose.

She walked toward Emma Lou, who got up and stood like a child, waiting for punishment and hoping all the while that it will dissipate itself in threats. The typewriter was stilled and Emma Lou could feel an extra pair of eyes looking at her. The girl drew close then spoke:

“I’m sorry, Miss. Mr. Brown says he has someone else in view for the job. We’ll call the agency. Thank you for coming in.”

Thank her for coming in? What could she say? What should she say? The girl was smiling at her, but Emma Lou noticed that her fair skin was flushed and that her eyes danced nervously. Could she be hoping that Emma Lou would hurry and depart? The door was near. It opened easily. The steps were steep. One went down slowly. Seventh Avenue was still spangled with forenoon sunshine and shadow. Its pavement was hard and hot. The windows in the buildings facing it, gleaming reflectors of the mounting sun.

---

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢琢磨着那是不是布朗先生。此人棕色的脸，穿一身棕色西装。不对，没准那是安格斯先生，而屋子那一头方形放大的柯达相片里的，恐怕才是布朗先生——一个瘦长的黄肤男人，站在一辆汽车旁边，那副神情仿佛想说：“没错，这就是我，这就是我的车。”她但愿他是安格斯先生。她不喜欢他这个名字；而既然要先见的是布朗先生，她便希望照得比较体面的那一位是布朗先生。

小隔间的门开了，被布朗先生唤作格雷斯的那位姑娘走了出来。她脸上的神情公事公办得太过了，反倒不自然，仿佛是特意摆上去、别有用意似的。

她朝埃玛·卢走来。埃玛·卢站起身，像个孩子似的立着，等着受罚，一面又一直盼着这惩罚能化作几句呵斥了事。打字机静了下来，埃玛·卢觉出多出一双眼睛在看她。姑娘走近了，开口道：

“对不起，小姐。布朗先生说这个位置另有人选了。我们会通知介绍所。谢谢你跑一趟。”

谢她跑一趟？她能说什么？她该说什么？姑娘还朝她微笑，可埃玛·卢注意到她那浅色的皮肤涨红了，眼睛慌乱地闪动。她是不是正盼着埃玛·卢识趣快走？门就在近处。开起来很容易。台阶很陡。得慢慢下去。第七大道依旧洒满午前的阳光和阴影。人行道又硬又烫。临街楼上的窗子，一面面亮闪闪的反光镜，映着步步升高的太阳。

---
===Original===
Emma Lou returned to the employment agency. It was still crowded and more stuffy than ever. The sun had advanced high into the sky and it seemed to be centering its rays on that solitary defenseless window. There was still much conversation. There were still people crowded around the desk, still people in all the chairs, people and talk and heat and smells.

“Mrs. Blake is waiting for you,” the gray-haired lady with the young face was unflustered and cool. Emma Lou went into the inner office. Mrs. Blake looked up quickly and forced a smile. The good-looking young man, more than ever resembling a Y.M.C.A. secretary, turned his back and fumbled with the card files. Mrs. Blake suggested that he leave the room. He did, beaming benevolently at Emma Lou as he went.

“I’m sorry,” Mrs. Blake was very kind and womanly. “Mr. Brown called me. I didn’t know he had someone else in mind. He hadn’t told me.”

“That’s all right,” replied Emma Lou briskly. “Have you something else?”

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢回到那家职业介绍所。屋里照旧拥挤，比先前更加闷热。太阳已经升到半空，仿佛把光线全聚在那扇孤零零、无处躲藏的窗子上。人声照旧鼎沸。桌子四周照旧围着人，椅子上照旧坐满了人，人、话声、热气、气味，一样不少。

“布莱克太太等着你呢。”那位脸庞不见老的灰发太太不慌不忙，冷冷静静。埃玛·卢走进里面的办公室。布莱克太太倏地抬头，挤出一个笑。那个俊俏的年轻男人——比先前更像个青年会干事了——转过身去，装模作样地翻卡片柜。布莱克太太示意他出去。他出去了，临走还朝埃玛·卢慈祥地咧嘴一笑。

“真对不起，”布莱克太太十分和气，一副女人心肠。“布朗先生给我来了电话。我不知道他心里另有人选。他先前没跟我说。”

“没关系，”埃玛·卢干脆地答道，“您还有别的吗？”

===Original===
“Not now. Er-er. Have you had luncheon? It’s early yet, I know, but I generally go about this time. Come along, won’t you, I’d like to talk to you. I’ll be ready in about thirty minutes if you don’t mind the wait.”

Emma Lou warmed to the idea. At that moment, she would have warmed toward any suggestion of friendliness. Here, perhaps, was a chance to make a welcome contact. She was lonesome and disappointed, so she readily assented and felt elated and superior as she walked out of the office with the “boss.”

They went to Eddie’s for luncheon. Eddie’s was an elbow-shaped combination lunch-counter and dining room that embraced a United Cigar Store on the northeast corner of 135th Street and Seventh Avenue. Following Mrs. Blake’s lead, Emma Lou ordered a full noontime dinner, and, flattered by Mrs. Blake’s interest and congeniality, began to talk about herself. She told of her birthplace and her home life. She told of her high school days, spoke proudly of the fact that she had been the only Negro student and how she had graduated cum laude. Asked about her college years, she talked less freely. Mrs. Blake sensed a cue.

===Chinese===
“眼下没有。呃——呃。用过午饭没有？我知道还早，可我平时差不多这个钟点就去。一起去吧，好吗？我想跟你聊聊。你要是不嫌等，我大约半个钟头就好。”

埃玛·卢对这个提议来了兴致。那一刻，只要有人示好，什么提议她都会来劲。这没准是个结交的好机会，求之不得。她又孤单又失望，于是爽快答应了，跟“上司”一同走出办公室时，只觉得飘飘然，还高人一等似的。

两人去埃迪餐馆（Eddie’s）吃午饭。埃迪餐馆是个肘拐形的铺面，快餐柜台连着饭堂，拢住了135街和第七大道东北角上的一家联合雪茄店。埃玛·卢跟着布莱克太太点了一份齐全的午市正餐，又受宠于布莱克太太的兴趣和投缘，便说起自己来。她说起自己的出生地和家里的事，说起高中时代，还颇为自豪地说起她是全校唯一的黑人学生，又如何以优等成绩（cum laude）毕业。问到大学那几年，她的话就少了。布莱克太太听出了端倪。

===Original===
“Didn’t you like college?”

“For a little while, yes.”

“What made you dislike it? Surely not the studies?”

“No.” She didn’t care to discuss this. “I was lonesome, I guess.”

“Weren’t there any other colored boys and girls? I thought.…”

Emma Lou spoke curtly. “Oh, yes, quite a number, but I suppose I didn’t mix well.”

===Chinese===
“你不喜欢大学生活吗？”

“喜欢过一阵子。”

“怎么又不喜欢了？总不会是功课吧？”

“不是。”她不愿谈这个。“我想，是太孤单了吧。”

“那儿就没有别的有色人种的男孩女孩吗？我还以为……”

埃玛·卢答得干脆：“哦，有，还不少呢，不过我看是我跟人处不来。”

===Original===
The waiter came to take the order for dessert, and Emma Lou seized upon the fact that Mrs. Blake ordered sliced oranges to talk about California’s orange groves, California’s sunshine—anything but the California college she had attended and from which she had fled. In vain did Mrs. Blake try to maneuver the conversation back to Emma Lou’s college experiences. She would have none of it and Mrs. Blake was finally forced to give it up.

When they were finished, Mrs. Blake insisted upon taking the check. This done, she began to talk about jobs.

“You know, Miss Morgan, good jobs are rare. It is seldom I have anything to offer outside of the domestic field. Most Negro business offices are family affairs. They either get their help from within their own family group or from among their friends. Then, too,” Emma Lou noticed that Mrs. Blake did not look directly at her, “lots of our Negro businessmen have a definite type of girl in mind and will not hire any other.”

Emma Lou wondered what it was Mrs. Blake seemed to be holding back. She began again:

===Chinese===
侍者上来请点甜品，埃玛·卢抓住布莱克太太点了橙片这件事，大谈起加利福尼亚的橙园、加利福尼亚的阳光——什么都谈，就是不谈她上过又逃离的那所加州大学。布莱克太太白费心机，想把话头引回埃玛·卢的大学经历。她偏不接茬，布莱克太太最后只好作罢。

饭罢，布莱克太太执意会了账。办完这事，她谈起差事来。

“您知道的，摩根小姐，好差事难得。除了帮佣这一行，我手里很少有别的可荐。黑人的商号多半是家族营生，用人都从自家骨肉里挑，要不就从朋友里挑。再说，”埃玛·卢注意到布莱克太太的眼睛并不直视她，“我们不少黑人实业家心里都认定了要哪一类姑娘，旁的一概不要。”

埃玛·卢寻思，布莱克太太这样吞吞吐吐，究竟藏着什么话。她又开了口：

===Original===
“My advice to you is that you enter Teachers’ College and if you *will* stay in New York, get a job in the public school system. You can easily take a light job of some kind to support you through your course. Maybe with three years’ college you won’t need to go to training school. Why don’t you find out about that? Now, if I were you.…” Mrs. Blake talked on, putting much emphasis on every “If I were you.”

Emma Lou grew listless and antagonistic. She didn’t like this little sawed-off woman as she was now, being businesslike and giving advice. She was glad when they finally left Eddie’s, and more than glad to escape after having been admonished not to oversleep, “But be in my office, and I’ll see what I can do for you, dearie, early in the morning. There’s sure to be something.”

Left to herself, Emma Lou strolled south on the west side of Seventh Avenue to 134th Street, then crossed over to the east side and turned north. She didn’t know what to do. It was too late to consider visiting another employment agency, and, furthermore, she didn’t have enough money left to pay another fee. Let jobs go until tomorrow, then she would return to Mrs. Blake’s, ask for a return of her fee, and find some other employment agency, a more imposing one, if possible. She had had enough of those on 135th Street.

===Chinese===
“我劝你进师范学院；要是你*一定*要留在纽约，就在公立学校系统里谋个教职。随便找份轻省活儿，供你读完全程不难。念过三年大学，也许连师范培训都不用再上。何不去打听打听？唉，要是我是你……”布莱克太太说个不停，每一句“要是我是你”都说得格外着力。

埃玛·卢变得无精打采，心里犯抵触。她不喜欢这个矮墩墩的小女人现在这副样子——一副公事公办的腔调，还要指点人。好容易离开埃迪餐馆，她松了口气；听罢叮嘱别睡过头——“可明儿一早要到我办公室来，亲爱的，我瞧瞧能给你想点什么法子。总会有些什么的”——脱身出来，她更是求之不得。

只剩下自己一个人，埃玛·卢沿着第七大道西侧慢慢往南走到134街，又过到东侧，折向北走。她不知道该做什么。再跑一家职业介绍所是来不及了，再说，手头的钱也不够再付一回介绍费。差事就留到明天吧，明天她先回布莱克太太那儿，讨回介绍费，再另找一家介绍所——可能的话，找家气派些的。135街这几家，她算领教够了。

===Original===
She didn’t want to go home, either. Her room had no outside vista. If she sat in the solitary chair by the solitary window, all she could see were other windows and brick walls and people either mysteriously or brazenly moving about in the apartments across the court. There was no privacy there, little fresh air, and no natural light after the sun began its downward course. Then the apartment always smelled of frying fish or of boiling cabbage. Her landlady seemed to alternate daily between these two foods. Fish smells and cabbage smells pervaded the long, dark hallway, swirled into the room when the door was opened and perfumed one’s clothes disagreeably. Moreover, urinal and fecal smells surged upward from the garbage-littered bottom of the court which her window faced.

If she went home, the landlady would eye her suspiciously and ask, “Ain’t you got a job yet?” then move away, shaking her head and dipping into her snuff box. Occasionally, in moments of excitement, she spat on the floor. And the little fat man who had the room next to Emma Lou’s could be heard coughing suggestively—tapping on the wall, and talking to himself in terms of her. He had seen her slip John in last night. He might be more bold now. He might even try—oh no he wouldn’t.

She was crossing 137th Street. She remembered this corner. John had told her that he could always be found there after work any spring or summer evening.

Emma Lou had met John on her first day in New York. He was employed as a porter in the theatre where Mazelle Lindsay was scheduled to perform, and, seeing a new maid on the premises, had decided to “make” her. He had. Emma Lou had not liked him particularly, but he had seemed New Yorkish and genial. It was John who had found her her room. It was John who had taught her how to find her way up and down town on the subway and on the elevated. He had also conducted her on a Cook’s tour of Harlem, had strolled up and down Seventh Avenue with her evenings after they had come uptown from the theater. He had pointed out for her the Y.W.C.A. with its imposing annex, the Emma Ranson House, and suggested that she get a room there later on. He had taken her on a Sunday to several of the Harlem motion picture and vaudeville theaters, and he had been as painstaking in pointing out the churches as he had been lax in pointing out the cabarets. Moreover, as they strolled Seventh Avenue, he had attempted to give her all the “inside dope” on Harlem, had told her of the “rent parties,” of the “numbers,” of “hot” men, of “sweetbacks,” and other local phenomena.

===Chinese===
她也不想回家。她那间屋子不见外面风景。要是坐到那把孤零零的椅子上、守着那扇孤零零的窗，能望见的无非是别家的窗子、砖墙，还有天井对面公寓里或鬼鬼祟祟、或大大咧咧走动的人影。那儿没有清静，缺少新鲜空气，太阳一偏西就再见不着天光。还有，屋子永远一股煎鱼或煮白菜的味儿。房东太太这两样吃食似乎一天一样轮着来。鱼味和白菜味灌满又长又黑的门厅，一开门便打着旋儿卷进屋，把人的衣服熏得气味难闻。更有甚者，她窗子朝着的那个天井，井底垃圾狼藉，尿臊粪臭一阵阵往上涌。

要是回去，房东太太会狐疑地把她上下打量，问一句“还没找着活儿哪？”然后摇着头走开，伸手到鼻烟盒里抠一撮。兴头上来了，她偶尔还往地板上啐一口。还有隔壁那个小个子胖男人，埃玛·卢听得见他意味深长的咳嗽——敲敲墙，用指涉她的字眼自言自语。昨夜他看见她把约翰偷偷领进来了。这会儿他胆子也许更大了。他说不定还会壮着胆子——不，谅他也不敢。

她正穿过137街。这个街角她记得。约翰说过，无论哪个春夏傍晚，下了工总能在那儿找到他。

埃玛·卢到纽约头一天就认识了约翰。玛泽尔·林赛订了要登台的那家剧院雇他做勤杂工，他见地方上来了个新面孔的女佣，便打定主意把她“拿下”。他也真拿下了。埃玛·卢谈不上多喜欢他，可他看着像个纽约通，人和气。是约翰替她找到了这间房。是约翰教会她乘地铁、坐高架，在城里城外来去。他还领她作了一次哈莱姆的库克式全线游览；每晚从剧院回到住宅区后，又陪她在第七大道上来回溜达。他指给她看气派非凡、带着新楼的基督教女青年会（Y.W.C.A.）和埃玛·兰塞姆之家（Emma Ranson House），建议她往后搬到那儿去住。一个星期天，他带她跑了哈莱姆好几家电影院和杂耍戏院；他指给她看教堂时那份不厌其烦，跟指认夜总会时的敷衍了事恰成对照。而且，两人在第七大道溜达时，他还一味想给她兜售哈莱姆的全部“内幕消息”，给她讲“交租会”，讲“数字”彩票（numbers），讲倒卖赃物的“热货贩子”（“hot” men），讲吃软饭的“小白脸”（sweetback），还有其他种种本地奇观。

===Original===
Emma Lou was now passing a barbershop near 140th Street. A group of men were standing there beneath a huge white and black sign announcing, “Bobbing’s, fifty cents; haircuts, twenty-five cents.” They were whistling at three schoolgirls, about fourteen or fifteen years of age, who were passing, doing much switching and giggling. Emma Lou curled her lips. Harlem streets presented many such scenes. She looked at the men significantly, forgetting for the moment that it was none of her business what they or the girls did. But they didn’t notice her. They were too busy having fun with those fresh little chippies.

Emma Lou experienced a feeling of resentment, then, realizing how ridiculous it all was, smiled it away and began to think of John once more. She wondered why she had submitted herself to him. Was it cold-blooded payment for his kind chaperoning? Something like that. John wasn’t her type. He was too pudgy and dark, too obviously an ex-cotton-picker from Georgia. He was unlettered and she couldn’t stand for that, for she liked intelligent-looking, slender, light-brown-skinned men, like, well … like the one who was just passing. She admired him boldly. He looked at her, then over her, and passed on.

Seventh Avenue was becoming more crowded now. Schoolchildren were out for their lunch hour, corner loafers and pool-hall loiterers were beginning to collect on their chosen spots. Knots of people, of no particular designation, also stood around talking, or just looking, and there were many pedestrians, either impressing one as being in a great hurry, or else seeming to have no place at all to go. Emma Lou was in this latter class. By now she had reached 142nd Street and had decided to cross over to the opposite side and walk south once more. Seventh Avenue was a wide, well-paved, busy thoroughfare, with a long, narrow, iron fenced-in parkway dividing the east side from the west. Emma Lou liked Seventh Avenue. It was so active and alive, so different from Central Avenue, the dingy main street of the black belt of Los Angeles. At night it was glorious! Where else could one see so many different types of Negroes? Where else would one view such a heterogeneous ensemble of mellow colors, glorified by the night?

===Chinese===
这时埃玛·卢正走过140街附近一家理发店。一群男人站在那儿，头顶一幅巨大的黑白招牌，上书：“剪短发五角；理发两角五。”他们正朝三个女学生吹口哨——姑娘们约莫十四五岁，一路扭着腰肢咯咯直笑。埃玛·卢撇了撇嘴。哈莱姆的街头，这路光景多的是。她意味深长地瞪了那伙男人一眼，一时忘了他们也好、姑娘们也好，干什么都与她无干。可他们没留意她。他们正忙着跟那几只鲜嫩的小雏儿调笑，哪顾得上别的。

埃玛·卢心里泛起一阵愤懑，随即又觉出这一切有多可笑，一笑置之，重新想起约翰来。她纳闷自己怎么会委身于他。是冷冰冰地偿付他那一番好心的护花之情？大概是吧。约翰不是她中意的类型。他太矮胖、太黑，一看就是乔治亚州摘棉花出身的。他大字不识，这个她可受不了，因为她喜欢的是那种看着聪明伶俐、瘦长条、浅棕色皮肤的男人，就像，唔……就像正打这儿走过的这一位。她放胆欣赏他。他看了她一眼，目光又越过她，走远了。

第七大道这会儿越来越拥挤。学生们放午饭钟出来了，街角的闲汉和弹子房里的游手好闲之辈也开始在他们各自相中的地界聚拢。一团一团的人，说不上是什么名目，也站在四下里聊天，或者只是张望；行人很多，有的给人风风火火赶路的印象，有的则仿佛根本无处可去。埃玛·卢属于后一类。这时她已走到142街，决意过到对面，再往南走。第七大道是一条宽阔平整、熙来攘往的大道，一道狭长的铁栏围起的绿带把东侧和西侧分开。埃玛·卢喜欢第七大道。它这样热闹、这样有生气，同洛杉矶黑人区那条灰扑扑的主街中央大道全然两样。到了夜里，它更是气象万千！还有哪儿能见到这么多形形色色的黑人？还有哪儿能一眼望见如此驳杂的一族柔和肤色，被夜色烘托得熠熠生辉？

===Original===
People passing by. Children playing. Dogs on leashes. Stray cats crouching by the sides of buildings. Men standing in groups or alone. Black men. Yellow men. Brown men. Emma Lou eyed them. They eyed her. There were a few remarks passed. She thought she got their import even though she could not hear what they were saying. She quickened her step and held her head higher. Be yourself, Emma Lou. Do you want to start picking men up off of the street?

The heat became more intense. Brisk walking made her perspire. Her underclothes grew sticky. Harlem heat was so muggy. She could feel the shine on her nose and it made her self-conscious. She remembered how the “Grace” in the office of Angus and Brown had so carefully powdered her skin before confronting her employer, and, as she remembered this, she looked up, and sure enough, here she was in front of the building she had sought so eagerly earlier that morning. Emma Lou drew closer to the building. She must get that shine off of her nose. It was bad enough to be black, too black, without having a shiny face to boot. She stopped in front of the tailor shop directly beneath the office of Angus and Brown, and, turning her back to the street, proceeded to powder her shiny member. Three noisy lads passed by. They saw Emma Lou and her reflection in the sunlit show window. The one closest to her cleared his throat and crooned out, loud enough for her to hear, “There’s a girl for you, ‘Fats.’ ” “Fats” was the one in the middle. He had a rotund form and a coffee-colored face. He was in his shirt sleeves and carried his coat on his arm. Bell bottom trousers hid all save the tips of his shiny tan shoes. “Fats” was looking at Emma Lou, too, but as he passed, he turned his eyes from her and broadcast a withering look at the lad who had spoken:

“Man, you know I don’t haul no coal.” There was loud laughter and the trio merrily clicked their metal-cornered heels on the sunbaked pavement as they moved away.

===Chinese===
行人来往。孩子玩耍。狗拴着绳。野猫伏在楼墙根。男人成群或独自站着。黑的男人。黄肤的男人。棕肤的男人。埃玛·卢拿眼瞧他们。他们也拿眼瞧她。有几句闲话飘过来。她虽听不清说的什么，自觉听懂了话里的意思。她加快脚步，把头扬得更高。放自然些，埃玛·卢。你难道想在大街上捡起男人来？

暑气更盛了。疾走使她出汗。内衣黏在身上。哈莱姆的热是这样闷湿。她觉出鼻尖上的油光，这让她浑身不自在。她想起安格斯和布朗事务所里那位“格雷斯”见雇主之前，把皮肤扑粉扑得多么仔细；正想着，一抬头，可不，她已经站在那天早上她急急寻来的那幢楼前。埃玛·卢朝楼走近了些。鼻尖的油光非去掉不可。人已经够黑、太黑了，再添上一张油光光的脸，那还了得。她在安格斯和布朗事务所正下方的裁缝铺前停住，背朝大街，动手给那个发亮的部位扑起粉来。三个吵吵嚷嚷的小子打身旁经过。他们看见了埃玛·卢，也看见了她映在洒满阳光的橱窗里的影子。离她最近的那位清了清嗓子，拖长调子唱出来，嗓门大得故意让她听见：“瞧，‘肥仔’，给你送妞儿来了。”“肥仔”是中间那个。他身圆脸也圆，一张咖啡色的脸。他只穿着衬衫，外衣搭在臂弯。喇叭裤长及脚面，只露出锃亮的黄皮鞋鞋尖。“肥仔”也在瞧埃玛·卢，可擦身而过时，他把目光从她身上挪开，朝开口那位播送过去一记鄙夷的眼色：

“老兄，你又不是不知道，我从来不拉煤。”哄笑声起，三人用鞋跟的金属护角在那晒得滚烫的人行道上敲出欢快的嗒嗒声，扬长而去。

## Part III / 第三部

Alva / 阿尔瓦

===Original===
It was nine o’clock. The alarm rang. Alva’s roommate awoke cursing.

“Why the hell don’t you turn off that alarm?”

There was no response. The alarm continued to ring.
