“Alva!” Braxton yelled into his sleeping roommate’s ear, “Turn off that clock. Wake up,” he began shaking him, “Wake up, damn you … ya dead?”

Alva slowly emerged from his stupor. Almost mechanically he reached for the clock, dancing merrily on a chair close to the bed, and, finding it, pushed the guilty lever back into the silent zone. Braxton watched him disgustedly:

“Watcha gettin’ up so early for? Don’tcha know this is Monday?”

“Shure, I know it’s Monday, but I gotta go to Uncle’s. The landlord’ll be here before eleven o’clock.”

“Watcha gonna pawn?”

“My brown suit. I won’t need it ’til next Sunday. You got your rent?”

“I got four dollars,” Braxton advanced slowly.

“Cantcha get the other two?”

Braxton grew apologetic and explanatory, “Not today… ya… see.…”

“Aw, man, you make me sick.”

===Chinese===
九点了。闹钟响了。阿尔瓦的同屋骂骂咧咧地醒来。

“他妈的，你干吗不把闹钟关了？”

没人应声。闹钟只管响。

“阿尔瓦！”布拉克斯顿冲着睡熟的室友的耳朵嚷，“把钟关了。醒醒。”他动手摇起他来，“醒醒啊，该死的……你死了吗？”

阿尔瓦慢吞吞地从迷糊里浮上来，几乎是不由自主地伸手去够那只闹钟——它正在床边一把椅子上欢蹦乱跳——摸着了，便把那根闯了祸的拨杆推回静默的地界。布拉克斯顿嫌恶地看着他：

“起这么早干吗？不知道今儿个是星期一？”

“咋不知道是星期一，可我得上当铺大叔那儿走一趟。房东十一点前就来。”

“你要当点啥？”

“我那套棕西装。下星期天以前反正用不着。你那份房租凑齐了？”

“凑了四块。”布拉克斯顿慢吞吞地说。

“差的那两块你就凑不出了？”

布拉克斯顿赔起不是，解释起来：“今儿个不成……你……瞧。……”

“嗐，老兄，你真叫我恶心。”

===Original===
Disgust overcoming his languor, Alva got out of the bed. This was getting to be a regular Monday morning occurrence. Braxton was always one, two or three dollars short of having his required half of the rent, and Alva, who had rented the room, always had to make it up. Luckily for Alva, both he and the landlord were Elks. Fraternal brothers must stick together. Thus it was an easy matter to pay the rent in installments. The only difficulty being that it was happening rather frequently. There is liable to be a limit even to a brother Elk’s patience, especially where money is concerned.

Alva put on his dressing gown, and his house shoes, then went into the little alcove which was curtained off in the rear from the rest of the room. Jumbled together on the marble topped stationary washstand were a half dozen empty gin bottles bearing a pre-prohibition Gordon label, a similar number of empty ginger ale bottles, a cocktail shaker, and a medley of assorted cocktail, water, jelly and whiskey glasses, filled and surrounded by squeezed orange and lemon rinds. The little two-burner gas plate atop a wooden dry goods box was covered with dirty dishes, frying pan, eggshells, bacon rinds, and a dominating though lopsided tea kettle. Even Alva’s trunk, which occupied half the entrance space between the alcove and the room, littered as it was with paper bags, cracker boxes and greasy paper plates, bore evidence of the orgy which the occupants of the room staged over every weekend.

===Chinese===
厌恶压倒了懒怠，阿尔瓦下了床。这渐渐成了每星期一早晨的固定戏码。布拉克斯顿该摊的那半份房租，总要短上一块、两块、三块，而租房的阿尔瓦总得替他补齐。阿尔瓦运气好，他和房东都是麋鹿会（Elks）的会友。兄弟会的兄弟理当互相拉扯。这样一来，房租分期付倒也不难。唯一难办的是，这种事未免来得太勤。哪怕是麋鹿会兄弟的耐性，也保不齐有个限度，尤其牵扯到钱的时候。

阿尔瓦披上晨衣，趿上便鞋，走进屋子后头那个用帘子隔出来的小凹室。大理石面的固定洗脸台上乱堆一气：半打空杜松子酒瓶，贴着禁酒令以前的戈登牌标签；数目相仿的空姜汁汽水瓶；一只鸡尾酒调酒壶；还有鸡尾酒杯、水杯、果冻杯、威士忌杯的大杂拌，杯里杯外满是挤瘪的橙皮柠檬皮。木货箱顶上那架双眼煤气灶，盖满了脏盘子、煎锅、蛋壳、咸肉皮，外加一把虽歪却占尽风头的茶壶。连阿尔瓦那只箱子——它占了凹室与正屋之间半个门口，纸袋、饼干盒、油渍麻花的纸盘子撒了一箱——也出来作证：这屋的住户，每个周末都要上演一场狂欢。

===Original===
Alva surveyed this rather intimate and familiar disorder, faltered a moment, started to call Braxton, then remembering previous Monday mornings set about his task alone. It was Braxton’s custom never to arise before noon. Alva who worked as a presser in a costume house was forced to get up at seven o’clock on every week day save Monday when he was not required to report for work until twelve o’clock. His employers thus managed to accumulate several baskets of clothes from the sewing room before their pressers arrived. It was better to have them remain at home until this was done. Then you didn’t have to pay them so much, and having let the sewing room get head start, there was never any chance for the pressing room to slow down.

Alva’s mother had been an American mulatto, his father a Filipino. Alva himself was small in stature as his father had been, small and well developed with broad shoulders, narrow hips and firm well modeled limbs. His face was oval shaped and his features more oriental than Negroid, His skin was neither yellow nor brown but something in between, something warm, arresting and mellow with the faintest suggestion of a parchment tinge beneath, lending it individuality. His eyes were small, deep and slanting. His forehead high, hair sparse and finely textured.

The alcove finally straightened up, Alva dressed rather hurriedly, and, taking a brown suit from the closet, made his regular Monday morning trip to the pawn shop.

---

===Chinese===
阿尔瓦打量着这片熟稔而亲昵的凌乱，迟疑了片刻，想喊布拉克斯顿，想起先前那些星期一早晨，便独自干了起来。布拉克斯顿的规矩，是不到晌午绝不起床。阿尔瓦在一家戏装铺当熨衣工，每个工作日都得七点起床，唯独星期一例外——那天不到十二点不必到工。雇主们就这样赶在熨衣工到来之前，从缝纫间攒下好几筐衣裳。让他们待在家里、等活儿攒齐了再来，更划算——工钱可以少付些，缝纫间又抢了先，熨烫间便永远没机会歇气。

阿尔瓦的母亲是个美国黑白混血，父亲是菲律宾人。阿尔瓦自己像父亲一样个子矮小，矮小而匀称：肩宽，胯窄，四肢结实有型。脸是鹅蛋脸，眉眼与其说像黑种，不如说更像东方人。皮肤既非黄又非棕，是介乎两者之间的什么东西，温润、惹眼、醇和，底下又透着极淡的一抹羊皮纸色，便有了自己的独特。眼睛小而深，微微带斜。额头高，头发稀，发质细。

凹室终于收拾利落，阿尔瓦匆匆穿好衣裳，从壁橱里取出那套棕西装，踏上了每星期一早晨雷打不动的当铺之行。

---

===Original===
Emma Lou finished rinsing out some silk stockings and sat down in a chair to reread a letter she had received from home that morning. It was about the third time she had gone over it. Her mother wanted her to come home. Evidently the hometown gossips were busy. No doubt they were saying, “Strange mother to let that gal stay in New York alone. She ain’t goin’ to school, either. Wonder what she’s doin’?” Emma Lou read all this between the lines of what her mother had written. Jane Morgan was being tearful as usual. She loved to suffer, and being tearful seemed the easiest way to let the world know that one was suffering. Sob stuff, thought Emma Lou, and, tearing the letter up, threw it into the wastepaper basket.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢漂完几双丝袜，坐到椅子上，把那天早晨家里来的信又读了一遍。这约莫是第三遍。母亲要她回家。显然，老家的长舌妇们正忙。她们一准在说：“这当妈的也怪，由着那丫头一个人待在纽约。学也不上了。不知道她在干些什么？”这些话，埃玛·卢从母亲信里字里行间全读了出来。简·摩根照例眼泪汪汪。她爱受苦，而眼泪汪汪似乎是最省事的法子，好教世人知道自己在受苦。苦情戏罢了，埃玛·卢想着，把信撕了，扔进字纸篓。

===Original===
Emma Lou was now maid to Arline Strange, who was playing for the moment the part of a mulatto Carmen in an alleged melodrama of Negro life in Harlem. Having tried, for two weeks to locate what she termed “congenial work,” Emma Lou had given up the idea and meekly returned to Mazelle Lindsay. She had found her old job satisfactorily filled, but Mazelle had been sympathetic and had arranged to place her with Arline Strange. Now her mother wanted her to come home. Let her want. She was of age, and supporting herself. Moreover, she felt that if it had not been for gossip her mother would never have thought of asking her to come home.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢如今给阿琳·斯特兰奇当女佣。阿琳眼下正在一部号称描写哈莱姆黑人生活的情节剧里，饰演一位黑白混血的卡门。找了两个星期她所谓“合意的差事”而不得，埃玛·卢死了这条心，乖乖回到玛泽尔·林赛那里。旧差事已有人干得好好的，好在玛泽尔心善，安排她到阿琳·斯特兰奇手下。如今母亲又想她回家了。想就想吧。她已成年，自食其力。再说，她觉得要不是那些闲话，母亲压根不会想到叫她回家。

===Original===
“Stop your mooning, dearie.” Arline Strange had returned to her dressing room. Act one was over. The Negro Carmen had become the mistress of a wealthy European. She would now shed her gingham dress for an evening gown.

Mechanically, Emma Lou assisted Arline in making the change. She was unusually silent. It was noticed.

“’Smatter, Louie. In love or something?”

Emma Lou smiled, “Only with myself.”

“Then snap out of it. Remember, you’re going cabareting with us tonight. This brother of mine from Chicago insists upon going to Harlem to check up on my performance. He’ll enjoy himself more if you act as guide. Ever been to Small’s?” Emma Lou shook her head. “I haven’t been to any of the cabarets.”

“What?” Arline was genuinely surprised. “You in Harlem and never been to a cabaret? Why I thought all colored people went.”

Emma Lou bristled. White people were so stupid. “No,” she said firmly. “All colored people don’t go. Fact is, I’ve heard that most of the places are patronized almost solely by whites.”

“Oh, yes, I knew that, I’ve been to Small’s and Barron’s and the Cotton Club, but I thought there were other places.” She stopped talking, and spent the next few moments deepening the artificial duskiness of her skin. The gingham dress was now on its hanger. The evening gown clung glamorously to her voluptuous figure. “For God’s sake, don’t let on to my brother you ain’t been to Small’s before. Act like you know all about it. I’ll see that he gives you a big tip.” The call bell rang. Arline said “Damn,” gave one last look into the mirror, then hurried back to the stage so that the curtain could go up on the cabaret scene in Act Two.

===Chinese===
“别再愁眉苦脸啦，亲爱的。”阿琳·斯特兰奇回到了化妆间。第一幕完了。黑人卡门已当上一位阔绰欧洲人的情妇，这就要脱下格子布裙，换上晚礼服。

埃玛·卢机械地帮阿琳换装。她难得地沉默。人家注意到了。

“怎么啦，露易，害相思还是怎么的？”

埃玛·卢一笑：“只跟我自己害。”

“那就振作点。记着，今晚你跟我们一块去逛夜总会。我这位芝加哥来的哥哥非上哈莱姆不可，要亲自验验我演得如何。你当向导，他玩得更尽兴。去过斯莫尔乐园吗？”埃玛·卢摇头。“哪家夜总会我都没去过。”

“什么？”阿琳是真惊讶。“你在哈莱姆，居然没进过夜总会？我还当有色人种个个都去呢。”

埃玛·卢火了。白人真是蠢得可以。“不，”她说得斩钉截铁，“有色人种不是个个都去。实话说，我听说那些地方的主顾几乎清一色是白人。”

“哦，这我知道，斯莫尔乐园、巴伦夜总会、棉花俱乐部我都去过，我还当另有别处呢。”她住了口，接下来好一阵子，都在给自己的皮肤加深人工的暗色。格子布裙已挂上衣架。晚礼服光彩照人地裹着她丰满的身段。“千万看在上帝分上，别让我哥哥知道你没去过斯莫尔乐园。装作门儿清的样子。我会让他多给你小费的。”催场铃响了。阿琳骂了句“该死”，对着镜子最后照了一眼，匆匆回台上去了——第二幕的夜总会场面就等着她开演。

===Original===
Emma Lou laid out the negligee outfit Arline would be killed in at the end of Act Three, and went downstairs to stand in the stage wings, a makeup box beneath her arm. She never tired of watching the so-called dramatic antics on the stage. She wondered if there were any Negroes of the type portrayed by Arline and her fellow performers. Perhaps there were, since there were any number of minor parts being played by real Negroes who acted much different from any Negroes she had ever known or seen. It all seemed to her like a mad caricature.

She watched for about the thirtieth time Arline acting the part of a Negro cabaret entertainer, and also for about the thirtieth time, came to the conclusion that Arline was being herself rather than the character she was supposed to be playing. From where she was standing in the wings she could see a small portion of the audience, and she watched their reaction. Their interest seemed genuine. Arline did have pep and personality, and the alleged Negro background was strident and kaleidoscopic, all of which no doubt made up for the inane plot and vulgar dialogue.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢摆出第三幕结尾阿琳送命时要穿的那套晨衣行头，便下楼到侧台站着，腋下夹着化妆箱。台上那些所谓的戏剧表演，她百看不厌。她纳闷，世上到底有没有阿琳和同台演员扮演的那种黑人。也许有，因为不少小角色由货真价实的黑人出演，而他们的做派，同她认识或见过的任何黑人都大不一样。在她看来，这一切活像一幅疯癫的漫画。

阿琳扮演黑人夜总会歌舞艺人，这她看了约莫第三十遍；也约莫第三十回得出同一个结论：阿琳演的是她自己，而不是她该演的那个角色。从侧台她站的位置望得见观众席的一角，她观察着他们的反应。他们的兴致像是真的。阿琳确实有活力有个性，那号称黑人的背景又喧腾花哨如万花筒，这一切无疑都补足了情节的白痴和台词的恶俗。

===Original===
They entered Small’s Paradise, Emma Lou, Arline and Arline’s brother from Chicago. All the way uptown he had plied Emma Lou with questions concerning New York’s Black Belt. He had reciprocated by relating how well he knew the Negro section of Chicago. Quite a personage around the Black and Tan cabarets there, it seemed. “But I never,” he concluded as the taxi drew up to the curb in front of Small’s, “have seen any black gal in Chicago act like Arline acts. She claims she is presenting a Harlem specie. So I am going to see for myself.” And he chuckled all the time he was helping them out of the taxi and paying the fare. While they were checking their wraps in the foyer, the orchestra began playing. Through the open entranceway Emma Lou could see a hazy, dim-lighted room, walls and ceiling colorfully decorated, floor space jammed with tables and chairs and people. A heavy set mulatto in tuxedo, after asking how many were in their party, led them through a lane of tables around the squared off dance platform to a ringside seat on the far side of the cabaret.

Immediately they were seated, a waiter came to take their order.

“Three bottles of White Rock.” The waiter nodded, twirled his tray on the tip of his fingers and skated away.

Emma Lou watched the dancers, and noticed immediately that in all that insensate crowd of dancing couples there were only a few Negroes.

===Chinese===
三人走进斯莫尔乐园——埃玛·卢、阿琳，还有阿琳那位芝加哥来的哥哥。一路往住宅区去，他连珠炮似的问埃玛·卢纽约黑人区的种种。作为回礼，他也讲起自己对芝加哥黑人区何等熟门熟路，看那样子，在城里那些黑白混场的夜总会里，他还是个人物。“不过，”出租汽车在斯莫尔乐园门前靠上路缘时，他总结道，“在芝加哥，我还从没见过哪个黑姑娘像阿琳演的这副做派。她号称演的是哈莱姆特产。我可得亲自来开开眼。”下车搀她们、付车钱的工夫，他一直咯咯地笑。三人在门厅里寄存外衣的当口，乐队奏起了乐。透过敞开的门道，埃玛·卢望得见一间光线昏沉的屋子，墙壁天花板装点得五彩缤纷，地面上桌椅和人挤作一团。一个穿无尾礼服、身板粗壮的黑白混血问过他们几位，领他们穿过桌间的夹道，绕着围出来的方形舞池，到夜总会那一头的池边座位落座。

刚坐定，侍者便过来听点。

“三瓶怀特罗克矿泉水。”侍者点点头，托盘在指尖上转了个圈，滑冰似的溜走了。

埃玛·卢望着舞客，一眼就看出来，那整片疯魔起舞的人群里，黑人只有寥寥几个。

===Original===
“My God, such music. Let’s dance, Arline,” and off they went, leaving Emma Lou sitting alone. Somehow or other she felt frightened. Most of the tables around her were deserted, their tops littered with liquid-filled glasses, and bottles of ginger ale and White Rock. There was no liquor in sight, yet Emma Lou was aware of pungent alcoholic odors. Then she noticed a heavy-jowled white man with a flashlight walking among the empty tables and looking beneath them. He didn’t seem to be finding anything. The music soon stopped. Arline and her brother returned to the table. He was feigning anxiety because he had not seen the type of character Arline claimed to be portraying, and loudly declared that he was disappointed.

“Why there ain’t nothing here but white people. Is it always like this?”

Emma Lou said it was and turned to watch their waiter, who with two others had come dancing across the floor, holding aloft his tray, filled with bottles and glasses. Deftly, he maneuvered away from the other two and slid to their table, put down a bottle of White Rock and an ice-filled glass before each one, then, after flicking a stub check on to the table, rejoined his companions in a return trip across the dance floor.

===Chinese===
“我的天，这音乐。跳一个去，阿琳。”两人起身去了，撇下埃玛·卢独坐。不知怎么，她觉得发怵。四周的桌子多半空着，桌面上杯盏狼藉，斟得满满的杯子，还有姜汁汽水和怀特罗克的瓶子。明面上不见酒，埃玛·卢却闻得到刺鼻的酒气。接着她注意到一个下颌厚重的白人，拿着手电筒在空桌间转悠，往桌子底下看。他像是什么也没找着。音乐很快停了。阿琳和她哥哥回到桌边。他没见着阿琳声称要扮演的那类人物，装出一副焦虑的样子，大声宣布自己失望了。

“怎么，这儿除了白人什么都没有啊。回回都这样？”

埃玛·卢说是回回这样，随即转头去看他们的侍者——他正同另外两个侍者手托满载瓶杯的托盘，举过头顶，舞着穿过舞池。他灵巧地摆脱另外两人，滑到桌前，给每人面前摆上一瓶怀特罗克、一只盛满冰块的杯子，又把一张账单条甩在桌上，便赶上同伴，再舞着横穿舞池回去。

===Original===
Arline’s brother produced a hip flask, and before Emma Lou could demur mixed her a highball. She didn’t want to drink. She hadn’t drunk before, but. …

“Here come the entertainers!” Emma Lou followed Arline’s turn of the head to see two women, one light brown skin and slim, the other chocolate colored and fat, walking to the center of the dance floor.

The orchestra played the introduction and vamp to “Muddy Waters.” The two entertainers swung their legs and arms in rhythmic unison, smiling broadly and rolling their eyes, first to the left and then to the right. Then they began to sing. Their voices were husky and strident, neither alto nor soprano. They muddled their words and seemed to inpregnate the syncopated melody with physical content.

As they sang the chorus, they glided out among the tables, stopping at one, then at another, and another, singing all the time, their bodies undulating and provocative, occasionally giving just a promise of an obscene hip movement, while their arms waved and their fingers held tight to the dollar bills and silver coins placed in their palms by enthusiastic onlookers.

===Chinese===
阿琳的哥哥掏出随身扁壶，不等埃玛·卢推辞，就给她兑了一杯海波酒。她不想喝。她从前没喝过酒，可是……

“歌舞艺人来了！”埃玛·卢顺着阿琳转头的方向望去，只见两个女人朝舞池中央走去，一个浅棕色皮肤、身条细瘦，一个巧克力色、胖墩墩。

乐队奏起《浑水》（Muddy Waters）的前奏和过门。两位歌舞艺人腿臂齐挥，节奏划一，咧着嘴笑，眼珠先向左滚、再向右滚。随后唱了起来。嗓子沙哑尖利，说女低音不是女低音，说女高音不是女高音。吐字含混，却仿佛把一股肉感灌进了那切分的旋律。

唱到副歌，两人滑进桌丛，在这桌停停，那桌停停，又一张桌，嘴里唱个不停，身子波浪般起伏撩拨，偶尔只稍稍露一点下流摆胯的苗头，两条胳膊挥着，手指头紧紧攥住热心看客塞进她们掌心的纸币和银币。

===Original===
Emma Lou, all of her, watched and listened. As they approached her table, she sat as one mesmerized. Something in her seemed to be trying to give way. Her insides were stirred, and tingled. The two entertainers circled their table; Arline’s brother held out a dollar bill. The fat, chocolate colored girl leaned over the table, her hand touched his, she exercised the muscles of her stomach, muttered a guttural “thank you” in between notes and moved away, moaning “Muddy Waters,” rolling her eyes, shaking her hips.

Emma Lou had turned completely around in her chair, watching the progress of that wah-wahing, jello-like chocolate hulk, and her slim light brown skin companion. Finally they completed their rounds of the tables and returned to the dance floor. Red and blue spotlights played upon their dissimilar figures, the orchestra increased the tempo and lessened the intensity of its playing. The swaying entertainers pulled up their dresses, exposing lace trimmed step-ins and an island of flesh. Their stockings were rolled down below their knees, their step-ins discreetly short and delicate. Finally, they ceased their swaying and began to dance. They shimmied and whirled, charlestoned and black-bottomed. Their terpsichorean ensemble was melodramatic and absurd. Their execution easy and emphatic. Emma Lou forgot herself. She gaped, giggled and applauded like the rest of the audience, and only as they let their legs separate, preparatory to doing one final split to the floor, did Emma Lou come to herself long enough to wonder if the fat one could achieve it without seriously endangering those ever tightening step-ins.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢整个人都又看又听。她们挨近她这桌时，她像被勾了魂似的坐着。她体内有什么东西仿佛正要决口。五脏六腑被搅动了，酥酥麻麻。两位歌舞艺人绕着桌子转；阿琳的哥哥递出一美元。那胖胖的巧克力色姑娘俯身过桌，手碰着他的手，运了运肚腹的肌肉，在唱腔的间隙含混地咕噜一声“谢谢”，便一边呻吟着《浑水》，翻着眼珠，摆着胯，一边挪开了。

埃玛·卢在椅子里整个人转了过去，目送那团哇呜哼唱、果冻似的巧克力色庞大身影，和她那细瘦浅棕皮肤的同伙。她们终于绕完桌丛，回到舞池。红蓝聚光灯照着两人一胖一瘦的身形，乐队加了速度，收了劲头。摇摆着的歌舞艺人撩起裙子，露出镶花边的短内裤和一座肉的岛屿。长袜卷到膝下，短内裤短得矜持、薄得娇嫩。终于，她们停了摇摆，跳起舞来。抖着肩，旋着身，跳查尔斯顿，跳黑臀舞。这一套舞艺又像情节剧又荒唐。跳起来却轻松利落、干净响亮。埃玛·卢忘了形。她跟满场观众一起张嘴呆看、咯咯傻笑、鼓掌叫好，直到两人双腿一分、准备朝地板劈出最后一个叉，埃玛·卢才回过神来，来得及想一想：那个胖的劈得下去吗，会不会当真绷坏了那条越绷越紧的短内裤。

===Original===
“Dam’ good, I’ll say,” a slender white youth at the next table asseverated, as he lifted an amber filled glass to his lips.

Arline sighed. Her brother had begun to razz her. Emma Lou blinked guiltily as the lights were turned up. She had been immersed in something disturbingly pleasant. Idiot, she berated herself, just because you’ve had one drink and seen your first cabaret entertainer, must your mind and body feel all aflame?

Arline’s brother was mixing another highball. All around, people were laughing. There was much more laughter than there was talk, much more gesticulating and ogling than the usual means of expression called for. Everything seemed unrestrained, abandoned. Yet, Emma Lou was conscious of a note of artificiality, the same as she felt when she watched Arline and her fellow performers cavorting on the stage in *Cabaret Gal*. This entire scene seemed staged, they were in a theater, only the proscenium arch had been obliterated. At last the audience and the actors were as one.

A call to order on the snare drum. A brutal sliding trumpet call on the trombone, a running minor scale by the clarinet and piano, an umpah, umpah by the bass horn, a combination four measure moan and strum by the saxophone and banjo, then a melodic ensemble, and the orchestra was playing another dance tune. Masses of people jumbled up the three entrances to the dance square and with difficulty, singled out their mates and became closely allied partners. Inadvertently, Emma Lou looked at Arline’s brother. He blushed, and appeared uncomfortable. She realized immediately what was on his mind. He didn’t know whether or not to ask her to dance with him. The ethics of the case were complex. She was a Negro and hired maid. But was she a hired maid after hours, and in this environment? Emma Lou had difficulty in suppressing a smile, then she decided to end the suspense.

===Chinese===
“没得说，真棒。”邻桌一个瘦削的白人青年举起盛着琥珀色酒液的杯子凑到唇边，郑重其事地下了断语。

阿琳叹了口气。她哥哥开始拿她起哄了。灯光亮起来，埃玛·卢心虚地眨眼。她方才浸泡在一种扰人的愉快里。傻子，她骂自己，不过喝了一杯酒、头一回见了夜总会歌舞艺人，身心就非得烧起来不成？

阿琳的哥哥在兑第二杯海波酒。四下里人人都在笑。笑声比话声多得多，挤眉弄眼、指手画脚，也比寻常表达所需的多得多。一切都毫无拘束，放浪形骸。然而埃玛·卢觉察到一丝做戏的味道，同她看阿琳和同台演员在《歌舞厅女郎》（Cabaret Gal）台上蹦跶时的感觉一模一样。眼前这一整幕都像排演好的，他们就在戏院里，只是台口的拱框抹掉了。观众和演员终于合而为一。

小军鼓敲出一声号令。长号蛮横地滑出一声号角，单簧管和钢琴跑一串小调音阶，低音号嘭嚓、嘭嚓，萨克斯管和班卓琴合作一声四小节的呜咽加弹拨，接着乐声汇成一片，乐队又奏起一支舞曲。一大团一大团的人挤在舞场的三处入口，费劲地认出自己的舞伴，结成紧贴着的搭档。无意间，埃玛·卢看了阿琳的哥哥一眼。他红了脸，显得局促。她立刻明白他在打什么主意。他不知道该不该请她跳舞。这里头的规矩不好拿捏。她是个黑人，是个雇来的女佣。可下了班、在这种地方，她还算不算女佣？埃玛·卢好不容易才憋住笑，随后决定结束这场悬心。

===Original===
“Why don’t you two dance. No need of letting the music go to waste.”

Both Arline and her brother were obviously relieved, but as they got up Arline said, “Ain’t much fun cuddling up to your own brother when there’s music like this.” But off they went, leaving Emma Lou alone and disturbed. John ought to be here, slipped out before she remembered that she didn’t want John any more. Then she began to wish that John had introduced her to some more men. But he didn’t know the kind of men she was interested in knowing. He only knew men and boys like himself, porters and janitors and chauffeurs and bootblacks. Imagine her, a college trained person, even if she hadn’t finished her senior year, being satisfied with the company of such unintelligent servitors. How had she stood John so long with his constant of defense, “I ain’t got much education, but I got mother wit.” Mother wit! Creation of the unlettered, satisfying illusion to the dumb, ludicrous prop to the mentally unfit. Yes, he had mother wit all right.

===Chinese===
“你们俩怎么不跳。别白白糟蹋了音乐。”

阿琳和她哥哥明显松了口气，只是起身时阿琳说：“听着这样的音乐，搂着自己亲哥哥跳，没什么意思。”两人还是去了，撇下埃玛·卢独自心乱。约翰倒该在这儿来——念头冒出来，她才想起自己已经不要约翰了。接着她又怨起约翰来：怎么不给她多引见几个男人。可他认得的男人，不是她要结交的那一类。他认得的，净是同他一样的人，勤杂工、看门的、开车的、擦鞋的。想想吧，她一个受过大学教育的人——纵然大四没念完——竟满足于同这等没脑筋的侍役为伍。约翰那句挂在嘴边的辩解，她是怎么忍了这么久的：“我书读得不多，可我有娘胎里带来的机灵劲儿。”机灵劲儿！不识字的人的发明，蠢人自我安慰的幻象，脑子不济的人的滑稽拐棍。是啊，他的机灵劲儿可足了。

===Original===
Emma Lou looked around and noticed at a nearby table three young colored men, all in tuxedos, gazing at her and talking. She averted her glance and turned to watch the dancers. She thought she heard a burst of ribald laughter from the young men at the table. Then someone touched her on the shoulder, and she looked up into a smiling oriental-like face, neither brown nor yellow in color, but warm and pleasing beneath the soft lights, and, because of the smile, showing a gleaming row of small, even teeth, set off by a solitary gold incisor. The voice was persuasive and apologetic, “Would you care to dance with me?” The music had stopped, but there was promise of an encore. Emma Lou was confused, her mind blankly chaotic. She was expected to push back her chair and get up. She did. And, without saying a word, allowed herself to be maneuvered to the dance floor.

In a moment they were swallowed up in the jazz whirlpool. Long strides were impossible. There were too many other legs striding for free motion in that over populated area. He held her close to him; the contours of her body fitting his. The two highballs had made her giddy. She seemed to be glowing inside. The soft lights and the music suggested abandon and intrigue. They said nothing to one another. She noticed that her partner’s face seemed alive with some inner ecstasy. It must be the music, thought Emma Lou. Then she got a whiff of his liquor-laden breath.

After three encores, the clarinet shrilled out a combination of notes that seemed to say regretfully, “That’s all.” Brighter lights were switched on, and the milling couples merged into a struggling mass of individuals, laughing, talking, over-animated individuals, all trying to go in different directions, and getting a great deal of fun out of the resulting confusion. Emma Lou’s partner held tightly to her arm, and pushed her through the insensate crowd to her table. Then he muttered a polite “thank you” and turned away. Emma Lou sat down. Arline and her brother looked at her and laughed. “Got a dance, eh Louie?” Emma Lou wondered if Arline was being malicious, and for an answer she only nodded her head and smiled, hoping all the while that her smile was properly enigmatic.

Arline’s brother spoke up. “Whadda say we go. I’ve seen enough of this to know that Arline and her stage director are all wet.” Their waiter was called, the check was paid, and they were on their way out. In spite of herself, Emma Lou glanced back to the table where her dancing partner was sitting. To her confusion, she noticed that he and his two friends were staring at her. One of them said something and made a wry face. Then they all laughed, uproariously and cruelly.

---

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢四下张望，瞧见邻桌三个年轻的有色男人，一律无尾礼服，正望着她议论。她挪开目光，转头看舞客。她仿佛听见那桌年轻人爆出一阵下流的哄笑。随后有人碰了碰她的肩，她抬头，撞上一张微笑的、东方人似的脸——肤色既不棕也不黄，在柔光底下温润讨喜；因为那笑，露出一排小而齐整的亮牙，当中一颗金门牙格外打眼。那嗓音又殷勤又带着歉意：“可以请您跳个舞吗？”音乐停了，却有再来的意思。埃玛·卢心里发慌，脑子里空空的乱成一团。她该拉开椅子站起来。她站了。于是一言不发，任人引着上了舞池。

转眼他们就被爵士的漩涡吞没了。迈大步是不可能的。那块人满为患的地界，挤着太多条要自由迈动的大腿。他把她搂得紧紧的；她身体的轮廓严丝合缝地贴着他的。两杯海波酒下了肚，她晕晕乎乎。她仿佛从里头往外发光。柔光和音乐都在怂恿放浪与暧昧。两人一句话也没说。她注意到舞伴的脸似乎被某种内在的销魂点亮着。一定是音乐的作用，埃玛·卢想。接着她嗅到一口他呼出的、酒气熏天的呼吸。

三度加演过后，单簧管尖啸出一串音符，像不无遗憾地说：“完了。”更亮的灯亮起来，打转的舞侣化开成乱哄哄的一群单个的人，笑着、说着、亢奋过头的人，人人想往不同的方向去，又从这份大乱里捞着不少乐子。埃玛·卢的舞伴紧紧挽住她的胳膊，推着她穿过疯魔的人群送回桌边，含糊地道了声客气的“谢谢”，转身走了。埃玛·卢坐下。阿琳和她哥哥看着她笑。“捞着一支舞啊，露易？”埃玛·卢拿不准阿琳是不是不怀好意，作为回答，她只点点头、笑一笑，一心盼着自己这一笑笑得恰到好处的莫测高深。

阿琳的哥哥开了腔：“走吧，我看够了。就凭这些我也看明白了，阿琳和她的舞台导演纯属瞎编。”侍者叫来，账单付讫，三人往外走。埃玛·卢不由自主地回头瞟了一眼她那位舞伴坐的桌子。她慌乱地发现，他和他那两个朋友正盯着她。其中一个说了句什么，做了个鬼脸。随即三人都笑了，笑得放肆而刻毒。

---

===Original===
Alva had overslept. Braxton, who had stayed out the entire night, came in about eight o’clock, and excitedly interrupted his drunken slumber.

“Ain’t you goin’ to work?”

“Work?” Alva was alarmed. “What time is it?”

“’Bout eight. Didn’t you set the clock?”

“Sure, I did.” Alva picked up the clock from the floor and examined the alarm dial. It had been set for ten o’clock instead of for six. He sulked for a moment, then attempted to shake off the impending mood of regretfulness and disgust for self.

“Aw, hell, what’s the dif’. Call ’em up and tell ’em I’m sick. There’s a nickel somewhere in that change on the dresser.” Braxton had taken off his tuxedo coat and vest.

“If you’re not goin’ to work ever, you might as well quit. I don’t see no sense in working two days and laying off three.”

“I’m goin’ to quit the damn job anyway. I been working steady now since last fall.”

“I thought it was about time you quit.” Braxton had stripped off his white full dress shirt, put on his bathrobe, and started out of the room, to go downstairs to the telephone. Alva reached across the bed and pulled up the shade, blinked at the inpouring daylight and lay himself back down, one arm thrown across his forehead. He had slipped off into a state of semiconsciousness again when Braxton returned.

===Chinese===
阿尔瓦睡过了头。在外头浪了一整夜的布拉克斯顿，八点来钟回来，兴冲冲地把他从醉酒的酣睡里搅醒。

“你不去上工？”

“上工？”阿尔瓦一惊。“几点了？”

“八点上下。你没定闹钟？”

“当然定了。”阿尔瓦从地板上捞起闹钟，查看闹铃盘。定的不是六点，是十点。他闷了片刻，随即想法子甩掉那阵涌上来的懊悔和自厌。

“嗐，见鬼，有啥两样。打电话给他们，说我病了。梳妆台上那堆零钱里有个五分镍币。”布拉克斯顿已经脱下了无尾礼服的上衣和马甲。

“你要是压根不打算上工，干脆辞了拉倒。干两天歇三天，我看不出个所以然来。”

“这破差事我横竖要辞。打去年秋天起，我可是连着做工没停过。”

“我琢磨着，你也早该辞了。”布拉克斯顿扒下白礼服衬衫，套上晨衣，出屋下楼打电话去了。阿尔瓦隔着床探过身去，拉起卷帘，对着倾泻进来的天光直眨眼，又躺了回去，一条胳膊搭在额头上。布拉克斯顿回来时，他已又滑回半昏迷的状态。

===Original===
“The girl said she’d tell the boss. Asked who I was as usual.” He went into the alcove to finish undressing, and put on his pajamas. Alva looked up.

“You goin’ to bed?”

“Yes, don’t you think I want some sleep?”

“Thought you was goin’ to look for a job?”

“I was, but I hadn’t figured on staying out all night.”

“Always some damn excuse. Where’d you go?”

“Down to Flo’s.”

“Who in the hell is Flo?”

“That little yaller broad I picked up at the cabaret last night.”

“I thought she had a nigger with her.”

“She did, but I jived her along, so she ditched him, and gave me her address. I met her there later.”

Braxton was now ready to get into the bed. All this time he had been preparing himself in his usual bedtime manner. His face had been cold-creamed, his hair greased and tightly covered by a silken stocking cap. This done, he climbed over Alva and lay on top of the covers. They were silent for a moment, then Braxton laughed softly to himself.

“Where’d *you* go last night?”

===Chinese===
“姑娘说会转告老板。照例问了我一句是谁。”他进凹室脱完衣服，换上睡衣。阿尔瓦抬起头。

“你要睡了？”

“不然呢，你以为我不想睡？”

“还当你去找差事呢。”

“本来要去，没算到会整夜在外头。”

“总有你妈的借口。你上哪儿去了？”

“去弗洛那儿了。”

“弗洛他妈的是谁？”

“昨儿夜里在夜总会搭上的那个浅黄肤色的小浪妞。”

“我记得她身边不是跟着个黑鬼吗。”

“是跟着来着，可我跟她耍了一路花腔，她就把那黑鬼甩了，把地址给了我。后来我就上她那儿去了。”

这时布拉克斯顿收拾停当，可以上床了。这半天工夫，他一直照他惯常的睡前程序拾掇自己：脸上搽了冷霜，头发抹了油，紧紧罩上丝质睡帽。收拾完，他从阿尔瓦身上翻过去，躺在被子上。两人静了一会儿，随后布拉克斯顿自个儿低声笑了。

“*你*昨儿夜里上哪儿去了？”

===Original===
“Where’d I go?” Alva seemed surprised. “Why I came home, where’d ya think I went?” Braxton laughed again.

“Oh, I thought maybe you’d really made a date with that coal scuttle blond you danced with.”

“Ya musta thought it.”

“Well, ya seemed pretty sweet on her.”

“Whaddaya mean, sweet? Just because I danced with her once. I took pity on her, cause she looked so lonesome with those ofays. Wonder who they was?”

“Oh, she probably works for them. It’s good you danced with her. Nobody else would.”

“I didn’t see nothing wrong with her. She might have been a little dark.”

“Little dark is right, and you know when they comes blacker’n me, they ain’t got no go.” Braxton was a reddish brown aristocrat, with clear-cut features and curly hair. His paternal grandfather had been an Iroquois Indian.

---

===Chinese===
“我上哪儿去了？”阿尔瓦像是吃了一惊。“我不回家了嘛，你当我上哪儿去了？”布拉克斯顿又笑。

“哦，我还当你真跟那个‘煤斗金发妞’（coal scuttle blond，讽指黑得像煤的女子）约上了呢，就是跟你跳舞的那个。”

“你可想得美。”

“嗐，你瞧着挺迷她。”

“迷她？这话怎么讲。不就跟她跳了一回舞。我可怜她罢了，她夹在那几个白鬼（ofay）中间，看着怪孤单的。也不知道他们是干吗的？”

“哦，八成是她的东家。你跟她跳挺好。旁人可没人肯。”

“我没瞧出她有什么毛病。兴许是黑了点儿。”

“岂止一点儿，你也知道的，凡比我黑的，就没戏唱了。”布拉克斯顿是位红棕色的贵族，眉眼清秀，头发卷曲。他祖父是易洛魁印第安人。

---

===Original===
Emma Lou was very lonesome. She still knew no one save John, two or three of the Negro actors who worked on the stage with Arline, and a West Indian woman who lived in the same apartment with her. Occasionally John met her when she left the theater at night and escorted her to her apartment door. He repeatedly importuned her to be nice to him once more. Her only answer was a sigh or a smile.

The West Indian woman was employed as a stenographer in the office of a Harlem political sheet. She was shy and retiring, and not much given to making friends with American Negroes. So many of them had snubbed and pained her when she was newly emigrant from her home in Barbados, that she lumped them all together, just as they seemed to do her people. She would not take under consideration that Emma Lou was new to Harlem, and not even aware of the prejudice American-born Harlemites nursed for foreign-born ones. She remembered too vividly how, on ringing the bell of a house where there had been a vacancy sign in the window, a little girl had come to the door, and, in answer to a voice in the back asking, “Who is it, Cora?” had replied, “monkey chaser wants to see the room you got to rent.” Jasmine Griffith was wary of all contact with American Negroes, for that had been only one of the many embittering incidents she had experienced.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢孤单极了。除了约翰、两三个同阿琳在台上做事的黑人演员，和一个跟她同住一所公寓的西印度群岛女人，她还是谁也不认识。偶尔约翰夜里在剧院门口等她，送她到公寓门口。他一再磨着她，要她再对他好一回。她的回答不是叹气，就是一笑。

那个西印度群岛女人在哈莱姆一份政治小报的编辑部当速记员。她怯生内向，不大同美国黑人交朋友。她刚从巴巴多斯老家移民来时，受过他们当中太多的冷落和刺伤，于是便把他们一竿子打翻，正如他们看待她的同胞那样。她不肯想一想，埃玛·卢初来哈莱姆，甚至不晓得本地生本地长的哈莱姆人对外来移民怀着怎样的偏见。她记得太清楚了：有一回，她按响一户窗上贴着“有空房”招牌的人家的门铃，一个小姑娘来开门，里屋有个声音问“谁呀，科拉”，她答道：“追猴子的（monkey chaser，美国黑人对西印度群岛移民的蔑称）要来看你出租的房间。”贾斯敏·格里菲思（Jasmine Griffith）对美国黑人的一切来往都存着戒心，因为这不过是她身历的许多寒心遭遇中的一桩。

===Original===
Emma Lou liked Jasmine, but was conscious of the fact that she could never penetrate her stolid reserve. They often talked to one another when they met in the hallway, and sometimes they stopped in one another’s rooms, but there was never any talk of going places together, never any informal revelations or intimacies.

The Negro actors in *Cabaret Gal*, all felt themselves superior to Emma Lou, and she in turn felt superior to them. She was just a maid. They were just common stage folk. Once she had had an inspiration. She had heard that *Cabaret Gal* was liable to run for two years or more on Broadway before road shows were sent out. Without saying anything to Arline she had approached the stage director and asked him, in all secrecy, what her chances were of getting into the cabaret ensemble. She knew they paid well, and she speculated that two or three years in *Cabaret Gal* might lay the foundations for a future stage career.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢喜欢贾斯敏，却也明白自己永远钻不透她那死板的矜持。两人在门厅里碰见，常聊上几句，有时也到彼此屋里坐坐，却从没有结伴出游的话，从没有随意的倾吐或亲密。

《歌舞厅女郎》里的黑人演员，个个自觉比埃玛·卢高一等，而她反过来也自觉比他们高一等。她不过是个女佣。他们不过是些下等戏子。有一回她灵机一动。她听说《歌舞厅女郎》在派出巡回班底之前，多半还能在百老汇再撑两年以上。她没跟阿琳透半个字，私下里去找舞台导演，问他自己有没有机会进那歌舞班底。她知道班底挣得多，她盘算着，在《歌舞厅女郎》里待上两三年，没准就打下将来舞台生涯的根基。

===Original===
“What the hell would Arline do,” he laughed, “if she didn’t have you to change her complexion before every performance?”

Emma Lou had smiled away this bit of persiflage and had reiterated her request in such a way that there was no mistaking her seriousness.

Sensing this, the director changed his mood, and admitted that even then two of the girls were dropping out of *Cabaret Gal* to sail for Europe with another show, booked for a season on the continent. But he hastened to tell her, as he saw her eyes brighten with anticipation:

“Well, you see, we worked out a color scheme that would be a complement to Arline’s makeup. You’ve noticed, no doubt, that all of the girls are about one color, and. …”

===Chinese===
“要是没有你每场演出前给她改换肤色，”他笑道，“阿琳他妈的可怎么办？”

这点插科打诨，埃玛·卢一笑置之，又把请求重申了一遍，那副郑重，错认不了。

导演觉出这层，换了口气，承认就在眼下，班里正有两个姑娘要退出《歌舞厅女郎》，随另一出戏渡海去欧洲，已订好在大陆演一季。可他眼见她的眼睛因期待而发亮，忙不迭告诉她：

“这个，你瞧，我们配好了一套肤色方案，跟阿琳的妆容正好相辅。你谅必也注意到了，姑娘们个个肤色都差不多，还有……”

===Original===
Unable to stammer any more, he had hastened away, embarrassed.

Emma Lou hadn’t noticed that all the girls were one color. In fact, she was certain they were not. She hastened to stand in the stage wings among them between scenes and observe their skin coloring. Despite many layers of liquid powder she could see that they were not all one color, but that they were either mulatto or light-brown skin. Their makeup and the lights gave them an appearance of sameness. She noticed that there were several black men in the ensemble, but that none of the women were dark. Then the breach between Emma Lou and the show people widened.

===Chinese===
他讪讪地再结巴不下去，红着脸赶紧走开了。

埃玛·卢原本没留意姑娘们是一个肤色。说实在的，她断定她们并非一个肤色。她赶紧趁幕间站到侧台，混到她们中间，观察她们的皮肤。隔着好多层粉底液，她仍看得出她们并非一个颜色，只是非黑白混血、即浅棕皮肤。妆容和灯光给了她们同一种外貌。她注意到班底里有好几个黑皮肤的男人，女人却没有一个是深肤色。从此，埃玛·卢和演戏人之间的裂痕更深了。

===Original===
Emma Lou had had another inspiration. She had decided to move. Perhaps if she were to live with a homey type of family they could introduce her to “the right sort of people.” She blamed her enforced isolation on the fact that she had made no worthwhile contacts. Mrs. Blake was a disagreeable remembrance. Since she came to think about it, Mrs. Blake had been distinctly patronizing like… like… her high school principal, or like Doris Garrett, the head of the only Negro sorority in the Southern California college she had attended. Doris Garrett had been very nice to all her colored schoolmates, but had seen to it that only those girls who were of a mulatto type were pledged for membership in the Greek letter society of which she was the head.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢又灵机一动。她决意搬家。倘若住进一户家常温馨人家，也许他们能引她结识“正经人”。她把自己这强加于身的孤寂，归咎于没结交上一个像样的人脉。布莱克太太是一段不痛快的记忆。细想起来，布莱克太太分明是居高临下，像……像……她的中学校长，又像多丽丝·加勒特——她上过的那所南加州大学里、唯一的黑人姊妹会的会长。多丽丝·加勒特对有色同学个个和气，却拿定了主意：只有黑白混血那一类型的姑娘，才配进她当会长的那个希腊字母姊妹会。

===Original===
Emma Lou reasoned that she couldn’t go on as she was, being alone and aching for congenial companionship. True, her job didn’t allow her much spare time. She had to be at Arline’s apartment at eleven every morning, but except on the two matinee days, she was free from two until seven-thirty p.m., when she had to be at the theater, and by eleven-thirty every night, she was in Harlem. Then she had all day Sunday to herself. Arline paid her a good salary, and she made tips from the first and second leads in the show, who used her spare moments. She had been working for six weeks now, and had saved one hundred dollars. She practically lived on her tips. Her salary was twenty-five dollars per week. Dinner was the only meal she had to pay for, and Arline gave her many clothes.

So Emma Lou began to think seriously of getting another room. She wanted more space and more air and more freedom from fish and cabbage smells. She had been in Harlem now for about fourteen weeks. Only fourteen weeks? The count stunned her. It seemed much longer. It was this rut she was in. Well, she would get out of it. Finding a room, a new room, would be the first step.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢盘算着，这样下去不成——孤零零一个人，又渴盼投缘的伴侣。不错，差事没给她多少空闲。每天上午十一点她得赶到阿琳的公寓，但除了两场日场的日子，从下午两点到晚上七点半她是自由的，七点半得到剧院，而每晚十一点半以前，她已回到哈莱姆。星期天更整日归她自己支配。阿琳给的工钱不薄，剧中的一号二号主演也常使唤她打零差，她从中挣些小费。她已做了六个星期，攒下一百美元。她的日子几乎全靠小费维持。周薪二十五美元。只有晚饭得自己掏钱，阿琳又送了她许多衣裳。

于是埃玛·卢开始认真盘算另找一间房。她要更宽敞、更透风，更要离煎鱼煮白菜的味儿远些。她来哈莱姆已约莫十四个星期了。才十四个星期？这个数目把她惊呆了。显得长得多呢。都怪她困在这摊死水里。好吧，她要爬出去。找间房，一间新房，就是头一步。

===Original===
Emma Lou asked Jasmine how one went about it. Jasmine was noncommittal, and said she didn’t know, but she had heard that *The Amsterdam News*, a Harlem Negro weekly, carried a large “Furnished rooms for rent” section. Emma Lou bought a copy of this paper, and, though attracted, did not stop to read the news columns under the streaming headlines to the effect “Headless Man Found In Trunk”; “Number Runner Given Sentence”; “Benefit Ball Huge Success”; but turned immediately to the advertising section.

There were many rooms advertised for rent, rooms of all sizes and for all prices, with all sorts of conveniences and inconveniences. Emma Lou was more bewildered than ever. Then, remembering that John had said that all the “dictys” lived between Seventh and Edgecombe Avenues on 136th, 137th, 138th and 139th Streets, decided to check off the places in these streets. John had also told her that “dictys” lived in the imposing apartment houses on Edgecombe, Bradhurst and St. Nicholas Avenues. “Dictys” were Harlem’s high-toned people, folk listed in the local social register, as it were. But Emma Lou did not care to live in another apartment building. She preferred, or thought she would prefer, living in a private house where there would be fewer people and more privacy.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢问贾斯敏这事儿该怎么办。贾斯敏不置可否，说不清楚，只听说哈莱姆的黑人周报《阿姆斯特丹新闻》登着好大一块“带家具房间出租”的版面。埃玛·卢买了一份，虽然心痒，却没停下来读新闻栏——那些醒目大标题底下，什么“箱中发现无头男尸”“‘数字’彩票跑腿仔获刑”“义演舞会大获成功”——径直翻到了广告版。

出租的房间多得很，大小不等，价钱不等，方便与不便也不一而足。埃玛·卢比先前更眼花缭乱。后来想起约翰说过，“体面人”（dictys）全住在第七大道和埃奇库姆大道之间的136街、137街、138街、139街上，便决定先把这些街上的地方圈出来。约翰还告诉她，埃奇库姆、布拉德赫斯特和圣尼古拉斯大道上那些气派的公寓楼里，住的也都是“体面人”。“体面人”就是哈莱姆的高等人，好比上了本地社交名录的人物。可埃玛·卢不愿再住公寓楼。她更喜欢——或者自以为喜欢——住私宅，人少些，清静些。

===Original===
The first place Emma Lou approached had a double room for two girls, two men, or a couple. They thought their advertisement had said as much. It hadn’t, but Emma Lou apologized, and left. The next three places were nice but exorbitant. Front rooms with two windows and a kitchenette, renting for twelve, fourteen and sixteen dollars a week. Emma Lou had planned to spend not more than eight or nine dollars at the most. The next place smelled far worse than her present home. The room was smaller and the rent higher. Emma Lou began to lose hope, then rallying, had gone to the last place on her list from *The Amsterdam News*. The landlady was the spinster type, garrulous and friendly. She had a high forehead, keen intellectual eyes, and a sharp profile. The room she showed to Emma Lou was both spacious and clean, and she only asked eight dollars and fifty cents per week for it.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢找的头一处是间双人房，招两个姑娘、两个男人或一对夫妻。人家以为自家广告上写明了。其实没写，可埃玛·卢道了歉，走了。接下来三处都不错，只是贵得离谱：临街双窗房带小厨房，周租十二、十四、十六美元。埃玛·卢打定的谱，至多八九块。再下一处，气味比她现在的住处难闻得多，房间更小，租金更高。埃玛·卢开始泄气，缓过劲来，又去了《阿姆斯特丹新闻》名单上的最后一处。房东太太是老姑娘型的人，话多而热络。她额头高，目光锐利有书卷气，侧脸线条利落。她领埃玛·卢看的房间又宽敞又干净，每周只收八美元半。

===Original===
After showing her the room, the landlady had invited Emma Lou downstairs to her parlor Emma Lou found a place to sit down on a damask covered divan. There were many other seats in the room, but the landlady, *Miss* Carrington, as she had introduced herself, insisted upon sitting down beside her. They talked for about a half an hour, and in that time, being a successful “pumper,” *Miss* Carrington had learned the history of Emma Lou’s experiences in Harlem. Satisfied of her ground, she grew more familiar, placed her hand on Emma Lou’s knee, then finally put her arm around her waist. Emma Lou felt uncomfortable. This sudden and unexpected intimacy disturbed her. The room was close and hot. Damask coverings seemed to be everywhere. Damask coverings and dull red draperies and mauve walls.

“Don’t worry any more, dearie, I’ll take care of you from now on,” and she had tightened her arm around Emma Lou’s waist, who, feeling more uncomfortable than ever, looked at her wristwatch.

“I must be going.”

“Do you want the room?” There was a note of anxiety in her voice. “There are lots of nice girls living here. We call this the ‘Old Maid’s Home.’ We have parties among ourselves, and just have a grand time. Talk about fun! I know you’d be happy here.”

===Chinese===
看过房间，房东太太请埃玛·卢下楼到她的客厅坐坐。埃玛·卢在一张锦缎面的长沙发上寻了座。屋里座位原多得很，可这位自称卡林顿*小姐*（*Miss* Carrington）的房东太太，非要挨着她坐。两人聊了约半个钟头，就在这半个钟头里，卡林顿小姐凭着她那手炉火纯青的“套话”功夫，把埃玛·卢来哈莱姆的经历摸了个底朝天。觉得有了底，她愈发亲热起来，手搭上埃玛·卢的膝盖，末了干脆一条胳膊搂住她的腰。埃玛·卢浑身不自在。这突如其来的亲昵让她发慌。屋里又闷又热。锦缎罩着的东西仿佛无处不在。锦缎，暗红的帷幔，淡紫的墙。

“别再愁了，亲爱的，往后有我照应你。”她搂着埃玛·卢腰的胳膊收紧了。埃玛·卢愈发浑身不自在，看了看腕表。

“我该走了。”

“你到底要不要这房间？”她嗓音里透出一丝急切。“这儿住着好多好姑娘呢。我们都管这儿叫‘老姑娘之家’。我们自己人常开聚会，玩得可痛快了。要说乐子，这才叫乐子！我包你住这儿快活。”

===Original===
Emma Lou knew she would too, and said as much. Then hastily, she gave *Miss* Carrington a three dollar deposit on the room, and left… to continue her search for a new place to live.

There were no more places on her *Amsterdam News* list, so noticing “Vacancy” signs in windows along the various streets, Emma Lou decided to walk along and blindly choose a house. None of the houses in 137th Street impressed her, they were all too cold looking, and she was through with 136th Street. *Miss* Carrington lived there. She sauntered down the “L” trestled Eighth Avenue to 138th Street. Then she turned toward Seventh Avenue and strolled along slowly on the south side of the street. She chose the south side because she preferred the appearance of the red brick houses there to the green brick ones on the north side. After she had passed by three “Vacancy” signs, she decided to enter the very next house where such a sign was displayed.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢知道她确实会快活，也这样说了。随后她匆匆付了卡林顿*小姐*三美元的房钱定金，走了……接着找她的新住处去。

《阿姆斯特丹新闻》的名单走完了，埃玛·卢瞧见沿街窗上贴着“有空房”的招牌，便决定信步走去，闭着眼睛挑一家。137街的房子没一处入她的眼，个个显得太冷清，而136街她是领教够了——卡林顿*小姐*就住那儿。她沿着“高架”铁架横跨的第八大道慢慢踱到138街，再折向第七大道，在街南侧缓步溜达。选南侧，是因为她喜欢那边红砖房子的样子，胜过北侧的绿砖房。走过三张“有空房”的招牌后，她打定主意，下一家贴这招牌的，进去就没商量。

===Original===
Seeing one, she climbed the terraced stone stairs, rang the doorbell and waited expectantly. There was a long pause. She rang the bell again, and just as she relieved her pressure, the door was opened by a bedizened yellow woman with sand colored hair and deep set corn colored eyes. Emma Lou noted the incongruous thickness of her lips.

“How do you do. I … I … would like to see one of your rooms.”

The woman eyed Emma Lou curiously and looked as if she were about to snort. Then slowly she began to close the door in the astonished girl’s face. Emma Lou opened her mouth and tried to speak, but the woman forestalled her, saying testily in broken English:

“We have nothing here.”

===Chinese===
见着一张，她登上阶台式的石阶，按了门铃，满怀期待地等着。好长一段空当。她又按了一回，指头刚松劲，门开了——一个打扮得花里胡哨的浅黄肤色女人，沙色的头发，深陷的玉米色眼睛。埃玛·卢注意到她那两片嘴唇厚得同这张脸不衬。

“您好。我……我……想看看您家的房间。”

那女人好奇地打量埃玛·卢，看那样子像要嗤出一声。随后她慢慢关上门，冲着这目瞪口呆的姑娘的脸关。埃玛·卢张口想说话，那女人抢在前头，用蹩脚的英语没好气地甩出一句：

“我们这儿什么都没有。”

===Original===
Persons of color didn’t associate with blacks in the Caribbean Island she had come from.

From then on Emma Lou intensified her suffering, mulling over and magnifying each malignant experience. They grew within her and were nourished by constant introspection and livid reminiscences. Again, she stood upon the platform in the auditorium of the Boise high school. Again that first moment of realization and its attendant strictures were disinterred and revivified. She was black, too black, there was no getting around it. Her mother had thought so, and had often wished that she had been a boy. Black boys can make a go of it, but black girls. …

No one liked black anyway. …

Wanted: light colored girl to work as waitress in tearoom. …

Wanted: Nurse girl, light colored preferred (children are afraid of black folks). …

“I don’ haul no coal. …”

“It’s like this, Emma Lou, they don’t want no dark girls in their sorority. They ain’t pledged us, and we’re the only two they ain’t, and we’re both black.”

*The ineluctability of raw experience! The muddy mirroring of life’s perplexities. … Seeing everything in terms of self. … The spreading sensitiveness of an adder’s sting.*

“Mr. Brown has someone else in mind. …”

“We have nothing here. …”

She should have been a boy. A black boy could get along, but a black girl. …

---

===Chinese===
在她来的那座加勒比海岛上，有色人种向来不同黑色人种来往。

打这以后，埃玛·卢的自苦变本加厉，把每一桩恶毒的遭遇翻来覆去地咀嚼、放大。它们在她心里长大，又被没完没了的内省和铁青的回忆滋养着。她又站上了博伊西中学礼堂的台子。那头一回恍然大悟的时刻，连同随之而来的种种苛责，又被掘出来，重新活了过来。她是黑的，太黑了，这没法绕开。母亲就这么想过，还常盼她本该是个男孩。黑男孩还能闯出个名堂，黑女孩呢……

再说，黑色本来就没人喜欢……

招聘：浅肤色姑娘，茶室女侍……

招聘：保姆一名，浅肤色者优先（孩子怕黑皮肤的人）……

“我可不拉煤……”

“是这么回事，埃玛·卢，她们姊妹会不要深肤色的姑娘。没被拉入会的就我们俩，我们俩都是黑的。”

*生涩经验的无可回避！人生困惑的泥泞映照。……凡事只以自身度量。……蝮蛇蜇痛般蔓延开去的敏感。*

“布朗先生另有人选……”

“我们这儿什么都没有……”

她本该是个男孩。黑男孩还过得下去，黑女孩呢……

---

===Original===
Arline was leaving the cast of *Cabaret Gal* for two weeks. Her mother had died in Chicago. The Negro Carmen must be played by an understudy, a real mulatto this time, who, lacking Arline’s poise and personality, nevertheless brought down the house because of the crude vividity of her performance. Emma Lou was asked to act as her maid while Arline was away. Indignantly, she had taken the alternative of a two weeks’ vacation. Imagine her being maid for a *Negro* woman! It was unthinkable.

Left entirely to herself, she proceeded to make herself more miserable. Lying in bed late every morning, semiconscious, body burning, mind disturbed by thoughts of sex. Never before had she experienced such physical longing. She often thought of John and at times was almost driven to slip him into her room once more. But John couldn’t satisfy her. She felt that she wanted something more than just the mere physical relationship with someone whose body and body coloring were distasteful to her.

===Chinese===
阿琳要离开《歌舞厅女郎》班底两个星期。她母亲在芝加哥去世了。黑人卡门得由替角来演，这回是个真正的黑白混血——虽不及阿琳那份从容和个性，却凭着一股生猛鲜活，场场博得满堂彩。有人来问埃玛·卢，阿琳不在的这两个星期，可愿伺候这位替角。她一怒之下选了另一条路：休整两个星期。想想吧，叫她给一个*黑人*女人当女佣！想都别想。

彻底只剩她自己了，她便着手把自己折腾得更惨。每天早晨赖在床上，半睡半醒，身体发烫，满脑子搅扰的情欲之念。这样赤裸裸的肉体渴求，她从来不曾有过。她常想起约翰，有几次险些又要把他偷偷领进屋。可约翰满足不了她。她要的好像不止是纯粹的肉体关系——何况那人的身子、那身肤色，她本就嫌恶。

===Original===
When she did decide to get up, she would spend an hour before her dresser mirror, playing with her hair, parting it on the right side, then on the left, then in the middle, brushing it straight back, or else teasing it with the comb, inducing it to crackle with electric energy. Then she would cover it with a cap, pin a towel around her shoulders, and begin to experiment with her complexion.

She had decided to bleach her skin as much as possible. She had bought many creams and skin preparations, and had tried to remember the various bleaching aids she had heard of throughout her life. She remembered having heard her grandmother speak of that “old fool, Carrie Campbell,” who, already a fair mulatto, had wished to pass for white. To accomplish this she had taken arsenic wafers, which were guaranteed to increase the pallor of one’s skin.

Emma Lou had obtained some of these arsenic wafers and eaten them, but they had only served to give her pains in the pit of her stomach. Next she determined upon a peroxide solution in addition to something which was known as Black and White Ointment. After she had been using these for about a month she thought that she could notice some change. But in reality the only effects were an increase in blackheads, irritating rashes, and a burning skin.

===Chinese===
真起了床，她能在梳妆镜前一坐一个钟头，摆弄头发：右边分线，左边分线，中间分线，直直向后梳，要么拿梳子撩拨它，逗得它噼里啪啦迸出电花。然后用帽子罩住，肩上别一条毛巾，开始在肤色上做起实验。

她打定主意，要把皮肤漂得越白越好。各色膏霜和护肤品买了一堆，又搜肠刮肚，想起这辈子听过的种种漂白偏方。她记得听外祖母说起过“老蠢货卡丽·坎贝尔”——本来已是浅肤色的黑白混血，还想冒充白人。为成此事，她吃砷片（arsenic wafers），据说保管叫人的皮肤白上加白。

埃玛·卢也弄了些这种砷片来吃，只落得心口窝一阵阵疼。接着她又用上双氧水，外加一种名叫“黑与白”药膏（Black and White Ointment）的东西。用了约莫一个月，她自以为看出了点变化。其实真正的功效，不过是黑头多了，皮疹刺痒，皮肤火辣辣地烧。

===Original===
Meanwhile she found her thoughts straying often to the chap she had danced with in the cabaret. She was certain he lived in Harlem, and she was determined to find him. She took it for granted that he would remember her. So day after day, she strolled up and down Seventh Avenue from 125th to 145th Street, then crossed to Lenox Avenue and traversed the same distance. *He* was her ideal. He looked like a college person. He dressed well. His skin was such a warm and different color, and she had been tantalized by the mysterious slant and deepness of his oriental-like eyes.

===Chinese===
这期间，她的心思常溜到那个在夜总会同她跳过舞的小伙子身上。她笃定他住在哈莱姆，铁了心要把他找着。她想当然地认定，他也记得她。于是她日复一日，沿第七大道从125街逛到145街，再折到伦诺克斯大道，把同样的路程走上一遍。*他*是她的理想。他看着像个念过大学的人。他穿得讲究。他的肤色那样温润、那样与众不同，还有他那双东方人似的眼睛，斜得神秘，深得撩人，害她心里痒痒的。

===Original===
After walking the streets like this the first few days of her vacation, she became aware of the futility of her task. She saw many men on the street, many well dressed, seemingly cultured, pleasingly colored men and boys. They seemed to congregate in certain places, and stand there all the day. She found herself wondering when and where they worked, and how they could afford to dress so well. She began to admire their well formed bodies and gloried in the way their trousers fit their shapely limbs, and in the way they walked, bringing their heels down so firmly and so noisily on the pavement. Rubber heels were out of fashion. Hard heels, with metal heel plates were the mode of the day. These corner loafers were so carefree, always smiling, eyes always bright. She loved to hear them laugh, and loved to watch them, when, without any seeming provocation, they would cut a few dance steps or do a jig. It seemed as if they either did this from sheer exuberance or else simply to relieve the monotony of standing still.

===Chinese===
假期的头几天这样逛下来，她明白了自己的差事多么徒劳。街上的男人多的是，许多衣冠楚楚、看着有教养、肤色喜人的男人和半大小子。他们仿佛在几处固定的地方聚堆，一站一整天。她不由纳闷，他们何时何地做工，怎么穿得起这么讲究。她开始欣赏他们匀称的身板，看裤子包裹着漂亮的腿脚那份熨帖看得心花怒放，看他们走路，鞋跟在人行道上踏得又稳又响——橡胶跟不时兴了，硬鞋跟、钉金属跟掌的，才是当今的时兴。这些街角闲汉多么无忧无虑，脸上总挂着笑，眼睛总亮着。她爱听他们大笑，爱看他们毫无来由地就舞上几步，或来一段吉格舞。他们要么是兴头上憋不住，要么不过是站乏了换换花样。

===Original===
Of course, they noticed her as she passed and repassed day after day. She eyed them boldly enough, but she was still too self-conscious to broadcast an inviting look. She was too afraid of public ridicule or a mass mocking. Ofttimes men spoke to her, and tried to make advances, but they were never the kind she preferred. She didn’t like black men, and the others seemed to keep their distance.

===Chinese===
当然，她日复一日走过去又走回来，他们也留意到她了。她瞧他们瞧得够大胆，可要她当众抛出一个邀请的眼风，她还太怕难为情。她太怕众人的讪笑、齐声的哄笑。时常有男人上来搭话，想献殷勤，可从来不是她中意的那一类。她不喜欢黑皮肤的男人，而别的男人，仿佛又同她保持着距离。

===Original===
One day, tired of walking, she went into a motion picture theater on the avenue. She had seen the feature picture before, but was too lethargic and too uninterested in other things to go someplace else. In truth, there was no place else for her to go. So she sat in the darkened theater, squirmed around in her seat, and began to wonder just how many thousands of Negroes there were in Harlem. This theater was practically full, even in mid-afternoon. The streets were crowded, other theaters were crowded, and then there must be many more at home and at work. Emma Lou wondered what the population of Negro Harlem was. She should have read that Harlem number of the *Survey Graphic* issued two or three years ago. But Harlem hadn’t interested her then for she had had no idea at the time that she would ever come to Harlem.

Someone sat down beside her. She was too occupied with herself to notice who the person was. The feature picture was over and a comedy was being flashed on the screen. Emma Lou found herself laughing, and, finding something on the screen to interest her, squared herself in her seat. Then she felt a pressure on one of her legs, the warm fleshy pressure of another leg. Her first impulse was to change her position. Perhaps she had touched the person next to her. Perhaps it was an accident. She moved her leg a little, but she still felt the pressure. Maybe it wasn’t an accident. Her heart beat fast, her limbs began to quiver. The leg which was pressed against hers had such a pleasant, warm, fleshy feeling. She stole a glance at the person who had sat down next to her. He smiled… an impudent boyish smile and pressed her leg the harder.

===Chinese===
有一天，走累了，她进了大道上的一家电影院。正片她看过了，可她懒得动，也提不起兴致去别处。说实话，她本就无处可去。于是她坐在黑黢黢的影院里，在座位上扭来扭去，开始琢磨哈莱姆的黑人到底有几千几万。这才下午三点来钟，影院竟几乎满座。街上人挤人，别家影院也满，还有更多的人想必在家里、在干活。埃玛·卢纳闷，黑人哈莱姆到底有多少人口。两三年前《调查画报》（Survey Graphic）出哈莱姆专号那阵，她本该读一读的。可那时候哈莱姆与她何干，她压根没想到自己有朝一日会来哈莱姆。

有人在她身边坐下。她满心思都在自己身上，没留意来的是谁。正片放完，银幕上闪起一部喜剧。埃玛·卢发觉自己在笑，银幕上有了让她来兴致的东西，便端端正正坐好了。随后她觉出一条腿上有股压力，另一条腿的温热的、肉乎乎的压力。她的第一反应是挪一挪。也许是她碰着了邻座。也许是无意。她把腿挪了挪，压力还在。也许不是无意。她的心怦怦跳，四肢打起颤。贴着她那条腿，有那么一种舒服的、温热的、肉乎乎的感觉。她偷偷瞟了一眼坐在身边的人。他笑了……一脸放肆的孩子气的笑，把她的腿贴得更紧了。

===Original===
“Funny cuss, that guy,” he was speaking to her.

*Slap him in the face. Change your seat. Don’t be an idiot. He has a nice smile. Look at him again.*

“Did you see him in *Long Pants*?”

He was leaning closer now, and Emma Lou took note of a teakwood tan hand resting on her knee. She took another look at him, and saw that he had curly hair. He leaned toward her, and she leaned toward him. Their shoulders touched, his hand reached for hers and stole it from her lap. She wished that the theater wasn’t so dark. But if it hadn’t been so dark this couldn’t have happened. She wondered if his hair and eyes were brown or jet black.

The feature picture was being reeled off again. They were too busy talking to notice that. When it was half over, they left their seats together. Before they reached the street, Emma Lou handed him three dollars, and, leaving the theater, they went to an apartment house on 140th Street, off Lenox Avenue. Emma Lou waited downstairs in the dirty marble hallway where she was stifled by urinal smells and stared at by passing people, waited for about ten minutes, then, in answer to his call, climbed one flight of stairs, and was led into a well furnished, though dark, apartment.

===Chinese===
“真逗，这家伙。”他在跟她说话。

*扇他一巴掌。换个座位。别犯傻。他笑起来挺好看。再看看他。*

“你看过他演的《长裤》（Long Pants）吗？”

这会儿他凑得更近了，埃玛·卢注意到一只柚木般棕褐的手搭在她膝上。她又看了他一眼，看清他长着卷发。他朝她俯过来，她朝他俯过去。两人的肩膀碰上了，他的手够到她的手，把它从她膝上偷走。她巴望影院别这么黑。可要不是这么黑，这一切也就不会发生。她猜他的头发和眼睛是棕色的呢，还是乌黑的呢。

正片又从头放起。他们光顾着说话，没留意。放到一半，两人一同起身离座。还没走到街上，埃玛·卢就塞给他三美元；出了影院，两人去了伦诺克斯大道旁140街上的一幢公寓楼。埃玛·卢在楼下肮脏的大理石门厅里等——尿臊气熏得她透不过气，过路的人拿眼盯她——等了约莫十分钟，听见他一唤，便登上一层楼梯，被领进一套陈设讲究、却黑黢黢的公寓。

===Original===
His name was Jasper Crane. He was from Virginia. Living in Harlem with his brother, so he said. He had only been in New York a month. Didn’t have a job yet. His brother wasn’t very nice to him… wanted to kick him out because he was jealous of him, thought his wife was more attentive than a sister-in-law should be. He asked Emma Lou to lend him five dollars. He said he wanted to buy a job. She did. And when he left her, he kissed her passionately and promised to meet her on the next day and to telephone her within an hour.

===Chinese===
他叫贾斯珀·克莱恩（Jasper Crane），弗吉尼亚人。据他自己说，同哥哥一起住在哈莱姆。来纽约才一个月。还没找到差事。哥哥待他不怎么样……想撵他走，因为吃他的醋，觉得自家老婆对这个小叔子，殷勤得过了当嫂子的本分。他向埃玛·卢借五美元，说要花钱买个差事。她给了。他临走时热吻了她，约定第二天见面，一小时内给她打电话。

===Original===
But he didn’t telephone nor did Emma Lou ever see him again. The following day she waited for an hour and a half in the vicinity of that hallway where they were supposed to meet again. Then she went to the motion picture theater where they had met, and sat in the same seat in the same row so that he could find her. She sat there through two shows, then came back on the next day, and on the next. Meanwhile several other men approached her, a panting fat Jew, whom she reported to the usher, a hunchback, whom she pitied and then admired as he “made” the girl sitting on the other side of him; and there were several not very clean, trampy-looking men, but no Jasper.

He had asked her if she ever went to the Renaissance Casino, a public hall, where dances were held every night, so Emma Lou decided to go there on a Saturday, hoping to see him. She drew twenty-five dollars from the bank in order to buy a new dress, a very fine elaborate dress, which she got from a “hot” man, who had been recommended to her by Jasmine. “Hot” men sold supposedly stolen goods, thus enabling Harlem folk to dress well but cheaply. Then she spent the entire afternoon and evening preparing herself for the night, had her hair washed and marcelled, and her fingernails manicured.

===Chinese===
可他没有打电话，埃玛·卢也再没见过他。第二天，她在两人约好再见的那截门厅附近，等了一个半钟头。随后她去了他们相遇的那家电影院，坐在同一排同一个座位上，好让他找得着她。她坐完了两场，第二天再来，第三天又来。这期间另有一些男人凑近过她：一个气喘吁吁的胖犹太人，她报告了引座员；一个驼背，她先可怜他，转而佩服他——眼看他怎么把坐在他另一边的姑娘“拿下”；还有几个不大干净、流里流气的男人。只是没有贾斯珀。

他问过她去不去文艺复兴娱乐场（Renaissance Casino），一所每晚开舞会的公共舞厅。于是埃玛·卢决定星期六去一趟，指望撞见他。她从银行取了二十五美元，要添一件新裙子，一件顶讲究、顶花哨的裙子——是从一个“热货贩子”手里买的，那人由贾斯敏引荐。“热货贩子”卖的据说是贼赃，让哈莱姆人花小钱也穿得体面。接着她把整个下午和傍晚都用来为夜里张罗：洗了头，烫了马塞尔波浪，修了指甲。

===Original===
Before putting on her dress she stood in front of her mirror for over an hour, fixing her face, drenching it with a peroxide solution, plastering it with a mudpack, massaging it with a bleaching ointment, and then, as a final touch, using much vanishing cream and powder. She even ate an arsenic wafer. The only visible effect of all this on her complexion was to give it an ugly purple tinge, but Emma Lou was certain that it made her skin less dark.

===Chinese===
穿裙子之前，她在镜子前头站了一个多钟头，收拾那张脸：双氧水浇透，泥膏糊满，漂白膏揉遍，末了一道工序，雪花膏和粉厚厚地上。她甚至吃了一片砷片。这一切在她肤色上唯一看得见的效果，是给脸添了一层难看的紫色，可埃玛·卢笃定，这一来她的皮肤没那么黑了。

===Original===
She hailed a taxi and went to the Renaissance Casino. She did feel foolish, going there without an escort, but the doorman didn’t seem to notice. Perhaps it was all right. Perhaps it was customary for Harlem girls to go about unaccompanied. She checked her wraps and wandered along the promenade that bordered the dance floor. It was early yet, just ten-thirty, and only a few couples were dancing. She found a chair, and tried to look as if she were waiting for someone. The orchestra stopped playing, people crowded past her. She liked the dance hall, liked its draped walls and ceilings, its harmonic color design and soft lights.

The music began again. She didn’t see Jasper. A spindly legged yellow boy, awkward and bashful, asked her to dance with him. She did. The boy danced badly, but dancing with him was better than sitting there alone, looking foolish. She did wish that he would assume a more upright position and stop scrunching his shoulders. It seemed as if he were trying to bend both their backs to the breaking point. As they danced they talked about the music. He asked her did she have an escort. She said yes, and hurried to the ladies’ room when the dance was over.

She didn’t particularly like the looks of the crowd. It was well-behaved enough, but… well… one could see that they didn’t belong to the cultured classes. They weren’t the right sort of people. Maybe nice people didn’t come here. Jasper hadn’t been so nice. She wished she could see him, wouldn’t she give him a piece of her mind?—And for the first time she really sensed the baseness of the trick he had played on her.

===Chinese===
她招了辆出租汽车，去文艺复兴娱乐场。没有人伴，她确实觉得傻气，可门房像是没觉出什么。也许没关系。也许哈莱姆的姑娘独来独往本就是常事。她寄了外衣，沿着舞池边的回廊信步走去。还早，才十点半，跳舞的只有寥寥几对。她寻了把椅子，装出等人的模样。乐队奏罢一曲，人流从她身边挤过去。她喜欢这舞厅，喜欢它垂幔的墙壁和天花板，喜欢它和谐的色彩和柔和的灯。

音乐又起。她没见着贾斯珀。一个细脚伶仃的浅黄肤色小子，又笨拙又腼腆，来请她跳舞。她跳了。小子跳得糟，可同他跳，总好过独坐那里干冒傻气。她真心希望他把腰杆挺直些，别再缩着肩膀——那样子活像要把他们两人的脊梁都弯断才罢休。两人边跳边谈音乐。他问她有没有舞伴。她说有，一曲跳完便赶紧躲进女盥洗室。

这场子的人的模样，她说不上多喜欢。规矩倒是够规矩，可是……唉……一眼看得出，不属于有教养的阶层。不是正经人。也许正经人不来这种地方。贾斯珀也不见得多正经。她巴望能见着他，好生数落他一顿，不是吗？——而且头一回，她真真切切觉出他耍她的那套把戏有多下作。

===Original===
She walked out of the ladies’ room and found herself again on the promenade. For a moment she stood there, watching the dancers. The floor was more crowded now, the dancers more numerous and gay. She watched them swirl and glide around the dance floor, and an intense longing for Jasper or John or anyone welled up within her. It was terrible to be so alone, terrible to stand here and see other girls contentedly curled up in men’s arms. She had been foolish to come, Jasper probably never came here. In truth he was no doubt far away from New York by now. What sense was there in her being here. She wasn’t going to stay. She was going home, but before starting toward the check room, she took one more glance at the dancers and saw her cabaret dancing partner.

He was dancing with a slender brown-skin girl, his smile as ecstatic and intense as before. Emma Lou noted the pleasing lines of his body encased in a form-fitting blue suit. Why didn’t he look her way?

“May I have this dance?” A well modulated deep voice. A slender stripling, arrayed in brown, with a dark brown face. He had dimples. They danced. Emma Lou was having difficulty in keeping track of Alva. He seemed to be consciously striving to elude her. He seemed to be deliberately darting in among clusters of couples, where he would remain hidden for some time, only to reappear far ahead or behind her.

Her partner was congenial. He introduced himself, but she did not hear his name, for at that moment, Alva and his partner glided close by. Emma Lou actually shoved the supple, slender boy she was dancing with in Alva’s direction. She mustn’t lose him this time. She must speak. They veered close to one another. They almost collided. Alva looked into her face. She smiled and spoke. He acknowledged her salute, but stared at her, frankly perplexed, and there was no recognition in his face as he moved away, bending his head close to that of his partner, the better to hear something she was asking him.

===Chinese===
她走出女盥洗室，又回到了回廊上。她站了片刻，看人跳舞。舞池这会儿更挤了，舞客更多、更欢。她望着他们绕着舞池旋转滑行，一股对贾斯珀、对约翰、对随便什么人的强烈渴望，从心底涌了上来。这样孤零零的太可怕了，站在这里眼看别的姑娘心满意足地蜷在男人怀里，太可怕了。她来真是犯傻，贾斯珀多半压根不来这儿。说实话，此刻他八成早远离纽约了。她还待在这里做什么呢。她不待了。她要回家，可还没动身去衣帽间，她又朝舞客望了一眼——望见了夜总会里同她跳过舞的那位舞伴。

他正同一位细瘦的棕肤姑娘跳舞，笑得同先前一样销魂、一样忘我。埃玛·卢打量着他裹在贴身蓝西装里的漂亮身条。他怎么不朝她这边看一眼呢？

“可否请你跳这一支？”一口抑扬有致的低音。一个瘦条的少年郎，一身棕色装束，深棕色的脸，脸上有酒窝。两人跳起来。埃玛·卢这边可犯了难：阿尔瓦的行踪难追。他像是存心躲她。像是故意一头扎进一对对舞侣堆里，藏上好一阵子，再冒出来时，已远远到了她前头或后头。

她的舞伴挺随和。他自报了姓名，她却没听清，因为正在那时，阿尔瓦和他的舞伴滑到了近旁。埃玛·卢简直是把怀里这个柔韧的瘦条少年朝阿尔瓦的方向推。这回可不能再丢了他。她得开口。两人侧身挨近。险些撞上。阿尔瓦看进她脸里。她笑着开了口。他回了她的招呼，却直勾勾盯着她，一脸坦白的困惑；他一边挪开，一边把头俯向他的舞伴，好听清她在问他什么——那张脸上，没有一丝认出她的影子。

===Original===
The slender brown boy clung to Emma Lou’s arm, treated her to a soda, and, at her request, piloted her around the promenade. She saw Alva sitting in a box in the balcony, and suggested to her companion that they parade around the balcony for a while. He assented. He was lonesome too. First summer in New York. Just graduated from Virginia Union University. Going to Columbia School of Law next year. Nice boy, but no appeal. Too—supple.

They passed by Alva’s box. He wasn’t there. Two other couples and the girl he had been dancing with were. Emma Lou and her companion walked the length of the balcony, then retraced their steps just in time to see Alva coming around the corner carrying a cup of water. She watched the rhythmic swing of his legs, like symmetrical pendulums, perfectly shaped; and she admired once more the intriguing lines of his body and pleasing foreignness of his face. As they met, she smiled at him. He was certain he did not know her but he stopped and was polite, feeling that he must find out who she was and where he had met her.

===Chinese===
那瘦条棕肤少年挽住埃玛·卢的胳膊，请她喝了杯汽水，又应她的请求，领她在回廊上兜了一圈。她瞧见阿尔瓦坐在楼座一间包厢里，便向同伴提议，到楼座上溜达一会儿。他应了。他也孤单着呢。头一个夏天来纽约。刚从弗吉尼亚联合大学毕业，明年进哥伦比亚法学院。人是好人，可不来电。太——柔韧。

两人路过阿尔瓦的包厢。人不在。包厢里是另外两对舞侣，还有方才同他跳舞的那个姑娘。埃玛·卢和同伴沿楼座走了一个来回，折回时正赶上阿尔瓦端着一杯水转过拐角。她望着他双腿摆动的节奏，像两只匀称的钟摆，线条完美；又一次欣赏他身上撩人的线条，和他脸上那讨人喜欢的异域味。两下相遇，她朝他一笑。他明知道自己不认识她，却停下脚步，客客气气，觉得非弄清她是谁、在哪儿见过不可。

===Original===
“How do you do?” Emma Lou held out her hand. He shifted the cup of water from his right hand to his left. “I’m glad to see you again.” They shook hands. His clasp was warm, his palm soft and sweaty. The supple lad stepped to one side. “I—I,” Emma Lou was speaking now, “have often wondered if we would meet again.” Alva wanted to laugh. He could not imagine who this girl with the purple-powdered skin was. Where had he seen her? She must be mistaking him for someone else. Well, he was game. He spoke sincerely:

“And I, too, have wanted to see you.”

===Chinese===
“你好！”埃玛·卢伸出手。他把那杯水从右手换到左手。“真高兴又见到你。”两人握了手。他的握力是热的，掌心又软又潮。那柔韧的少年郎退到一边。“我——我，”这回是埃玛·卢在说话了，“常想不知咱们还会不会再见面。”阿尔瓦直想笑。他想不出这个脸上扑着紫粉的姑娘是谁。在哪儿见过她？她八成是把他错认成别人了。好吧，他奉陪。他诚恳地说：

“我也一直想见你。”

===Original===
Emma Lou couldn’t blush, but she almost blubbered with joy.

“Perhaps we’ll have a dance together.”

“My God,” thought Alva, “She’s a quick worker.”

“Oh, certainly, where can I find you?”

“Downstairs on the promenade, near the center boxes.”

“The one after this?” This seemed to be the easiest way out. He could easily dodge her later.

“Yes,” and she moved away, the supple lad clinging to her arm again.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢的脸红不出来，心里却欢喜得几乎哭出来。

“也许咱们能一起跳个舞。”

“我的天，”阿尔瓦想，“这姑娘下手真快。”

“哦，那敢情好，上哪儿找你？”

“楼下回廊上，中间包厢附近。”

“下一支？”这似乎是最省事的脱身法。回头躲她还不容易。

“好。”她走开了，那柔韧的少年郎又挽上她的胳膊。

===Original===
“Who’s the ‘spade,’ Alvar?” Geraldine had seen him stop to talk to her.

“Damned if I know.”

“Aw, sure you know who she is. You danced with her at Small’s.” Braxton hadn’t forgotten.

“Well, I never. Is that *it*?” Laughter all around as he told about their first meeting. But he didn’t dodge her, for Geraldine and Braxton riled him with their pertinacious badinage. He felt that they were making more fun of him than of her, and to show them just how little he minded their kidding he stalked off to find her. She was waiting, the slim, brown stripling swaying beside her, importuning her not to wait longer. He didn’t want to lose her. She didn’t want to lose Alva, and was glad when they danced off together.

“Who’s your boyfriend?” Alva had fortified himself with gin. His breath smelled familiar.

“Just an acquaintance.” She couldn’t let him know she had come here unescorted. “I didn’t think you’d remember me.”

“Of course, I did; how could I forget you?” Smooth tongue, phrases with a double meaning.

“I didn’t forget *you*.” Emma Lou was being coy. “I have often looked for you.”

===Chinese===
“那个‘黑桃’（spade，对黑人的谑称）是谁，阿尔瓦？”杰拉尔丁瞧见他停下来同她搭话。

“鬼才知道。”

“嗐，你明明认得她。你在斯莫尔乐园跟她跳过舞。”布拉克斯顿可没忘。

“嚯，真有这事？原来*是*她呀！”他讲起两人初见的情形，四下笑声一片。可他到底没躲她——杰拉尔丁和布拉克斯顿那没完没了的打趣把他激了。他觉得他们取笑他多过取笑她，为了让他们瞧瞧这点取笑他何等不在乎，他大步流星找她去了。她正等着，那细瘦的棕色少年郎在她身边摇来晃去，苦劝她别再等了。他不想失掉她。她也不想失掉阿尔瓦，见两人相携下池，正中下怀。

“你那位相好是谁？”阿尔瓦已拿杜松子酒壮足了胆。他呼出的气味熟悉得很。

“不过是个熟人。”她可不能让他知道，自己是只身来的。“没想到你还记得我。”

“当然记得，我哪能忘了你？”舌头抹了油，话里带着双关。

“我也没忘了*你*。”埃玛·卢撒娇卖乖。“我常找你来着。”

===Original===
Looked for him where? My God, what an impression he must have made! He wondered what he had said to her before. Plunge in boy, plunge! The blacker the berry—he chuckled to himself.

Orchestra playing “Blue Skies,” as an especial favor to her. Alva telling her his name and giving her his card, and asking her to phone him some day. Alva close to her and being nice, his arms tightening about her. She would call him tomorrow. Ecstasy ended too soon. The music stopped. He thanked her for the dance and left her standing on the promenade by the side of the waiting slender stripling. She danced with him twice more, then let him take her home.

===Chinese===
在哪儿找他来着？我的天，他想必给她留下了何等的印象！他寻思先前都对她说过些什么。闯吧小子，闯！浆果越黑——他心里直乐。

乐队奏起《蓝天》（Blue Skies），像是格外赏她的脸。阿尔瓦报上自己的名字，递上名片，请她哪天来个电话。阿尔瓦凑近她，献着殷勤，胳膊把她越搂越紧。明天就给他打电话。销魂结束得太快。音乐停了。他谢过这一舞，把她留在回廊上，留在那候着的瘦条少年郎身边。她又同少年郎跳了两回，随后由他送她回家。

===Original===
At ten the next morning Emma Lou called Alva. Braxton came to the telephone.

“Alva’s gone to work; who is it?” People should have more sense than to call that early in the morning. He never got up until noon. Emma Lou was being apologetic.

“Could you tell me what time he will be in?”

“’Bout six-thirty. Who shall I say called? This is his roommate.”

“Just… Oh… I’ll call him later. Thank you.”

Braxton swore. “Why in the hell does Alva give so many damn women his phone number?”

Six-thirty-five. His roommate had said about six-thirty. She called again. *He* came to the phone. She thought his voice was more harsh than usual.

“Oh, I’m all right, only tired.”

“Did you work hard?”

“I always work hard.”

“I…I…just thought I’d call.”

“Glad you did, call me again some time. Goodbye”—said too quickly. No chance to say “When will I see you again?”

She went home, got into the bed and cried herself to sleep.

---

===Chinese===
第二天上午十点，埃玛·卢给阿尔瓦打电话。接电话的是布拉克斯顿。

“阿尔瓦上工去了。哪位？”大清早十点来钟打电话的人，也该多长点常识。他从不过晌午不起床。埃玛·卢赔着小心。

“能告诉我他几点回来吗？”

“六点半上下吧。怎么称呼您？我是他同屋的。”

“就……哦……回头我再打来。谢谢。”

布拉克斯顿骂了句娘。“阿尔瓦这小子到底犯的什么邪，电话号码见女人就给？”

六点三十五分。同屋的说约莫六点半。她又打了一回。*他*来接的电话。她觉得他的嗓音比平时更哑。

“哦，我挺好，就是累。”

“活儿重吗？”

“我干活向来卖力。”

“我……我……就是想着打个电话。”

“你打来挺好，回头常打。再见”——说得太快。她来不及问“什么时候再见到你？”

她回了家，上床哭着哭着睡着了。

---

===Original===
Arline returned two days ahead of schedule. Things settled back into routine. The brown stripling had taken Emma Lou out twice, but upon her refusal to submit herself to him, had gone away in a huff, and had not returned. She surmised that it was the first time he had made such a request of anyone. He did it so ineptly. Work. Home. Walks. Theaters downtown during the afternoon, and thoughts of Alva. Finally, she just had to call him again. He came to the phone:

“Hello. Who? Emma Lou? Where have you been? I’ve been wondering where you were?”

She was shy, afraid she might be too bold. But Alva had had his usual three glasses of before-dinner gin. He helped her out.

“When can I see you, Sugar?”

Sugar! He had called her “sugar.” She told him where she worked. He was to meet her after the theater that very night.

---

===Chinese===
阿琳提前两天回来了。一切照旧运转。那个棕色少年郎带埃玛·卢出去过两回，可她不肯委身，他便负气而去，再没露面。她猜，他是头一回对人提这种要求，提得那样笨拙。做工。回家。散步。下午去城里看戏，想着阿尔瓦。到头来，她实在忍不住，又给他打了电话。他来接了：

“喂，哪位？埃玛·卢？你上哪儿去了？我一直纳闷你在哪儿呢？”

她害羞，怕自己太冒失。好在阿尔瓦照例灌过了饭前的三杯杜松子酒。他替她解了围。

“几时能见你，甜心？”

甜心！他管她叫“甜心”了。她告诉了他她在哪儿做工。说好当天夜里散了戏他就来接她。

---

===Original===
“How many nights a week you gonna have that little inkspitter up here?”

“Listen here, Brax, you have who you want up here, don’t you?”

“That ain’t it. I just don’t like to see you tied up with a broad like that.”

“Why not? She’s just as good as the rest, and you know what they say, ‘The blacker the berry, the sweeter the juice.’”

“The only thing a black woman is good for is to make money for a brown-skin papa.”

“I guess I don’t know that.”

“Well,” Braxton was satisfied now, “if that’s the case. …”

He had faith in Alva’s wisdom.

===Chinese===
“你打算一礼拜几晚上，把那个‘喷墨丫头’（inkspitter）带到楼上来？”

“听着，布拉克斯，你想带谁上来就带谁，不是吗？”

“不是那码事。我就是看不惯你叫这么个黑娘们缠住。”

“怎么？她跟旁人一样不差。俗话说得好：‘浆果越黑，汁水越甜。’”

“黑女人就一样用处——给棕肤大爷挣钱。”

“这话我还能不懂。”

“唔，”布拉克斯顿这才满意了，“要是这样的话……”

他信得过阿尔瓦的精明。

## Part IV / 第四部

Rent Party / 交租会

===Original===
Saturday evening. Alva had urged her to hurry uptown from work. He was going to take her on a party with some friends of his. This was the first time he had ever asked her to go to any sort of social affair with him. She had never met any of his friends save Braxton, who scarcely spoke to her, and never before had Alva suggested taking her to any sort of social gathering either public or semipublic. He often took her to various motion picture theaters, both downtown and in Harlem, and at least three nights a week he would call for her at the theater and escort her to Harlem. On these occasions they often went to Chinese restaurants or to ice cream parlors before going home. But usually they would go to City College Park, find an empty bench in a dark corner where they could sit and spoon before retiring either to her room or to Alva’s.

Emma Lou had, long before this, suggested going to a dance or to a party, but Alva had always countered that he never attended such affairs during the summer months, that he stayed away from them for precisely the same reason that he stayed away from work, namely, because it was too hot. Dancing, said he, was a matter of calisthenics, and calisthenics were work. Therefore it, like any sort of physical exercise, was taboo during hot weather.

Alva sensed that sooner or later Emma Lou would become aware of his real reason for not taking her out among his friends. He realized that one as color-conscious as she appeared to be would, at some not so distant date, jump to what for him would be uncomfortable conclusions. He did not wish to risk losing her before the end of summer, but neither could he risk taking her out among his friends, for he knew too well that he would be derided for his unseemly preference for “dark meat,” and told publicly without regard for her feelings, that “black cats must go.”

Furthermore he always took Geraldine to parties and dances. Geraldine with her olive colored skin and straight black hair. Geraldine, who of all the people he pretended to love, really inspired him emotionally as well as physically, the one person he conquested without thought of monetary gain. Yet he had to do something with Emma Lou, and release from the quandary presented itself from most unexpected quarters.

Quite accidentally, as things of the sort happen in Harlem with its complex but interdependable social structure, he had become acquainted with a young Negro writer, who had asked him to escort a group of young writers and artists to a house-rent party. Though they had heard much of this phenomenon, none had been on the inside of one, and because of their rather polished manners and exteriors, were afraid that they might not be admitted. Proletarian Negroes are as suspicious of their more sophisticated brethren as they are of white men, and resent as keenly their intrusions into their social world. Alva had consented to act as cicerone, and, realizing that these people would be more or less free from the color prejudice exhibited by his other friends, had decided to take Emma Lou along too. He was also aware of her intellectual pretensions, and felt that she would be especially pleased to meet recognized talents and outstanding personalities. She did not have to know that these were not his regular companions, and from then on she would have no reason to feel that he was ashamed to have her meet his friends.

===Chinese===
星期六傍晚。阿尔瓦催她下了工赶紧回住宅区来。他要带她去同他的几个朋友聚会。这是他头一回邀她同去任何一种社交场合。他的朋友她一个也没见过，只有布拉克斯顿例外，而那人几乎不同她讲话；阿尔瓦也从没提议过带她去任何公开或半公开的社交集会。他常带她去各家电影院，城里的也去，哈莱姆的也去；每星期至少有三个晚上，他到剧院门口接她，护送她回哈莱姆。这种时候，两人常常先去中国馆子或冷饮店，然后才回家。不过通常是去市立学院公园（City College Park），找个暗角落里的空长凳坐下搂抱亲热一番，然后各自就寝——回她的屋，或者阿尔瓦的屋。

早在这些之前，埃玛·卢就提议过去跳舞或参加聚会，可阿尔瓦总有话挡回来：夏天他从不赴这类场合，他躲着它们的理由同他躲着工作的理由一模一样——因为天太热。他说，跳舞是门体力操，体力操就是干活。因此，同任何一种身体锻炼一样，天热时它是禁忌。

阿尔瓦心里明白，埃玛·卢迟早会察觉他不带她到朋友中间去的真实缘由。他意识到，一个像她这样满脑子肤色的人，不出多久就会得出令他难堪的结论。他不愿冒这个险，在夏天过完之前失去她；可带她见朋友，这个险他同样冒不起，因为他太清楚了：人家会笑话他那副不成体统、专好“深色肉”（dark meat）的口味，还会当众宣布、全然不顾她的感受：“黑猫就得滚蛋。”

再说，带去参加聚会和舞会的，从来都是杰拉尔丁。橄榄色皮肤、黑直头发的杰拉尔丁。在他假装爱着的一切人里，唯有杰拉尔丁真正在情感上、也在肉体上点燃他，是他不图钱财便拿下了的人。可埃玛·卢这边总得有个交代，而把他从这桩两难里解救出来的出路，却从最意想不到的方向冒了出来。

事情纯属偶然，就像这类事在哈莱姆那个盘根错节又环环相扣的社会结构里常有的那样——他结识了一位年轻的黑人作家，对方请他领着一群年轻的作家和艺术家去赴一场交租会。这桩奇观他们早有耳闻，却没有一个从里头看过；又因为他们举止谈吐都太文雅，还怕人家不放他们进门。无产者的黑人对他们那些更文雅世故的同胞，疑心同对白人一样重，对这种人闯进自己的社交世界，怨恨也一样尖。阿尔瓦答应充当向导，又想到这伙人多少能免于他其他朋友那种肤色偏见，便决意把埃玛·卢也带上。他也清楚她在智识上的自负，觉得让她见见公认的才子和出众人物，她会格外欢喜。她不必知道这些人并非他平日的伙伴，如此一来，她再没有理由觉得，他羞于让她见自己的朋友了。

===Original===
Emma Lou could hardly attend to Arline’s change of complexion and clothes between acts and scenes, so anxious was she to get to Alva’s house and to the promised party. Her happiness was complete. She was certain now that Alva loved her, certain that he was not ashamed or even aware of her dusky complexion. She had felt from the first that he was superior to such inane truck, now she knew it. Alva loved her for herself alone, and loved her so much that he didn’t mind her being a coal scuttle blond.

Sensing something unusual, Arline told Emma Lou that she would remove her own makeup after the performance, and let her have time to get dressed for the party. This she proceeded to do all through the evening, spending much time in front of the mirror at Arline’s dressing table, manicuring her nails, marcelling her hair, and applying various creams and cosmetics to her face in order to make her despised darkness less obvious. Finally, she put on one of Arline’s less pretentious afternoon frocks, and set out for Alva’s house.

As she approached his room door, she heard much talk and laughter, moving her to halt and speculate whether or not she should go in. Even her unusual and high-tensioned jubilance was not powerful enough to overcome immediately her shyness and fears. Suppose these friends of Alva’s would not take kindly to her? Suppose they were like Braxton, who invariably curled his lip when he saw her, and seldom spoke even as much as a word of greeting? Suppose they were like the people who used to attend her mother’s and grandmother’s teas, club meetings and receptions, dismissing her with—“It beats me how this child of yours looks so unlike the rest of you … Are you sure it isn’t adopted.” Or suppose they were like the college youth she had known in Southern California? No, that couldn’t be. Alva would never invite her where she would not be welcome. These were his friends. And so was Braxton, but Alva said he was peculiar. There was no danger. Alva had invited her. She was here. Anyway she wasn’t so black. Hadn’t she artificially lightened her skin about four or five shades until she was almost brown? Certainly it was all right. She needn’t be a foolish ninny all her life. Thus, reassured, she knocked on the door, and felt herself trembling with excitement and internal uncertainty as Alva let her in, took her hat and coat, and proceeded to introduce her to the people in the room.

===Chinese===
埃玛·卢简直顾不上照应阿琳各幕各场之间换妆换衣的工夫——她一心只想赶紧到阿尔瓦家、赴那场应许了的聚会。她的幸福圆满无缺。如今她确信阿尔瓦爱她，确信他不以此为耻，甚至压根没把她的深肤色放在心上。她从一开始就觉出，他高出那种无聊的劳什子一等，如今更是确知了。阿尔瓦爱的是她这个人本身，而且爱得这样深，连她是个“煤斗金发妞”也不介意。

阿琳觉出有些不寻常，便对埃玛·卢说，散戏后她自己卸妆，好腾出时间让她打扮了去赴会。于是她整个晚上都泡在这上头，长时间坐在阿琳梳妆台前的镜子跟前，修指甲，烫马塞尔波浪，往脸上抹各式油膏和脂粉，好教她那受人鄙夷的黑不那么扎眼。最后，她穿上阿琳一件不太张扬的下午裙，动身去阿尔瓦家。

走近他房门时，她听见屋里笑语喧哗，不由停住脚，盘算该不该进去。就连她这不寻常的、绷得紧紧的欢喜劲儿，也没能一下子压倒她的羞怯和惶恐。万一阿尔瓦这些朋友待她不亲善呢？万一他们像布拉克斯顿——那人一见她就撇嘴，连一句招呼都难得赏——呢？万一他们像从前出入她母亲和外祖母那些茶会、俱乐部聚会和招待会的人们，一句“我真闹不明白，你这孩子怎么长得跟你们家其他人这么不像……是不是抱养的”就把她打发了呢？又万一，他们像她在南加利福尼亚认识的那些大学生呢？不，不会的。阿尔瓦绝不会请她到不受欢迎的地方去。这些是他的朋友。布拉克斯顿也是，可阿尔瓦说他脾气古怪。没有危险。是阿尔瓦请的她。她人已经到了。再说她也没那么黑。她不是靠人工把皮肤漂亮了四五个色阶、几乎漂成了棕色了吗？自然万事妥帖。总不能一辈子当个犯傻的傻丫头。这样宽下心来，她敲了敲门。阿尔瓦开门让她进去，接过她的帽子和外套，把她一一介绍给屋里的人；她只觉自己浑身发颤，一半是兴奋，一半是心里没底。

===Original===
“Miss Morgan, meet Mr. Tony Crews. You’ve probably seen his book of poems. He’s the little jazz boy, you know.”

Emma Lou bashfully touched the extended hand of the curly-headed poet. She had not seen or read his book, but she had often noticed his name in the newspapers and magazines. He was all that she had expected him to be except that he had pimples on his face. These didn’t fit in with her mental picture.

“Miss Morgan, this is Cora Thurston. Maybe I should’a introduced you ladies first.”

“I’m no lady, and I hope you’re not either, Miss Morgan.” She smiled, shook Emma Lou’s hand, then turned away to continue her interrupted conversation with Tony Crews.

“Miss Morgan, meet …,” he paused, and addressed a tall, dark yellow youth stretched out on the floor, “What name you going by now?”

The boy looked up and smiled.

“Why, Paul, of course.”

“All right then Miss Morgan, this is Mr. Paul, he changes his name every season.”

Emma Lou sought to observe this person more closely, and was shocked to see that his shirt was open at the neck and that he was sadly in need of a haircut and shave.

“Miss Morgan, meet Mr. Walter.” A small slender dark youth with an infectious smile and small features. His face was familiar. Where had she seen him before?

“Now that you’ve met everyone, sit down on the bed there beside Truman and have a drink. Go on with your talk folks,” he urged as he went over to the dresser to fill a glass with a milk colored liquid. Cora Thurston spoke up in answer to Alva’s adjuration:
